  * **Tests.** `TTLCacheRaceTest` (+1): `get` and `values()` read a key for 400 ms while another thread replaces its value; neither may see `null`. Fails on the previous code with 5,000–17,000 `null` reads per run. Full suite: 21,213 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **A `TTLCache` built with a TTL near `Long.MAX_VALUE` keeps its entries instead of expiring each one at birth.** An entry's expiry is the current time plus the TTL. For a TTL above `Long.MAX_VALUE` minus the current time (for example `Long.MAX_VALUE`, a natural way to say "never expire"), the sum wrapped negative: the expiry was in the past, so `get` returned `null` immediately after `put`, unbounded or size-limited, through `put`, `putIfAbsent` and `computeIfAbsent`. The constructor now caps the TTL at `Long.MAX_VALUE / 2` milliseconds (146 million years). A check on every write instead cost 1.2 ns per `put` on JDK 21; capping once costs nothing. Put is on par: 39.2 vs 39.4 ns size-limited, 28.9 vs 28.9 ns unbounded on JDK 21; 40.9 vs 40.8 and 28.8 vs 28.8 ns on JDK 8 (new vs old).
  * **Tests.** `TTLCacheLimitsTest` (1): TTLs of `Long.MAX_VALUE`, `Long.MAX_VALUE - 1000` and `Long.MAX_VALUE / 2 + 1`, unbounded and size-limited, through `put`, `computeIfAbsent` and `putIfAbsent`, then `get`, `containsKey` and `keySet()`. Fails on the previous code. Full suite: 21,214 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`TTLCache.clear()` racing a `put` no longer leaves an entry that can never be evicted, or an LRU list that loses entries.** A size-limited cache emptied its map outside the lock and reset its LRU list inside it. A `put` holding the lock in between put its entry in the emptied map and linked it into the old list, which the reset then threw away. The entry stayed in the map, could never be evicted, and still had links into the old list; a later `get` of it reordered it along those links and cut every other entry out of the new list, so they could never be evicted either. In the review's probe, `maxSize` 3 ended with `[a99, a98, b99]` instead of the three most recent keys. `clear()` now empties the map and the list together under the lock, and gives every removed node null links so a reader still holding one cannot reorder it into the new list. Unbounded caches have no list and were not affected. `clear()` now visits every node: about 2.4 ns more per entry on a 100,000-entry cache (0.56 → 0.81 ms per clear on JDK 21, 0.54 → 0.73 ms on JDK 8). Unlinking only the two nodes next to the list ends would avoid this, but a reader could then leave old nodes in the new list, held until eviction reached them.
  * **Tests.** `TTLCacheRaceTest` (+1): `clear()` waits while a `put` holds the lock. Afterwards 100 puts, a `get` of the raced key and 100 more puts must leave exactly the three most recent keys. Fails on the previous code. Full suite: 21,215 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`ConcurrentHashMapNullSafe.replaceAll` and `ConcurrentNavigableMapNullSafe.replaceAll` no longer skip a key whose value became `null` meanwhile; `forEach` is about twice as fast, and so is a `putIfAbsent` miss on the navigable map.** The two maps inherited three `ConcurrentMap` defaults:
  * **`replaceAll`.** The default retries an entry whose value changed, unless the new value reads as `null`, which it takes to mean the key was removed. In these maps `null` is a value. When another write set a key to `null` while `replaceAll` was computing that key's new value, the key was skipped and kept `null`, though the function should have run on it. `replaceAll` now uses the backing map's own, which retries with the current value (null included).
  * **`forEach`.** The default walked the entry set's wrapped entries. It now runs on the backing map's own traversal: 5.75 → 2.70 ns per entry (hash) and 4.74 → 1.96 (navigable) on JDK 21; 5.61 → 2.78 and 3.88 → 2.18 on JDK 8.
  * **`putIfAbsent` miss.** It went through `compute` with a capturing lambda. It is now the backing map's `putIfAbsent`, followed by `replace` when the key is mapped to `null` (which `Map.putIfAbsent` treats as absent). Remove plus `putIfAbsent`:
    * hash map: 23.0 → 18.4 ns on JDK 21; 26.2 → 27.1 ns on JDK 8 (+0.9 ns, minor);
    * navigable map: 589 → 472 ns on JDK 21, 540 → 429 ns on JDK 8.

    Hits are on par. Found by the review of the concurrent collections (its L2).
  * **Tests.** `ConcurrentNullSafeMapDefaultsTest` (2):
    * `replaceAll` whose function sets the key to `null` the first time it runs, on both maps. It must then return `"null!"`, and fails on the previous code.
    * A differential test: 20 seeds × 500 random `put`, `putIfAbsent`, `remove`, `replaceAll` and `forEach` steps with `null` keys and values, against `HashMap` and a null-last `TreeMap`. It checks return values, contents and `forEach` order after every step. It passes on the previous code too (a guard), and catches two planted mutants: a `null` mapping not replaced by `putIfAbsent`, and `forEach` handing out the internal null marker.

    Full suite: 21,217 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **An unbounded `TTLCache`'s `putIfAbsent` and `computeIfAbsent` no longer overwrite a `put` that lands while they run.** Unbounded, `put` takes no lock. `putIfAbsent` and `computeIfAbsent` took the lock, read the key and then wrote it unconditionally, so a `put` landing between the read and the write was lost. Both callers were told they had inserted: `putIfAbsent` returned `null` and its value replaced the `put`'s; `computeIfAbsent` returned its computed value, which replaced the value of a `put` made while the function ran. Now:
  * both write only over exactly the entry they read (`putIfAbsent` / `replace` on the map);
  * when another write got there first and is live, that write stands and its value is returned.

  `putIfAbsent` no longer takes the lock at all when unbounded; `computeIfAbsent` keeps it, so its function still runs once per key among `computeIfAbsent` callers. Size-limited caches take the lock for every write and were not affected. Unbounded `putIfAbsent` is faster: hit 22.0 → 17.5 ns and remove-then-insert 45.8 → 33.7 ns on JDK 21; 20.0 → 15.2 and 47.4 → 37.3 ns on JDK 8. Remove-then-`computeIfAbsent` costs 44.3 → 46.3 ns on JDK 21 and 46.0 → 47.2 ns on JDK 8: the conditional write the fix needs, on top of the null-safe map's faster `putIfAbsent` above. Hits and size-limited caches are on par.
  * **Tests.** `TTLCacheRaceTest` (+2): a `put` forced between `putIfAbsent`'s read and write, and a `put` made while `computeIfAbsent`'s function runs. The `put`'s value must stand and be returned. Both fail on the previous code. Full suite: 21,219 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`TTLCache.equals`, `hashCode` and `toString` no longer run other code while holding the cache's lock, so two caches compared with each other at the same moment cannot deadlock, and a slow `toString` no longer holds up writes.** All three took the cache's lock and, holding it, ran the keys', values' and other map's code:
  * **`equals` deadlock.** `a.equals(b)` asked `b`, and `b` takes its own lock to drop an entry it finds expired. With `b.equals(a)` running at the same moment, each thread held one lock and waited for the other forever. The JVM's deadlock detector reported it.
  * **Writes held up.** Every write of a size-limited cache needs the lock, so a value whose `toString` or `hashCode` was slow held up all writes for as long as it ran; the review measured a `put` waiting 259 ms behind `toString`.

  The lock never made these three atomic anyway: removals and expiry checks run without it, and unbounded writes too. They now run without it and are documented as not atomic, as `ConcurrentHashMap`'s are. On par: `hashCode` 6.69 vs 6.70 ns and `toString` 34.2 vs 34.5 ns per entry on JDK 21; `equals` 14.8 → 13.1 ns per entry on JDK 8 (1,000 entries, old vs new).
  * **Tests.** `TTLCacheRaceTest` (+2):
    * `a.equals(b)` and `b.equals(a)` at once, each cache holding live an entry the other has expired. The keys line the two threads up inside their comparisons; both must finish within 5 s.
    * A value whose `toString` (then `hashCode`) waits on a latch: a `put` from another thread must not wait behind it.

    Both fail on the previous code. Full suite: 21,221 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **One failing background purge no longer ends a `TTLCache`'s purging for good.** Each cache's purge is a periodic task on a shared scheduler. If a purge threw (a key's `hashCode` failing, an `OutOfMemoryError`), the exception escaped the task, and the scheduler silently cancels a periodic task that throws. Expired entries then stayed in that cache until each was read; the review's probe still held 1,001 expired entries half a second later, with a 10 ms purge interval. The purge task now catches any `Throwable`, logs it at `FINE`, and tries again at the next interval. The `shutdown()` Javadoc said existing caches resume purging when the scheduler restarts; they do not, and the doc now says so (they still never return an expired entry, and drop one when it is read).
  * **Two related items were measured and left alone:**
    * **Resuming existing caches after `shutdown()`** would need a registry of open caches, adding 46 ns to every construct-plus-close.
    * **Removing a closed cache's task from the scheduler at once** (`setRemoveOnCancelPolicy`), instead of when its next run comes round, adds 107 ns.

    Together these cost 75 → 282 ns on JDK 21 and 134 → 406 ns on JDK 8 per construct-plus-close. All they buy: `shutdown()` is meant for application shutdown and test cleanup, and a closed cache's task holds about 100 bytes for at most one interval. With this change, construct-plus-close is on par: 73 vs 74 ns on JDK 21 (old vs new).
  * **Tests.** `TTLCachePurgeTest` (1): a key whose `hashCode` throws on the purge thread for 150 ms. Afterwards the periodic purge must still be scheduled and must remove 100 newly expired entries. Fails on the previous code. Full suite: 21,222 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **An `Error` while trimming one THREADED `LRUCache` no longer stops the trimming of every THREADED cache in the JVM.** All THREADED caches share one periodic cleanup task, which caught `Exception` but not `Error`. The scheduler silently cancels a periodic task that throws, so one `OutOfMemoryError`, or a `StackOverflowError` from a key's `hashCode`, ended trimming for every THREADED cache, including caches created later, for the life of the JVM. They then grew without bound. The task now catches `Throwable`, per cache and around the whole pass. Nothing changes on any path that does not throw.
  * **Tests.** `ThreadedLRUCacheCleanupTest` (1): a key whose `hashCode` throws an `Error` on the cleanup thread for 1.2 s. Afterwards another cache filled to 1,000 entries must be trimmed to its capacity of 10. Fails on the previous code (still 1,000). Full suite: 21,223 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **PERFORMANCE**: **The float-to-decimal-double behind `MultiKeyMap`'s float equality now reads a tiny float about 10x faster, through the Eisel-Lemire core of the bundled fastdoubleparser.** `FloatDecimal` gives the double for the decimal a float prints (0.1f is 0.1), without printing. It finds the shortest decimal with Schubfach, then turns significand × 10^exponent into a double. That second step used Clinger's fast path (exponents −22 to 22, one rounding of exact operands) and parsed text for anything smaller, about 1e-14 and below. It now calls fastdoubleparser's `FastDoubleMath.tryDecToDoubleWithFastAlgorithm`, already in java-util, which takes the significand and exponent directly. It runs Clinger's fast path, then Eisel-Lemire, and returns `NaN` in the rare case it cannot decide; text parsing remains only for that case. Results (4,096 floats each):
  * typical floats: on par, 7.4 → 7.3 ns on JDK 21 and 7.6 → 7.8 ns on JDK 8;
  * tiny floats: 126 → 10.1 ns on JDK 21 and 95 → 10.2 ns on JDK 8.

  `FloatDecimal`'s own table of exact powers of ten is gone. `FastDoubleMath` and that one method are now public, for `FloatDecimal`; the package is not exported by the module. For comparison, printing with `Float.toString` and parsing costs 35–43 ns on JDK 21 and 52–54 ns on JDK 8.
  * **Checks.**
    * Every one of the 645,922,816 positive floats whose decimal is past Clinger's reach, and its negation, gives the same bits as `Double.parseDouble` of the decimal, on JDK 21 and on JDK 8.
    * All 1,249,902,592 positive floats with a fraction, and their negations, match `Double.parseDouble(Float.toString(f))` on JDK 21.
    * `FloatDecimalTest` (4 million floats plus edge families) passes, and fails 3 of 3 on a planted mutant that shifts the exponent of tiny floats.

    Full suite: 21,223 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **PERFORMANCE**: **`Converter` widens a `Float` to `Double` about 4x faster, with the same answer on every JDK.** A `Float` becomes the `Double` of the decimal it prints (3.7f is 3.7, not 3.700000047683716), so far by printing it with `Float.toString` and parsing the text. It now computes that double directly with `FloatDecimal` (Schubfach, then the fastdoubleparser core above): 47.1 → 11.9 ns per `convert` on JDK 21 and 60.4 → 14.7 ns on JDK 8. The answer is the decimal JDK 19 and later print:
  * **JDK 19 and later:** exactly the same as before. Every positive finite float (2,139,095,039 of them) and its negation was checked against `Double.parseDouble(Float.toString(f))` on JDK 21, with 0 differences.
  * **Older JDKs:** their `Float.toString` prints a longer decimal for many floats (233,138,629 positive floats on JDK 8). Most are whole floats from 2^25 to about 3.7e19, for example 33554448f, which JDK 8 prints `3.3554448E7` and JDK 19 prints `3.355445E7`; the rest include 19 tiny ones such as `2.24E-44` for `2.2E-44`. `Converter` gave a different `Double` for those on JDK 8 than on JDK 21; it now gives JDK 21's on both.

  `FloatDecimal` moved to `com.cedarsoftware.util.internal` (exported to json-io only, not public API) so that `Converter` can use it. It gained `printedValue(float)`, which keeps zeros, infinities and `NaN` as they are. `MultiKeyMap` keeps its own rule, under which whole floats are their exact value. `Float` to `BigDecimal` and `Float` to `String` still go through `Float.toString`.
  * **Tests.** `ConverterFloatToDoubleTest` (2): the 19 tiny floats older JDKs print differently, and their negations, must convert to the JDK 19 decimal; fractions, whole numbers, both infinities, `NaN` and both zeros. The first fails on the previous code on a JDK 8 runtime (`2.24E-44` for `2.2E-44`). `FloatDecimalTest` moved with the class. Full suite: 21,225 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **A THREADED `LRUCache` over capacity is trimmed in one cleanup pass, instead of costing more with every entry it evicts.** The cleanup thread evicts the oldest of a 10-entry sample, but each sample started a new iteration at the front of the hash table. Every sample therefore examined the same leading entries, and once evictions had emptied the leading bins, each call walked past more of them first, so evicting n entries cost on the order of n². A cleanup pass has a 400 ms budget, and a 100,000-entry surplus took 7 passes (1.6 s) on JDK 21 and 16 passes (3.6 s) on JDK 8. A 1,000,000-entry surplus was still 739,000 over after 50 passes (9.4 s). Meanwhile the shared cleanup thread starved other caches.

  Samples now continue from where the last one ended, wrapping round at the end of the table, so successive samples sweep the whole table at 10 entries per eviction. One pass now drains each surplus:

  | Surplus | JDK 21 | JDK 8 |
  |---|---|---|
  | 10,000 | 26 → 5 ms | 39 → 5 ms |
  | 100,000 | 1,628 → 21 ms | 3,555 → 20 ms |
  | 1,000,000 | 103 ms | 83 ms |

  The cursor is moved by one evictor at a time: the cleanup thread skips a cache that `forceCleanup` is trimming. `get` and `put` are unchanged.
  * **Tests.** `ThreadedLRUCacheCleanupTest` (+1): a capacity-100,000 cache holding 200,000 entries must be at capacity after one `forceCleanup()`. Fails on the previous code (180,405 left). Full suite: 21,226 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **A writer faster than the cleanup thread can no longer grow a THREADED `LRUCache` without bound.** THREADED caches never evict on a write; one shared thread trims them every 500 ms. In between, a single writer could insert millions of entries: the review's probe ran a 1 GB heap out of memory within about a second, at 8.8 million entries for a capacity of 10,000, and 4 writers reached 549 times the capacity. Now a writer helps once the cache is past twice its capacity. When `put`, `putIfAbsent` or `computeIfAbsent` adds a new key, a writer trims the cache back to capacity with the cleanup thread's bulk eviction. Only one writer trims at a time; the others carry on.

  Only a new node whose timestamp (a tick of the shared logical clock, one per node created) is a multiple of 32 checks the size, so a cache can pass twice its capacity by a few dozen entries. The sampling matters for cost: checking `ConcurrentHashMap.size()` on every insert reads every counter cell the writers update, and cost 70% more per new-key `put` at 4 threads on JDK 21. Sampling with `ThreadLocalRandom` instead cost 2–3 ns per insert single-threaded.

  New-key `put` plus `remove` at a steady size, where nothing needs trimming:
  * one thread: 15.6 → 16.1 ns on JDK 21, 21.3 → 22.2 ns on JDK 8;
  * four threads: 127.8 → 121.6 ns on JDK 21, 109.8 → 117.8 ns on JDK 8, within the noise of a 4-thread run.

  Writes to existing keys and `get` are unchanged. A workload that only ever inserts new keys now pays for evicting, which it never did: the old cache grew instead, and with 4 threads on JDK 8 it slowed to 1,382 ns per `put` under garbage collection.
  * **Tests.** `ThreadedLRUCacheCleanupTest` (+1): with capacity 1,000, two million new keys through `put`, `putIfAbsent` and `computeIfAbsent`, sampling the size every 256 inserts; it must never pass 3,000. Fails on the previous code (capacity 100: 200,000 after 200,000 puts). Full suite: 21,227 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **A THREADED `LRUCache`'s evictor and a trimming writer no longer shut each other out (a regression from the two fixes above, never released).** The fixes above gave each cache one eviction lock. The cleanup thread (or `forceCleanup`) held it for its whole pass, up to 400 ms, while evicting one sampled entry at a time. A writer flooding new keys found the lock taken at each attempt to trim, and the one-at-a-time evictor fell behind it, so the cache grew to hundreds of thousands of entries for a capacity of 1,000. The JDK 8 run of the full suite caught it as an intermittent failure of the writer test. Now:
  * the evictor takes the lock for one step at a time, so a writer can trim between steps;
  * each step re-checks the surplus, and past twice the capacity evicts in bulk, up to 1,024 entries from the sample cursor;
  * past four times the capacity, a writer waits for the lock (at most one step) instead of leaving the work to the evictor, so the bound holds however fast the writers are.

  Bulk eviction now continues from the sample cursor, so chunks never re-walk emptied bins. Draining 100,000 entries costs about 10% more for the per-step locking: 21 → 24 ms on JDK 21, 19 → 23 ms on JDK 8. Insert plus remove is on par.
  * **Tests.** `ThreadedLRUCacheCleanupTest` (+1): one thread calls `forceCleanup()` in a loop while a writer puts two million new keys into a capacity-1,000 cache; the cache must never pass 5,000. Fails on the previous code (63,169 to 786,407). Five consecutive runs pass on JDK 21 and on JDK 8. Full suite: 21,228 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **PERFORMANCE**: **THREADED `LRUCache.get` scales with threads: 157 → 1,574 million gets per second at 8 threads on JDK 21 (166 → 2,351 on JDK 8), and every read is now recorded.** To mark an entry as recently used, one `get` in eight (chosen at random) incremented a JVM-wide `AtomicLong` clock, which every reading thread then contended for: 8 threads read barely faster than one. The clock now advances only when a node is created. A `get` stamps the node with the current tick and writes the node only when its tick is stale, so the shared counter is only read. The tick means "used after the N-th insertion", which is all eviction compares, and every read now counts: an entry read once after newer entries were inserted no longer looks older than them seven times in eight. Single-threaded `get` is faster too, 186 → 301 Mops/s on JDK 21 and 250 → 322 on JDK 8 (10,000 entries). New-key insert plus remove is on par: 15.6 vs 15.6 ns on JDK 21, 22.1 vs 21.8 on JDK 8.
  * **Sampled eviction and clustered keys (measured, not changed).** A sample is ten neighbouring entries of the hash table. Keys with consecutive hash codes, such as `"newcomer-10"` … `"newcomer-19"`, are neighbours, so a sample can hold several stale entries and evicts only one of them. In the test's scenario with such keys, 14 of the 100 evictions were the never-read newcomers; with keys spread over the table, at least 30 (0 on the previous code). Redis samples neighbouring buckets the same way but scatters keys with SipHash, which `String.hashCode` does not.
  * **Tests.** `ThreadedLRUCacheCleanupTest` (+1): 1,000 entries are inserted, then 100 newcomers, then each of the 1,000 is read once. After a cleanup pass, at least 30 of the 100 evictions must be newcomers (keys spread over the table). Fails on the previous code: 0. The fast-writer test's bound becomes 5,000 for a capacity of 1,000, the hard bound of the fix above, since the cleanup thread can be evicting while the writer floods (it reached 3,463 once against the old 3,000). Full suite: 21,229 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`replaceAll` on `LRUCache` and `TTLCache` replaces the values instead of silently doing nothing, and `setValue` on an entry from `entrySet()` writes through or refuses.** None of the caches had its own `replaceAll`. The `Map` default writes each new value with `setValue` on the entry set's entries, and those were copies:
  * `LRUCache`'s entry set is a documented snapshot;
  * `TTLCache` handed out fresh `SimpleEntry` objects.

  So `replaceAll` and `entry.setValue` changed only the copies, with no error. This affected both LRU strategies and both kinds of `TTLCache`. Now:
  * `LRUCache` delegates `replaceAll` to its strategy. LOCKING replaces each value under the lock and leaves the recency order alone, as `LinkedHashMap.replaceAll` does. THREADED uses the map's own `replaceAll`, which retries an entry changed meanwhile, and each entry keeps its recency.
  * `LRUCache`'s snapshot entry sets hand out read-only entries, so `setValue` throws `UnsupportedOperationException`.
  * `TTLCache`'s entry set is a view of the cache, so its entries write `setValue` through, as `put` does (the value lives a full TTL again), and the inherited `replaceAll` works through them.

  Entry-set iteration and `toString` are on par for all three (1,000 entries): LOCKING iteration 13.75 vs 13.54 ns per entry on JDK 21; TTL 24.5 vs 24.7; JDK 8 likewise.
  * **Tests.** `CacheWriteThroughTest` (2), on LOCKING, THREADED, size-limited TTL and unbounded TTL:
    * `replaceAll` must change every value, a `null` one included;
    * `setValue` must write through (TTL) or throw (LRU).

    Both fail on the previous code. Full suite: 21,231 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`merge`, `compute`, `computeIfPresent`, `replace` and `remove(key, value)` on `LRUCache` and `TTLCache` are atomic: concurrent updates no longer overwrite each other.** `LRUCache` delegated only `computeIfAbsent` and `putIfAbsent` to its strategy, and neither strategy nor `TTLCache` implemented the rest. They were therefore the `Map` defaults: a `get`, then a `put`. Eight threads each adding 1 to a counter 100,000 times with `merge` ended at 188,947 instead of 800,000 (LOCKING), 179,224 (THREADED), 19,505 (size-limited TTL) and 487,316 (unbounded TTL), and `remove(key, value)` could succeed for two threads at once. All five now act on the key atomically:
  * **LOCKING** under its lock (the function runs under it, as in `computeIfAbsent`). This is faster than the default's two lookups: `compute` 18.7 → 12.7 ns on JDK 21 and 16.1 → 10.7 ns on JDK 8, with the same for `merge` and `replace`.
  * **THREADED** with compare-and-set loops on its nodes: read the node, compute, then `replace`, `putIfAbsent` or `remove` exactly that node (by identity), retrying when overtaken. Faster: `compute` 16.7 → 14.6 ns, `merge` 16.6 → 14.4 and `replace` 16.0 → 12.4 on JDK 21, and likewise on JDK 8. Going through the map's own `compute` instead cost up to 18% on JDK 8, for its capturing lambdas.
  * **`TTLCache`, size-limited**, under its lock: about 6% faster (`compute` 48.5 → 45.6 ns on JDK 21).
  * **`TTLCache`, unbounded**, whose `put` takes no lock, with the same compare-and-set loops: `compute` and `merge` within 3% on JDK 21 and 5–6% on JDK 8. `replace(key, value)` has its own allocation-free loop: 39.7 → 41.4 ns on JDK 21, 35.1 → 37.6 on JDK 8.

  In every cache, an expired entry counts as absent and a new value is written as `put` writes it. Under contention the loops may run the function more than once, as `ConcurrentMap`'s defaults may.
  * **Tests.** `CacheAtomicityTest` (6), on LOCKING, THREADED, size-limited and unbounded TTL:
    * 8 threads × 20,000 increments each, through `merge`, `compute`, `computeIfPresent`, and `replace(key, old, new)` as a compare-and-set; every total must be exact;
    * 200 rounds of 8 threads racing `remove(key, value)`; exactly one may succeed per round;
    * a single-threaded walk through the `Map` contract, `null` values and absent keys included, which passes on the previous code too.

    The five concurrent tests fail on the previous code. Full suite: 21,237 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **A `compute`, `merge` or `computeIfPresent` function that writes the very key it is computing no longer spins forever on a THREADED `LRUCache` or an unbounded `TTLCache` (a regression from the fix above, never released).** Those two use compare-and-set: read, run the function, then write only over exactly what was read, retrying when another write got in first. A function that itself writes the key (which the `Map` contract forbids) overtakes every attempt, so the retry never ended. The test hung on both JDKs. After two attempts, the function now runs under the map's own lock for the key, which nothing can overtake. The call completes, or the backing map refuses the function (`IllegalStateException`, as `ConcurrentHashMap` does). The uncontended path is unchanged: `compute` 14.6 vs 14.6 ns and `merge` 14.5 vs 14.4 ns on JDK 21 (THREADED); unbounded TTL within 1% on both JDKs. THREADED `computeIfAbsent` is unchanged: it has always gone through `ConcurrentHashMap.computeIfAbsent`, which refuses such a function on JDK 9 and later, and loops on JDK 8 (that JDK's own bug, JDK-8062841).
  * **Tests.** `CacheAtomicityTest` (+1): `compute`, `merge` and `computeIfPresent` with a function that `put`s its own key, on all four caches. Each must finish within 5 s, completing or refused, and the cache must still take a `put`. Fails on the previous code (THREADED `compute` still running after 5 s). Full suite: 21,238 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **A LOCKING `LRUCache` stays within its capacity when a `computeIfAbsent` or `compute` function writes the key it is computing.** LOCKING runs these functions under its lock, which is reentrant, so a function that puts (or removes and puts) its own key goes through. The `Map` contract forbids that, and `HashMap` throws for it. LOCKING then linked a second node for the key next to the one the function had added. The map held one entry and the recency list two, and every later eviction removed the key's map entry while leaving its stray node behind, so a capacity-3 cache held 4 entries after any number of puts. Now:
  * a write after the function replaces a node the function added, rather than keeping both;
  * it writes into the key's current node when the function removed the one read before;
  * removal takes the key's node only if it is still the one read.

  `TTLCache` already copes, since its eviction skips stale nodes. `compute`, `merge` and `replace` are on par: 12.6 vs 12.9 ns, 12.2 vs 12.5 and 12.1 vs 12.5 on JDK 21; 10.8 vs 10.6 ns, 11.5 vs 11.6 and 9.5 vs 9.3 on JDK 8.
  * **Tests.** `CacheAtomicityTest` (+1): on LOCKING and size-limited TTL, `computeIfAbsent`, `compute`, `merge` and `computeIfPresent` with a function that puts (or removes and puts) its own key. The outer result must stand, and after 10 more puts a capacity-3 cache must hold exactly the 3 most recent keys. Fails on the previous code (LOCKING: 4 entries). Full suite: 21,239 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **Streams over the null-safe maps' views and over `ConcurrentNavigableSetNullSafe` no longer throw when the collection changes during the stream.** These views did not override `spliterator()`:
  * `keySet()`, `values()` and `entrySet()` of `ConcurrentHashMapNullSafe` and `ConcurrentNavigableMapNullSafe`, with their descending and head/tail/sub views;
  * `ConcurrentNavigableSetNullSafe` and its views.

  The `Collection` / `SortedSet` default is an iterator-based spliterator that promises a fixed size. When the source grew or shrank mid-stream, `toArray()` and `sorted()` threw `IllegalStateException` ("Accept exceeded fixed size of 4", "End size 2 is less than fixed size 44"). The review's probe, with one writer thread, counted 70,878 such failures against 9,592 successes. Each now returns the backing view's own spliterator, which is weakly consistent and never sized, with elements unmasked: the null marker becomes `null`. That is how `ConcurrentSet` already did it. The new package-private `UnmaskingSpliterator` drops `NONNULL` and reports the view's own comparator when sorted, and entry-set entries still write `setValue` through.

  Streams over the navigable views are faster: `count()` over 10,000 keys 4.71 → 2.14 ns per key on JDK 21 and 8.95 → 2.44 on JDK 8 (the set likewise). The hash views are on par or faster, apart from the entry set's `stream().toArray()` on JDK 21, 6.95 → 7.52 ns per entry: without a fixed size, `toArray` grows its buffer instead of sizing it up front.
  * **Tests.** `ConcurrentNullSafeViewStreamTest` (4), over 10 views:
    * a stream whose `peek()` adds 50 elements to the source must not throw;
    * one whose `peek()` removes most of it must not throw;
    * a stream must hand out what iteration does, in order, nulls included;
    * a streamed entry must write `setValue` through.

    The first two fail on the previous code. Full suite: 21,243 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`ConcurrentSet.toArray(T[])` no longer hands out the set's internal null marker.** To return a `null` element as `null`, `toArray(T[])` checked that the set held no `null` and, if so, copied the backing set directly into the caller's array. A `null` added between the check and the copy went out as the private marker object. The review's probe found it in 914,478 of 4.1 million calls with one thread adding and removing `null`. A typed array such as `String[]` cannot hold the marker: storing it throws `ArrayStoreException`, which already fell back to the unmasking copy. The fix is therefore needed only for arrays that can hold it, and only those are scanned now, turning the marker back into `null`. That in practice means `Object[]`; the marker is an enum, so the others are `Serializable[]`, `Comparable[]` and `Enum[]`.

  Cost (old → new, ns per element, `toArray(new String[0])` / `toArray(new Object[0])`):

  | Elements | JDK 21 | JDK 8 |
  |---|---|---|
  | 1,000 | 3.85 → 3.83 / 3.76 → 3.93 | 1.90 → 1.91 / 1.90 → 2.02 |
  | 16 | 3.88 → 4.23 / 3.86 → 4.22 | 2.55 → 3.12 / 2.47 → 4.54 (varying from run to run) |

  At 16 elements that is about 6 ns per call on JDK 21 and 9 to 33 ns on JDK 8. Two alternatives were measured and were no cheaper at that size: scanning every result, and scanning without the `contains` pre-check.
  * **Tests.** `ConcurrentSetBehaviorTest` (1): for 300 ms one thread adds and removes `null` while another calls `toArray(new Object[0])`; every element must be `null` or one of the set's strings. Fails on the previous code (243,273 stray markers). Full suite: 21,244 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`ConcurrentSet.retainAll` keeps what the argument contains by the argument's own rule.** Given a collection other than a `ConcurrentSet`, it copied the argument into a `HashSet` and kept the elements that copy contained, so membership was decided by `equals`. A set that decides membership another way was overruled:
  * a case-insensitive `TreeSet` holding `"A"` removed `"a"`;
  * an identity set kept a string merely equal to its member.

  The `Collection` contract, and `HashSet.retainAll`, ask the argument (`c.contains`). Now an argument that is a `Set` but not a `HashSet` is asked directly, element by element. A `HashSet`, `LinkedHashSet`, `List` or other collection compares by `equals` anyway and keeps the fast copy. Cost, retaining 500 of 1,000 elements:
  * `HashSet` and `List` arguments: unchanged;
  * an unmodifiable set: faster, 11.2 → 7.3 ns per element on JDK 21 and 9.2 → 6.5 on JDK 8 (no copy);
  * a `TreeSet`: 12.0 → 25.8 ns on JDK 21 and 9.5 → 25.0 on JDK 8. The tree is now asked, as `HashSet.retainAll` asks it, and its comparator is what decides.
  * **Tests.** `ConcurrentSetBehaviorTest` (+1): `retainAll` given a case-insensitive `TreeSet`, an identity set, a set holding `null`, and a `List` with duplicates must keep what the argument contains. Fails on the previous code. Full suite: 21,245 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`ConcurrentNavigableMapNullSafe` orders its keys as its `comparator()` says.** Two ways its order and `comparator()` disagreed:
  * **Natural order (`comparator()` returns `null`).** Keys were compared with `compareTo` only when their classes were identical; otherwise they were ordered by class name. Enum constants with bodies have classes of their own (`Op$1`), and a subclass of a `Comparable` class has another class too, so these keys sorted apart from their siblings: `[B, A, C]` for an enum `A, B, C`. `headMap(B)` came out empty, and `new TreeMap<>(map)`, trusting the reported natural order, could not find `B`. Now constants of one enum, and keys whose common class is itself `Comparable` (a class with its subclasses), compare by `compareTo`. Unrelated classes still order by class name, so a plain `Object` key next to a `String` one still sorts without throwing. `ConcurrentNavigableSetNullSafe` already ordered these correctly.
  * **A comparator that places `null`** (`Comparator.nullsFirst(...)`). The map always put the `null` key last whatever the comparator said, while `comparator()` reported that comparator, so the map, its views, `firstKey()` and a `TreeMap` copy disagreed. Now, as in `ConcurrentNavigableSetNullSafe`, the `null` key is offered to the comparator first, and goes last only if the comparator rejects `null` (`NullPointerException`).

  The comparator's common case (two non-null keys: the user comparator, or two keys of one class) is kept small, with nulls and mixed classes out of line. The first version, all in one lambda, made `get` 30% slower, presumably because the larger comparator no longer inlined into the skip list. `get` over 10,000 keys, medians of five runs, is on par within run-to-run variation:
  * natural order: 163.1 → 162.3 ns on JDK 21, 152.1 → 162.3 on JDK 8 (old runs ranged 145–174);
  * with a comparator: 160.5 → 177.9 ns on JDK 21 (new runs 165–192), 173.7 → 158.6 on JDK 8.
  * **Tests.** `ConcurrentNavigableMapNullSafeOrderTest` (4):
    * enum constants with bodies, including `headMap` and a `TreeMap` copy;
    * a `Comparable` class mixed with its subclass;
    * unrelated classes, a plain `Object` among them;
    * `Comparator.nullsFirst`, checked against a `TreeMap` forwards and descending, plus a comparator that rejects `null`.

    The first, second and fourth fail on the previous code. Full suite: 21,249 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **In a natural-order `ConcurrentNavigableMapNullSafe`, two equal keys of a class that is not `Comparable` are now one key, and lookups of such keys are faster.** Keys of one non-`Comparable` class were ordered by identity hash, so two equal but distinct keys were two keys. `get(Arrays.asList(1, 2))` found nothing unless given the very instance put, a second `put` added a second entry, and `map.equals(hashMap)` was `true` while `hashMap.equals(map)` was `false`. A `Map`'s keys are equal by `equals`. Such keys are now ordered by hash code, then `equals`; distinct keys whose hash codes collide still get a stable tie-breaker, as before.

  Ordering by hash code alone made `get` slower (lists of three `Integer`s: 170 → 214 ns on JDK 21, about double on JDK 8), since each of the ~14 comparisons per lookup computed both keys' hash codes. So the map now holds each such key with its hash code computed once, when the key goes in or is looked up. A comparison is then an `int` compare, and `get` is faster than before for both kinds of key:
  * lists: 163.8 → 109.9 ns on JDK 21, 184.9 → 145.8 on JDK 8;
  * plain `Object`s: 164.8 → 108.1 ns on JDK 21, 158.6 → 135.8 on JDK 8, where the old comparison called `System.identityHashCode` for both keys each time.

  `String` keys are on par: medians 162.3 → 159.8 ns with natural order on JDK 21, 158.7 → 159.3 on JDK 8.
  * **Tests.** `ConcurrentNavigableMapNullSafeOrderTest` (+1): `get`, `containsKey` and a second `put` with an equal list key must find the one entry. `equals` with a `HashMap` must hold both ways, and distinct lists and plain objects must stay distinct. Fails on the previous code. Full suite: 21,250 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **PERFORMANCE**: **`ConcurrentList` walks its elements 2–6x faster: `toArray`, `contains`, `indexOf`, `lastIndexOf`, `hashCode`, `addAll`, and the copy behind `add` / `remove` / `addAll` at an index.** Elements live in 1,024-slot buckets kept in a `ConcurrentHashMap` keyed by `Integer` bucket number. Every loop over the list looked its element's bucket up once per element, with the bucket arithmetic done by division with a correction for negative positions. That is a map lookup by a boxed key, which allocates once the bucket number passes 127 (positions past 131,071). Appends also incremented the tail and size counters atomically per element. Now:
  * each loop looks a bucket up once and runs over its slots;
  * the bucket number and offset are a shift and a mask, which give the floor and the mathematical modulus for negative positions too;
  * appends move tail and size once, after the elements are stored, which is safe since only `size()` and `isEmpty()` read them without the lock.

  ns per element, old → new:

  | Operation | JDK 21, 1,000 | JDK 21, 200,000 | JDK 8, 1,000 | JDK 8, 200,000 |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | `toArray` | 3.3 → 1.1 | 2.8 → 1.4 | 2.2 → 0.5 | 2.3 → 0.6 |
  | `contains` | 2.85 → 0.71 | 2.78 → 0.81 | 2.20 → 0.59 | 2.41 → 0.64 |
  | `hashCode` | 3.0 → 0.8 | 2.7 → 1.2 | 2.1 → 0.9 | 2.4 → 0.8 |
  | `addAll` | 4.5 → 1.2 | 4.9 → 1.2 | 3.2 → 0.6 | 3.7 → 0.6 |

  `lastIndexOf` moved likewise, about 3.2 → 0.8. Found by the review of the concurrent collections (its L5).
  * **Tests.** `ConcurrentListDifferentialTest` (1): 12 seeds × 400 random operations, compared after each step with an `ArrayList`: contents, `indexOf`, `lastIndexOf`, `contains`, `hashCode`, `equals` both ways, `toString` and `get`. The operations include `addFirst` and `pollFirst`/`pollLast` in bulk, which run the list across bucket boundaries at both ends with the head below zero. It passes on the previous code too (a guard), and catches planted mutants: a truncating bucket division fails, an off-by-one slot read at a bucket's end fails, and a `lastIndexOf` that skips a bucket's first slot never finishes. Full suite: 21,251 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`ConcurrentList`'s bulk operations no longer deadlock when two lists work on each other.** `addAll`, `addAll(index, c)`, `removeAll`, `retainAll`, `remove(Object)` and `removeLastOccurrence` held the list's write lock while running the other collection's code. That code is the argument's iteration, its `contains`, or an element's `equals`. When the other collection is a `ConcurrentList`, that code takes its read lock, so `a.addAll(b)` meeting `b.addAll(a)`, likewise for the other operations, deadlocked within a few dozen rounds. So did `a.remove(probe)` meeting `b.remove(probe)` for lists that hold each other, since comparing the probe with a list iterates it. The JVM's deadlock detector does not see read-lock holders, so one variant hung with no report. Now no other code runs under the write lock:
  * `addAll` and `addAll(index, c)` take `c.toArray()` before locking;
  * `removeAll` and `retainAll` decide on a snapshot, asking `c` with no lock held, then apply the decision under the write lock if the list still holds exactly the snapshot's elements (compared by identity), and decide again if not;
  * `remove(Object)` copies elements out under the read lock in doubling chunks and compares them with no lock held, so a match at index i copies about i elements. It removes under the write lock if indexes 0..i are unchanged, and reports a miss only once the whole list is seen unchanged;
  * `removeLastOccurrence` searches a snapshot and verifies the part from the match to the end.

  These operations are no longer atomic with respect to `c` changing meanwhile, which no caller could rely on. Cost against the speed-up above: `addAll` on par; `removeAll` and `retainAll` +7–17% on JDK 21 and 13% faster on JDK 8 at 10,000 elements. `remove(Object)` of an element near the front is on par. In the middle of 10,000 elements it costs +33% on JDK 21 (41.6 → 55.2 µs) and +27% on JDK 8: the search copies the half it passes, while the removal itself still copies and rebuilds the whole list. Against the code before both changes, every one of these operations is faster.
  * **Tests.** `ConcurrentListBulkTest` (7):
    * `addAll`, `addAll(index)`, `removeAll` and `retainAll` of two lists given each other, 300 rounds each;
    * `remove(Object)` on two lists holding each other;
    * the bulk operations against `ArrayList`'s answers, the list given itself included;
    * a match at every chunk and bucket boundary.

    The five concurrency tests fail on the previous code (each deadlocks within 25 rounds). Full suite: 21,258 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **A `ConcurrentList` stream is a snapshot, as its iteration is, and no longer skips elements or throws when the list changes; it is also 3–5x faster.** The class promises point-in-time iteration, and `iterator()` copies the list. `spliterator()` was not overridden, though. On JDK 9 and later the `List` default for a `RandomAccess` list reads the live list with `get(i)`, so a stream over a changing list:
  * skipped elements: 1,123 times in the review's probe, against 9 clean runs;
  * threw `ConcurrentModificationException` (383 times) or `IndexOutOfBoundsException`.

  `spliterator()` now runs over a snapshot taken when the stream is made, as `CopyOnWriteArrayList`'s does. A stream summing the list: 9.3 → 1.7 ns per element on JDK 21, 2.9–3.8 → 0.9–1.0 on JDK 8, since one array copy replaces a read lock and a bucket lookup per `get`.
  * **Tests.** `ConcurrentListSnapshotTest` (1): a stream over 20 elements whose `peek()` drains 10 of them must still see all 20. Fails on the previous code (`ConcurrentModificationException`). Full suite: 21,259 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **Serializing a `ConcurrentList` while another thread changes it writes a list it really held.** Default serialization copied the bucket map, head, tail and size counter one field at a time with no lock held. A copy written while another thread appended and polled could read back with `null`s that were never added (8,402 of the review's probe runs), elements out of order (588), or a missing bucket, so the copy threw `NullPointerException` when used. `writeObject` now holds the read lock while writing the default form, so writes wait and the copy is consistent. The serial form is unchanged, so existing streams still read. Its size is still large (2,002 bytes for an empty list), which a serialization proxy would fix at the cost of compatibility; that was not done. Writing a 1,000-element list is on par: 51.7 vs 51.8 µs on JDK 21, 62.5 vs 62.1 µs on JDK 8.
  * **Tests.** `ConcurrentListSnapshotTest` (+1): for 400 ms one thread uses the list as a queue (append the next number, poll the oldest) while another serializes it and reads it back. Every copy must be a run of consecutive numbers with no `null`. Fails on the previous code (`NullPointerException` from a missing bucket). Full suite: 21,260 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`ConcurrentList.removeIf`, `replaceAll`, `sort` and `Collections.sort` work, instead of throwing `UnsupportedOperationException`.** The `List` / `Collection` defaults for these go through `iterator().remove()` or `listIterator().set()`. `ConcurrentList`'s iterators run over a snapshot and reject both, so all four threw. They now work like `removeAll` above: the predicate, operator or comparator runs over a snapshot with no lock held, and the result is applied under the write lock if the list still holds exactly the snapshot's elements, or computed again. `removeIf` rebuilds the list without the removed elements; `replaceAll` and `sort` write the new values into the existing slots. `removeAll` and `retainAll` keep their own loop and are on par: going through the shared predicate instead cost them up to 23%.
  * **Tests.** `ConcurrentListBulkTest` (+1): `removeIf`, `replaceAll`, `sort(null)`, `sort(reverseOrder())` and `Collections.sort` must give `ArrayList`'s results. Fails on the previous code (`UnsupportedOperationException`). Full suite: 21,261 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`FastWriter` loses nothing when its underlying writer fails, and is closed after a failed `close()`.** Three failure paths left it broken or lost data:
  * **`write(int)`.** It flushes the buffer as soon as it fills. If that flush threw, the buffer stayed full. The next `write(int)` then stored past its end (`ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException`), and every write and flush after it failed too. It now retries the flush before storing; `BufferedWriter` outputs `abcde` in the same scenario, and so does `FastWriter` now.
  * **`write(String, off, len)`.** After a failed flush, it skipped the flush of the full buffer and wrote the new text over it, losing the buffered characters with no error (`xyz12` instead of `abcdxyz12`). The full buffer is now always sent before it is reused.
  * **`close()`.** If the underlying `close()` threw, the writer stayed open, and a later write went through; with json-io's pooled buffer, possibly into a buffer already handed back. A flush failure was also replaced by the close failure. The writer is now closed whatever fails, and a flush failure is thrown with the close failure suppressed into it, as `BufferedWriter` does.

  Cost: string writes are on par (0.428 vs 0.429 ns per char on JDK 21, 0.387 vs 0.390 on JDK 8). `write(int)` costs about 0.05 ns more per char (0.547 → 0.598 on JDK 21, 0.572 → 0.637 on JDK 8), for the check before the store. Flushing lazily, as `BufferedWriter` does, would avoid that, but changes when a full buffer is sent, which `FastWriterTest` pins.
  * **Tests.** `FastWriterFailureTest` (3): a `write(int)` and a string write after a failed flush, against a writer whose first write fails; and `close()` with a failing underlying `close()`, alone and after a failing flush. All 3 fail on the previous code. `FastWriterTest.testWriteStringWhenBufferExactlyFull` asserted the dropping behavior, a full buffer written over. It now asserts the full buffer is sent first. Found by the review of the Fast streams (its F2, F3, F8). Full suite: 21,264 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`FastReader.readLine` no longer spins forever when given a huge `maxLen`, and no longer reads past the end of input.**
  * **The spin.** `readLine` bounded each scan at the current position plus the room left in `maxLen`. With `maxLen` near `Integer.MAX_VALUE` (no limit, in effect) and reading already started, that sum overflowed negative: nothing was copied, the position never moved, and the call spun forever. `readUntilBorrowed`'s tests treat `Integer.MAX_VALUE` as a legal `maxLen`, so callers can reasonably pass it here too. The bound is now the smaller of the room left and the characters buffered, which cannot overflow.
  * **Reading past the end.** Once the input had ended, every further `readLine` asked the underlying reader again (on a terminal or a pipe, each such read blocks again). It now answers `-1` at once, as `read()`, `readUntil` and the borrowed methods already did.

  `readLine` throughput is on par: 0.272 vs 0.272 ns per char on JDK 21, 0.281 vs 0.280 on JDK 8. Found by the review of the Fast streams (its F1 and F6).
  * **Tests.** `FastReaderEdgeTest` (2): `readLine` with `maxLen` `Integer.MAX_VALUE` after one `read()` must return the rest of the line within 5 s. Further `readLine` calls at the end must not read the underlying reader again. Both fail on the previous code. Full suite: 21,266 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **PERFORMANCE**: **`FastReader` keeps pushed-back characters in its input buffer, making json-io's parsing up to 22% faster, and three edge cases are fixed.** Pushback used to live in a separate array, so every `read()` checked that array before the buffer, and `readLine` / `readUntil` drained it first. Now each refill leaves room at the front of the one array for the requested pushback capacity. A pushback stores the character just before the next one to read, and `read()` is a position check and an array load, with the end of input recorded once.

  json-io, 512 KB of JSON (ms per document, old → new):

  | | JDK 21 | JDK 8 |
  |---|---|---|
  | tokenizer, pretty-printed | 0.99 → 0.77 | 0.73 → 0.70 |
  | tokenizer, compact | 1.07 → 1.02 | 0.96 → 0.88 |
  | `toMaps`, pretty-printed | 1.26 → 1.08 | on par |
  | `toMaps`, compact | on par | 2.05 → 1.80 (noisy) |

  Fixed along the way:
  * **`read(char[], off, len)`** kept reading until it had `len` characters, so on a pipe or socket it blocked although it already held data to return (`Reader.skip`, `transferTo` and `read(CharBuffer)` call it). Like `BufferedReader`, it now reads the underlying reader again only while that reader is `ready()`; `ready()` is overridden too.
  * **`readLine`** meeting `\r` as the last character of a buffer fill peeked for `\n` with `read()` plus `pushback()`. That threw "Pushback buffer is full" when the reader had no pushback capacity, and otherwise used up a pushback slot the caller never pushed. It now looks at the next fill directly.
  * **`readUntil`** satisfied by pushed-back characters alone still refilled from the source, blocking on a pipe. With `maxLen` 0 and pushback pending at the end of input, it returned `-1`, though `read()` would then return a character. Both now answer from what is there.

  Behavior that changes: pushback capacity is now "at least" the requested size. It is exactly that size right after a refill and more in mid-buffer, so a reader built with capacity 0 accepts pushback in mid-buffer. The borrowed methods no longer return `COPY_REQUIRED` merely because pushback is pending. A caller-supplied buffer keeps its first `pushbackBuffer.length` characters for pushback (json-io's pooled 65,536-character buffer gives up 16).
  * **Tests.** `FastReaderBehaviorTest` (4):
    * A differential test: 30,000 random texts (CR, LF, quotes, backslash and surrogate pairs) over buffer sizes 1–8 and pushback capacities 0–4, 30 random operations each, every answer checked against a model pushback reader. The operations are `read`, `pushback`, `readUntil`, `readUntilBorrowed`, `readLine`, `readLineBorrowed` and `read(char[])`.
    * Three targeted tests: `read(char[])` on a source that gives 5 characters and then blocks; `readLine` with `\r` at a fill's end, with pushback capacity 0 and 1; `readUntil` satisfied by pushback on a blocking source, and with `maxLen` 0 at the end.

    All 4 fail on the previous code. The 55 existing `FastReaderTest` tests pass unchanged. Found by the review of the Fast streams (its PERF-1, F4, F5, F7), whose prototype this follows; its 1.8 million-sequence fuzz and a 3 MB json-io round trip (escapes, surrogate pairs, CRLF) were clean on JDK 21 and JDK 8. Full suite: 21,270 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **PERFORMANCE**: **`FastByteArrayOutputStream` doubles its buffer when it grows, as `ByteArrayOutputStream` does: bulk writes about twice as fast.** It grew by 1.5x, which copies the contents more often on the way to a large size; the comment said this reduced the risk of overflow, which the guards right below the growth step already handle. Writing 1 MB in 8 KB chunks costs 0.089 → 0.047 ns per byte from a 32-byte start on JDK 21 (0.093 → 0.058 on JDK 8), and 0.092 → 0.047 from a 16 KB start; 64 KB costs 0.094 → 0.058 (0.117 → 0.067 on JDK 8). Single-byte writes are slightly faster: 1.80 → 1.77 ns on JDK 21, 0.81 → 0.77 on JDK 8. The price is more unused capacity, at most half the buffer instead of a third. IOUtilities and UrlUtilities use this stream. Found by the review of the Fast streams (its PERF-2); it checked the overflow guards with 2x growth in 3.5 million cases, sizes above 1 GB included.
  * **Tests.** `FastByteArrayOutputStreamDifferentialTest` (1): 200 runs of 60 random single-byte writes, bulk writes and resets from random initial sizes, compared after every step with `ByteArrayOutputStream`. It passes on the previous code too (a guard), and fails on a planted mutant that grows one byte short. Full suite: 21,271 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **PERFORMANCE**: **Emptying an `IdentitySet` that was once large is cheap again: Traverser over a wide graph followed by a long chain is about 60x faster.** `remove` (and the iterator's `remove`) cleared the whole table whenever the set became empty. The table stays as large as the set ever was, so once a set had held a million elements, every add-then-remove cycle that emptied it cost a pass over two million slots (343 µs in the review's probe). `Traverser`'s queue of objects to visit empties at every step of a chain. A graph that had once been wide therefore made each later step pay: a 100,000-element array followed by a 20,000-node chain took 801 ms to traverse on JDK 21 (749 ms on JDK 8). An emptying remove now leaves a tombstone like any other remove, and `add` already rehashes the tombstones away once live plus deleted slots reach the threshold. That traversal now takes 13 ms on JDK 21 and 15 ms on JDK 8. Add, `contains` and remove are on par at 1,000 and 1,000,000 elements: add plus `contains` 10.27 vs 10.26 ns on JDK 21, 8.56 vs 8.65 on JDK 8. `MultiKeyMap`, `DeepEquals` and others use `IdentitySet` the same way. Found by the reviews of `IdentitySet` and `Traverser` (IS-4, TR-1).
  * **Tests.** `IdentitySetBehaviorTest` (1): a set grown to a million elements and emptied must then run 2,000 add/remove cycles of one element in under 200 ms. Fails on the previous code (1,110 ms). Full suite: 21,272 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`IdentitySet`'s iterator is fail-fast: a change to the set made other than through the iterator throws `ConcurrentModificationException`, as the JDK's identity set does, instead of corrupting the set.** The iterator kept going over a table that may have changed or been rebuilt:
  * **Removal behind its back.** After `next()`, a `remove` on the set followed by the iterator's `remove()` left `size()` at 1 while two elements were reachable. An emptying remove then lost the survivor.
  * **Growth behind its back.** After an `add` grew the table, the iterator's `remove()` deleted a different element than `next()` had returned.
  * **A stale `hasNext()`.** After a removal, `hasNext()` answered `true` and `next()` then threw `NoSuchElementException`.

  A modification count, bumped by adds, removes and clears, is checked by `next()` and `remove()`; the iterator's own `remove()` keeps it in step. Cost: add, `contains` and remove on par; iteration 2.53 → 2.58 ns per element on JDK 21, 2.23 → 2.47 on JDK 8 (the check `HashMap`'s iterator makes too).
  * **Tests.** `IdentitySetBehaviorTest` (+1): a removal from the set, then the iterator's `remove()` and `next()`; an `add` that grows the table, then the iterator's `remove()`; and the iterator's own removals, which must keep working. Fails on the previous code. Found by the review of `IdentitySet` (its IS-3). Full suite: 21,273 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`IdentitySet.removeAll` and `hashCode` behave as the JDK's identity set does (`Collections.newSetFromMap(new IdentityHashMap<>())`), as the class claims to be a drop-in for.**
  * **`removeAll`.** It was the inherited `AbstractSet` version, whose answer depends on the sizes. When this set was larger, it removed the argument's elements by identity; otherwise it asked the argument's `contains`. A string equal to, but not the same object as, an element was removed from a one-element set but not from a two-element one. It now asks the argument (`c.contains(e)`) for each element, as the JDK's identity set does, walking the table directly. When the argument is itself an `IdentitySet`, both sides compare by identity, so it removes the argument's elements one by one if those are fewer.
  * **`hashCode`.** It called each element's own `hashCode()`. It now sums the elements' identity hash codes, as the JDK's identity set does, so equal sets of either kind hash alike (they did not: 120 against 1,950,701,640 in the review's probe). It also no longer overflows the stack for a set that contains itself, or contains a list that contains itself.

  Cost (1,000 elements, removing 500): `removeAll` given an `IdentitySet` is on par (1.81 vs 1.82 ns per element on JDK 21). Given another collection, it now asks that collection about every element, 1.86 → 3.30 ns on JDK 21 and 1.32 → 3.19 on JDK 8, as the JDK's identity set does. `hashCode` is on par or faster: 2.29 → 1.42–2.92 ns on JDK 21, 1.73 → 1.42 on JDK 8.
  * **Tests.** `IdentitySetBehaviorTest` (+2): `removeAll` of an equal stranger from sets of one, two and three elements must answer as the JDK's identity set does; `hashCode` and `equals` must agree with it both ways, and a set holding itself must hash without recursion. Both fail on the previous code. Found by the review of `IdentitySet` (its IS-1 and IS-2). Full suite: 21,275 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **Adding an element already in an `IdentitySet` no longer grows its table.** At the resize threshold, `add` doubled the table before finding the element was already there: 16 slots became 32 for an `add` that returned `false`. At the threshold, and only there, `add` now checks for the element first. Add plus `contains` is on par: 11.27 vs 11.30 ns at 1,000 elements on JDK 21, 8.79 vs 8.65 on JDK 8.
  * **Tests.** `IdentitySetBehaviorTest` (+1): at 12 elements in 16 slots, re-adding one must leave the table at 16 slots, and adding a new element must still double it. Fails on the previous code. Found by the review of `IdentitySet` (its IS-5). Full suite: 21,276 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`IntervalSet`'s reads see each write whole. `contains` no longer reports a value uncovered while the interval covering it is being split, and a copy (also behind `union` and `difference`) no longer keeps half of a write.**
  * **Point reads.** Reads took no lock and relied on the order of a write's steps, but `floorEntry` finds a node, then reads its end. When a split linked the right-hand fragment in between, the reader found the left interval and read its trimmed end: `contains(25)` answered false while [20-30) was covered before, during and after the split (19,981 of 23.7M reads in the review's probe). `intervalContaining`, `previousInterval` and `nextInterval` erred the same way, and the navigation methods could return an interval that only a half-made merge held.
  * **Copies and whole-set readers.** The copy constructor copied the live map without the lock and kept what it saw: `{[0-100), [60-100)}`, total duration 140 (4.8M of 11.4M copies in the review's probe). `equals`, `hashCode`, `toString`, `totalDuration`, `intersection`, `intersects` and the range lists walked the live map the same way: `equals` found the set equal to `{[0-40)}`, a state it never held.
  * **The fix.** The lock is now a `StampedLock`; writers hold it exclusively, as before. A read runs without locking and is kept if no write started or finished meanwhile. Otherwise it runs again under the read lock, or over a copy for the readers that call the bounds' own `hashCode`, `equals` and `toString` or the caller's `totalDuration` function, so that code never runs under the lock. Readers of two sets never hold both locks. `contains` keeps a covering answer without validating, because every step of a write stores only coverage the set held before it or will hold after it. Iterators and the key sets stay weakly consistent, as documented; `snapshot()` is the consistent list. `totalDuration`'s function may be applied more than once when a write overlaps the call, as a `ConcurrentSkipListMap.compute` function may be.
  * **Faster copies.** The copy constructor builds the skip list from the sorted source in linear time instead of inserting each entry: 62.1 → 4.3 µs at 1,000 intervals on JDK 21 (63.4 → 11.4 on JDK 8), and `union` 260 → 96 µs (285 → 244).
  * **Cost.** Point reads are on par: `contains` 27.4 → 27.6 ns at 100 intervals and 191.6 → 186.3 at 100,000 on JDK 21 (31.8 → 29.6 and 222.4 → 210.9 on JDK 8). `intervalContaining`, `previousInterval`, `nextInterval` and the range lists are on par or faster on both. `first` and `last` pay for the validation, about 1 ns (0.77 → 1.66 on JDK 21). `equals`, `hashCode`, `toString`, `totalDuration` and `intersection` are within 6% on JDK 21 and up to 19% faster on JDK 8; `intersects`, which stops at the first overlap, goes 10.2 → 11.5 ns. Writes are on par. With a writer that never pauses, readers of covered values keep 90% of their throughput on JDK 21 (22.7 → 20.3 reads per µs) and 95% on JDK 8. An uncovered answer must be confirmed, so such a read can wait for that writer.
  * **Tests.** `IntervalSetConsistencyTest` (4), each against a writer toggling merges and splits:
    * Four readers, each on its own thread, must never find a right-fragment value uncovered (the old code erred in every run, 1 to 11,946 wrong answers per reader), nor a never-covered value covered.
    * Copies, `union` and `snapshot` must never hold overlapping intervals (68,570 to 102,461 per run before).
    * The whole-set readers must answer for a state the set held (before: `equals` 16,459, `hashCode` 87,516, `toString` 67,302, `totalDuration` 50,971, range lists 42,201, `intersection` 1,921).

    Planted mutants fail them: dropping any of the validations, or copying the live map. Found by the review of `IntervalSet` (its IV-1 and IV-2). Full suite: 21,280 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`IntervalSet.keySet()` and `descendingKeySet()` are read-only views.** They returned the skip list's own writable key views. `pollFirst()`, `remove()`, `clear()` or a `headSet(...).clear()` on them deleted intervals without the set's lock, alongside its merges and splits; in the review's probe, `pollFirst()` and `remove(10)` emptied the set. They are still live views (they show later writes) and still weakly consistent; change the set through its own methods. **Behavior change:** a mutating call on either view now throws `UnsupportedOperationException`; nothing in java-util, json-io or n-cube made one. Cost: iterating the keys is on par (153.8 vs 149.9 ns for 100 keys on JDK 21, 171.7 vs 183.8 on JDK 8). On JDK 8, getting a view costs about 4 ns more for the wrapper (on par on JDK 21).
  * **Tests.** `IntervalSetBehaviorTest` (1): every mutating call on both views must throw, and leave the set as it was; the views must still show later writes. Fails on the previous code. Found by the review of `IntervalSet` (its IV-3). Full suite: 21,281 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`IntervalSet.removeExact` matches the end as the set orders values (`compareTo`), as it already matched the start.** The start is found in the skip list by `compareTo`, but the end was checked with `equals`, so for `BigDecimal` the scale had to match too: `removeExact(1.0, 2.00)` on `[1.0-2.0)` returned `false` and kept the interval. The end now matches when `compareTo` says it is equal. Cost: on par (668 vs 667 ns for a `removeExact` and re-add at 1,000 intervals on JDK 21, 657 vs 662 on JDK 8). Not changed: `Interval.equals` and `IntervalSet.equals` still compare bounds with `equals`, so `{[1-2.0)}` and `{[1-2.00)}` are unequal while their difference is empty. Whether they should be equal is left as an open question.
  * **Tests.** `IntervalSetBehaviorTest` (+1): `removeExact` with differently scaled but equal bounds must remove the interval, and with an unequal end must not. Fails on the previous code. Found by the review of `IntervalSet` (its IV-4). Full suite: 21,282 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`IntervalSet.totalDuration()` throws `ArithmeticException` when a numeric difference does not fit a long, as its Javadoc promised, instead of returning a wrong duration.** For numeric bounds it subtracted `longValue()`s, which wraps a `BigInteger` and saturates a `double`: `[0, 2^64 + 5)` came out as 5 ns and `[0, 1e30)` as `Long.MAX_VALUE` ns. Infinite and NaN bounds were also accepted. `BigInteger` and `BigDecimal` bounds are now subtracted exactly; a `double` or `float` bound must lie within the long range. The difference must fit a long, so `[2^64, 2^64 + 5)` is still 5 ns. Whole parts are taken as before: `[0.5, 3.7)` is 3 ns. Cost: integer bounds on par (4.60 vs 4.59 µs per 1,000 intervals on JDK 21); `double` bounds +4% on JDK 21, +8% on JDK 8 for the range check (5.17 vs 4.96 µs and 5.20 vs 4.81 µs).
  * **Tests.** `IntervalSetBehaviorTest` (+1): differences past a long and infinite bounds must throw, and in-range differences of huge bounds, fractional bounds and `BigDecimal` bounds must keep their values. Fails on the previous code. Found by the review of `IntervalSet` (its IV-5). Full suite: 21,283 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`TrackingMap` works with null keys wherever the wrapped map takes them, and `expungeUnused()` keeps an entry read through a key the wrapped map matches, by that map's own key matching.**
  * **Null keys.** For a concurrent map, read keys were recorded in a `ConcurrentHashMap` key set, which rejects null. `CaseInsensitiveMap`, `ConcurrentHashMapNullSafe` and `ConcurrentNavigableMapNullSafe` all take a null key and are all `ConcurrentMap`s, so `get(null)`, `containsKey(null)` and `remove(null)` threw `NullPointerException`; `remove(null)` also left the entry in the map. Read keys of a concurrent map are now recorded in java-util's `ConcurrentSet`, which takes null.
  * **Key matching.** Expunging kept the entries whose keys `equals()` a recorded read key. A sorted map matches by its ordering, so an entry read through a different spelling was expunged: `get("APPLE")` on a `TreeMap(String.CASE_INSENSITIVE_ORDER)` holding `apple`, or `get(2.00)` on a `TreeMap` holding the `BigDecimal` 2.0. For an `IdentityHashMap`, an unread entry was kept because a missed `get` used an equal but different key object.
    * For a navigable map, `expungeUnused()` now asks the map which of its keys each read matched (its floor entry, compared by its ordering), so reads stay O(1).
    * For an `IdentityHashMap`, read keys are recorded by identity.
  * **Cost.** `get` on par for every map kind: 7.25 vs 7.18 ns on a 16-key `CaseInsensitiveMap` and 7.01 vs 7.09 on a 1,000-key `ConcurrentHashMap` on JDK 21; 12.31 vs 11.57 on JDK 8 for the latter. `get` on an `IdentityHashMap` is faster on JDK 8 (3.56 vs 5.96 ns). `expungeUnused()` on a sorted map now makes one map lookup per read key: on a 1,000-entry `TreeMap` with 500 keys read, about 41 vs 21 µs on JDK 21 (29 vs 16 on JDK 8). Nothing in java-util, json-io or n-cube calls it; on other maps it is on par.
  * **Tests.** `TrackingMapBehaviorTest` (2): `get`, `containsKey`, `keysUsed`, `expungeUnused` and `remove` with a null key on four maps that take one. Expunging after reads through other spellings on case-insensitive `TreeMap`, `ConcurrentSkipListMap` and `ConcurrentNavigableMapNullSafe`, a `BigDecimal` key, and an `IdentityHashMap`. Both fail on the previous code. Found by the review of `TrackingMap` (its TM-3 and TM-4). Full suite: 21,285 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **PERFORMANCE**: **`TrackingMap` re-reads of a key it has already recorded are 1.6x to 2.7x faster on one thread and up to 17x faster from four threads: the key is only looked up in the tracking set.** Every `get`, `containsKey`, `getOrDefault`, `compute*`, `merge` and navigation read called the tracking set's `add`, which writes even when the key is already there. For a concurrent map, `ConcurrentHashMap`'s `add` locks a hash bin on JDK 8, and on JDK 9+ unless the key is first in its bin, so readers of the same keys queued behind one another; for a `HashSet` it stores the value again. Reads record a key only if the set does not hold it yet. `CaseInsensitiveMap` is a `ConcurrentMap`, so n-cube's rule input took this path.
  * **Single thread, re-reads.** `CaseInsensitiveMap` of 16 keys: 7.21 → 4.54 ns on JDK 21, 11.00 → 6.83 on JDK 8. `ConcurrentHashMap`: 6.94 → 3.16 and 12.93 → 6.29. `HashMap`: 5.63 → 2.09 on JDK 21, on par on JDK 8. `TreeMap`: 48.1 → 42.3, on par on JDK 8.
  * **Four threads on 16 hot keys.** `ConcurrentHashMap`: 126 → 1,658 million reads per second on JDK 21, 61 → 1,043 on JDK 8. `CaseInsensitiveMap`: 116 → 512, and 61 → 703.
  * **First reads** (a lookup, then the add) are not slower: reading every key of a fresh 1,000-entry map once, map copy included, took 6.7 vs 11.7 µs on a `HashMap` on JDK 21. In isolation, a lookup before adding a new key is on par.
  * **Tests.** `TrackingMapReadMarkTest` (2):
    * Random reads, misses, re-reads, writes and removes on five kinds of wrapped map must record exactly the keys a model says were read.
    * Reads of overlapping key ranges from four threads must all be recorded.

    Both pass on the previous code. A mutant that inverts the lookup fails both. Found by the review of `TrackingMap` (its TM-PERF). Full suite: 21,287 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **A `TrackingMap` view (`subMap`, `headMap`, `tailMap`) no longer wipes its parent's record of reads.** A view shares its parent's set of read keys. `expungeUnused()` on a view correctly removed the view's unread entries, but then reset the shared set to the view's own keys. `clear()` and `replaceContents()` on a view emptied the shared set. Either way, the parent's next `expungeUnused()` removed entries it had read: after a view's expunge it kept only `[b]` of `[a, b, d]` read, and after `headMap("b").clear()` it kept nothing although `d` had been read. A view now forgets only reads of keys it holds: its expunge leaves the shared set alone (the entries it removes were unread), and its `clear()` forgets only the reads of its own keys. Reads are unchanged; `clear()` and `expungeUnused()` take one more branch.
  * **Tests.** `TrackingMapBehaviorTest` (+1): reads in the parent must survive a view's `expungeUnused()`, `clear()` and `replaceContents()`, and the parent's next expunge must keep them. Fails on the previous code; reverting only the `clear()` change also fails it. Found by the review of `TrackingMap` (its TM-1 and TM-2). Full suite: 21,288 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`equals()` between two `TrackingMap`s records no reads in the other one.** It compared the wrapped map against the other `TrackingMap` itself, whose `get()` recorded every key as read, so that map's next `expungeUnused()` kept entries the program never read: after `one.equals(two)`, `two.keysUsed()` held all of `[a, b, c, d]`. Another `TrackingMap` is now compared by its wrapped map. A plain map's `equals(trackingMap)` still reads through `get()`, and the Javadoc now says so. Comparing two 1,000-entry `TrackingMap`s is 2x faster (6.9 → 3.1 µs on JDK 21, 6.7 → 3.4 on JDK 8); against a plain map, on par.
  * **Tests.** `TrackingMapBehaviorTest` (+1): `equals` both ways between equal and unequal `TrackingMap`s must leave both `keysUsed()` empty, and the other map's expunge must then remove everything. Fails on the previous code. Found by the review of `TrackingMap` (its TM-5). Full suite: 21,289 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`TrackingMap.putIfAbsent`, both `replace` forms and `remove(key, value)` call the wrapped map's own method, so they are as atomic as that map makes them, and faster.** For a map that is not a `ConcurrentMap`, they did a `get()` then a `put()` under a lock that only `TrackingMap` took. That bypassed the wrapped map's own implementation, including `Collections.synchronizedMap`'s lock: a `putIfAbsent` through the `TrackingMap` racing the map's own `putIfAbsent` could see both return `null` (780 of 50,000 keys in the review's probe). They also bypassed a subclass's overrides. The Javadoc no longer says these methods are "available" only on a `ConcurrentMap` and throw otherwise (they never did). On a `HashMap`, `putIfAbsent` 3.58 → 2.23 ns on JDK 21 (4.90 → 2.92 on JDK 8) and `replace` 5.09 → 2.78 (5.66 → 3.00), one lookup instead of two; on a `ConcurrentHashMap`, on par.
  * **Tests.** `TrackingMapBehaviorTest` (+1): over a `HashMap` subclass, every compound write must reach the subclass's own method with `Map`'s semantics: a null value counts as absent, and a successful `remove(key, value)` forgets the read. A `putIfAbsent` racing the map's own over `Collections.synchronizedMap` must never let both win. Fails on the previous code (0 of 7 calls reached the map's own methods; 2 to 11 keys won twice). Found by the review of `TrackingMap` (its TM-8). Full suite: 21,290 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **MEMORY**: **A `TrackingMap` over a large map no longer allocates a tracking table sized for the whole map on its first read.** The set of read keys was pre-sized to the wrapped map's size, so the first `get()` on a `TrackingMap` over a 1,000,000-entry map allocated a 2M-slot table: 9.43 MB retained per `TrackingMap` after a single `get()` on JDK 21 (8.12 MB on JDK 8). It also took 1,077 µs on JDK 21 (39.6 on JDK 8). The pre-size is now capped at 1,024 keys, and a larger map's set grows as its keys are read. Now: nothing measurable retained, and 1.7 µs (2.8 on JDK 8).
  * **The trade-off.** Maps of up to 1,024 entries are unchanged (reading every key: 8.2 vs 8.5 ns per key on JDK 21). Reading every key of a larger map pays the set's growth on each key's first read: 9.6 → 13.6 ns per key at 100,000 entries on JDK 21 (4.5 → 6.2 on JDK 8), and 10.8 → 13.1 at 1,000,000 (on par on JDK 8). A higher cap would not help unless it reached the map's size: the last few doublings dominate.
  * **Tests.** Behavior is unchanged; the existing `TrackingMap` tests (69) pass, and the review's retained-memory probe gives the numbers above. Found by the review of `TrackingMap` (its TM-10). Full suite: 21,290 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`TrackingMap.replaceContents()` given the map itself, its wrapped map, or a view of either keeps those contents instead of emptying the map.** It cleared the map and then copied from the source, which by then was empty too: `replaceContents(getWrappedMap())` or `replaceContents(this)` printed `{}`, and `replaceContents(headMap("c"))`, meant to keep only the keys below `c`, kept nothing. A source that can share this map's entries is now copied first: this map, its wrapped map (a `TrackingMap` source counts as the map it wraps), or, for a sorted map, any sorted map that could be a view of it. A `TreeMap` or `ConcurrentSkipListMap` itself is never a view (their views are other classes), so it is not copied.
