Cost: on par (3.84 vs 3.82 µs to replace 1,000 entries from a `HashMap` on JDK 21, 6.24 vs 6.24 from a `TreeMap` into a `TreeMap`; likewise on JDK 8); a copy costs a second linear pass when made.
  * **Tests.** `TrackingMapBehaviorTest` (+1): replacing the contents with the map itself, its wrapped map, and a `headMap` view must keep those contents. Fails on the previous code. Found by the review of `TrackingMap` (its TM-11). Full suite: 21,291 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **DOCUMENTATION**: **`TrackingMap`'s Javadoc says what `expungeUnused()` actually removes, and when it is safe to call.**
  * It said `expungeUnused()` removes entries not accessed "since the last call to `expungeUnused()`". Entries that survive an expunge stay marked as read, so a second `expungeUnused()` with no reads in between removes nothing, as `TrackingMapTest` already pins.
  * Removing through `keySet()`, `entrySet()` or `values()` does not forget the key's read, as `remove()` does, so a key put back and never read again survives the next expunge.
  * `expungeUnused()` must not run while other threads read: a `get()` running during it can return a value whose entry it then removes (64,909 such entries over 150 trials in the review's probe). Marking the key before reading it narrows the window but cannot close it, since the expunge checks, then removes; it would also record a read for a lookup that throws, so it was not done.

  Found by the review of `TrackingMap` (its TM-6, TM-7 and TM-9). Full suite: 21,291 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **PERFORMANCE**: **`Traverser` visits a node in half the time: one identity set records every object ever queued, instead of a "visited" set plus a "queued" set that every node was added to, removed from and added to again.** An object is queued at most once and never removed from the set, so the queued-but-unvisited objects are exactly those on the stack. The visit-limit check now counts the stack: same number, same exceptions. Per visited node 90 → 45 ns on JDK 21 and 80 → 40 on JDK 8. A 200,000-node chain takes 12 → 9 ms (14 → 10 on JDK 8), and a 100,000-wide array followed by a 50,000-node chain 11 → 6 ms (11 → 9). The IS-4 fix above had already removed that graph's 2-second pathology. `GraphComparator` walks both graphs with `Traverser`.
  * **Proof of no behavior change.** The review's differential replays 3,000 random graphs: shared nodes, cycles, lists, maps and arrays, skip sets, `collectFields` both ways, and on a third of them small security limits (611 end in a `SecurityException`). Comparing visit order, field values and exceptions between the previous and the new code gave 0 differing graphs on JDK 21 and on JDK 8. A one-character mutant of the limit check (`>=` to `>`) gives 970.
  * **Tests.** `TraverserVisitOrderTest` (1): on 600 random graphs, the visit order must match a model (depth first, each reachable object once, skipped classes neither visited nor walked through, JDK classes as leaves). It passes on the previous code. A mutant that forgets to record queued objects fails it. Found by the review of `Traverser` (its TR-1). Full suite: 21,292 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **PERFORMANCE**: **`Traverser` with `collectFields=true` no longer throws and catches an exception for every unreadable field, which on JDK 17+ is nearly every field of a JDK class: 9x to 29x faster per node there.** `ReflectionUtils` has already tried to make every field it returns accessible. A field it could not is now reported as `"<inaccessible>"` without calling `get()`, whose `IllegalAccessException` took about 600 ns to build and throw. A public field of a public class is still read, since `get()` can succeed when only `setAccessible()` was refused (a `SecurityManager`). On JDK 21, per node: `Integer` 620 → 66 ns, `String` 2,375 → 82, `ArrayList` 1,716 → 77. JDK 8, where these fields are readable, is on par. `GraphComparator`'s three walks and the deprecated `Visitor` APIs read only the nodes, so they now walk with `collectFields=false`; fields are still collected on demand by `getFields()`.
  * **Proof of no behavior change.** The differential over 3,000 random graphs compares visit order, field values and exceptions between the previous and the new code: 0 differing graphs on JDK 21 and on JDK 8, with 611 ending in a `SecurityException` on each side. That run used insertion-ordered maps. With the review's harness, which also builds `IdentityHashMap`s, 729 graphs differed on JDK 21. Those maps iterate by identity hash, and HotSpot assigns identity hashes from a per-thread sequence that the old code's thrown exceptions also drew from, so the maps iterated differently. Each such graph visited the same nodes and fields in another order; the 26 that differed in length all hit a security limit at a different point.
  * **Tests.** `TraverserVisitOrderTest` (+1): a field that really holds the `String` `"<inaccessible>"` must still be read, reported and walked, with `collectFields` both ways, since a sentinel now tells "not read" apart from it. It passes on the previous code. Found by the review of `Traverser` (its TR-2). Full suite: 21,293 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`Traverser` walks application classes under a custom system class loader.** `Traverser` treats JDK classes as leaves: it visits them but does not walk their fields. It recognised them by their class loader, taking the JDK's own loader to be the system loader's parent. Under a custom system class loader (`-Djava.system.class.loader`, as some agents and launchers install), that parent is the application loader, so every application class counted as a JDK class: traversing `A → B → C` visited only `A`. The JDK's loader is now `ClassLoader.getPlatformClassLoader()` on JDK 9+ (called reflectively), and on JDK 8 the topmost loader above the system loader (the extension loader). In a JVM started with a pass-through custom system loader, the walk visits `[A, B, C]` instead of `[A]` on JDK 21 and on JDK 8. Computed once, when the class initializes.
  * **Tests.** `TraverserBehaviorTest` (1): the loader chosen under a pass-through custom system loader must be the JDK's platform loader (the extension loader on JDK 8). Fails on the previous logic. Found by the review of `Traverser` (its TR-3). Full suite: 21,294 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`Traverser` gives a user subclass of a JDK class the same graph on every JDK: it walks the fields the user's classes declare, not those of the JDK superclass.** JDK classes are leaves because their fields are unreadable from JDK 17 on. Their fields inherited by a user subclass were still walked, which only reached anything on JDK 8-16. A user exception gave 5 nodes on JDK 8 (its stack trace, the suppressed-exceptions list) and 2 on JDK 21. A user `Thread` subclass gave 6 nodes on JDK 8, walking into the application class loader, and 1 on JDK 21. A field is now walked only if it is non-primitive and declared by a non-JDK class; `getFields()` still reports every field. Both now give 2 and 1 nodes on JDK 8 too. Which fields are walked is worked out once per class per traversal instead of fetching the field list for every node. Per node on par or slightly faster: with `collectFields=true` 50 → 48 ns on JDK 21, 53 → 46 on JDK 8; a 200,000-node chain 9 → 9 ms, 10 → 8 on JDK 8. The 3,000-graph differential, whose classes extend no JDK class, gives 0 differing graphs on both JDKs.
  * **Tests.** `TraverserBehaviorTest` (+1): a user exception must be walked to its own field only, with `collectFields` both ways. Fails on the previous code on JDK 8. Found by the review of `Traverser` (its TR-6). Full suite: 21,295 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`Traverser` walks the fields a user subclass of a `Collection` or `Map` declares, besides its elements.** For a collection or map, only the elements (or keys and values) were walked. An object held in a field of a `TaggedList extends ArrayList` or a `TaggedMap extends HashMap` was never reached, so `GraphComparator`'s orphan detection could not see it either. Such fields are now walked too. As for any other node, only fields a non-JDK class declares are walked, so `ArrayList`'s own fields are not. Per node on par (an `ArrayList` node 44 vs 45 ns on JDK 21, 40 vs 40 on JDK 8), and the 3,000-graph differential gives 0 differing graphs on both JDKs; its collections are all JDK classes.
  * **Tests.** `TraverserBehaviorTest` (+1): a user `ArrayList` subclass and a user `HashMap` subclass must be walked to the object in their field, as well as to their elements, and to nothing else, with `collectFields` both ways. Fails on the previous code. Found by the review of `Traverser` (its TR-4). Full suite: 21,296 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`Traverser` keeps going past a collection or map it cannot read.** An exception from a collection's `size()` or `iterator()`, a map's `entrySet()`, or its iteration ended the whole traversal. A lazy-loading proxy whose session is gone throws "could not initialize proxy", and closed resources and maps modified mid-walk fail similarly. A field that could not be read was already passed over. Such a collection or map is now visited but not walked further: the elements read before the failure are walked, and the failure is logged at `FINEST`. The security limits' own `SecurityException` still ends the walk, and the size is still checked before iterating. Per node on par (43 vs 43 ns on JDK 21, 41 vs 42 on JDK 8), and the 3,000-graph differential gives 0 differing graphs on both JDKs. A first version opened the iterator before checking the size limit. The differential caught that on JDK 8 in 110 graphs that hit the limit: a `LinkedHashMap`'s lazily created `entrySet` view appeared earlier.
  * **Tests.** `TraverserBehaviorTest` (+1): a collection whose `iterator()` throws and a map whose `entrySet()` throws must be visited and their sibling still reached, with `collectFields` both ways. A list whose iterator fails at its second element must still have its first element walked. Fails on the previous code. Found by the review of `Traverser` (its TR-5). Full suite: 21,297 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`Traverser` no longer writes system properties when it loads, and a security limit that is not set is off however it came to be unset.** Its static initializer set every unset `traverser.max.*` property to `"0"`, process-wide, which is how "not set = 0, disabled" (as documented) was achieved. A limit cleared after that fell back to the built-in default instead: with `traverser.security.enabled=true`, a 60,000-element list then failed the 50,000-element default collection limit. The limits are now read, never written: not set means 0 (disabled), a number is that limit, and anything else uses the built-in default, as before. The class Javadoc and the user guide now say so. The four limit readers share one method; the limits are read once per traversal, as before.
  * **Tests.** `TraverserBehaviorTest` (+1): loading `Traverser` in a fresh class loader must leave the limit properties unset, and with security on and no limit set, a 60,000-element list must be traversed. Fails on the previous code. Found by the review of `Traverser` (its TR-8). Not changed: the `"<inaccessible>"` placeholder is a plain `String`, so it cannot be told apart from a field that holds that text; changing it would change what `getFields()` callers see. Full suite: 21,298 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`TypeUtilities` no longer resolves a class's type variable to that class's own superclass or interface, which made json-io replace data with an empty object or fail.** To find what binds a type variable, `TypeUtilities` climbs the class hierarchy, and at each superclass or interface it resolved that supertype itself and returned it as the variable's value. `Holder<T> extends Base` gave `T` = `Base`, `Box<T> implements Serializable` gave `Serializable`, `Node<T> implements Comparable<Node<T>>` gave `Comparable<Node<T>>`, and a class unrelated to the variable gave its own superclass. Reading `{"value":{"name":"x","n":1}}` with json-io as a `Holder` therefore set `value` to an empty `Base`, silently dropping the data, and as a `Box` threw `Cannot instantiate interface: java.io.Serializable`. Now both read the value as a map, as for a class with no supertype. The climb now only looks for a binding: an unbound variable stays a variable (`Object` when resolved against an instance). Bindings made by a superclass or interface at any depth resolve as before. Cost: on par, as fresh resolution does less (64.4 → 61–64 ns on JDK 21; within noise on JDK 8) and cached lookups are unchanged (11.4 ns). The defect came in with a1df12c5 (2025-02-22).
  * **Tests.** `TypeUtilitiesBehaviorTest` (1): the review's cases must leave the variable unresolved, while bindings through a superclass, two levels of superclass, an instance and an interface still resolve. Fails on the previous code. Found by the review of `TypeUtilities` (its TU-1). Full suite: 21,299 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`TypeUtilities`' cache no longer keeps a webapp's or plugin's class loader alive, and a root type that is not a `Class` is resolved afresh, 2.4x faster on JDK 21.** The cache keeps each answer in a class that lives at least as long as everything the answer refers to. Two kinds of reference were not counted.
  * **java-util's own Type objects.** An answer can be one of `TypeUtilities`' own `Type` implementations. When java-util sits in a webapp's `WEB-INF/lib` or a plugin's loader and resolves a type made only of JDK classes, such as `AbstractMap<K,V>` against `HashMap`, the answer went to a JDK class that never unloads, and kept java-util's loader for good. json-io's `resolveType(type, type)` on a `Map<String,Object>` takes this path. `TypeUtilities.class` itself now counts, so such an answer stays with java-util.
  * **A caller's own Type objects.** A plugin's own `ParameterizedType` object whose arguments were all JDK classes was kept on `java.util.List`, keeping the plugin's loader. The class of each `Type` object now counts too.
  * **Roots that are not a `Class`.** These are no longer cached at all: finding where to keep the answer cost more than resolving it, 58 ns for a hit against 24 ns fresh on JDK 21 (found by the same review, its TU-P). Resolving `List<Concrete>` against `Map<String, List<Concrete>>` now takes 24.0 ns, down from 57.9, on JDK 21, and 26.7 ns, up from 24.8, on JDK 8. Repeated calls with such a root return equal answers, no longer the identical object. Hits on a `Class` root (11 ns) and fresh resolution are on par on both JDKs.
  * **Tests.** `TypeUtilitiesClassLoaderLeakTest` (extended):
    * java-util loaded in a throwaway loader resolving `AbstractMap<K,V>` against `HashMap` must let that loader be collected.
    * A plugin's `ParameterizedType`, resolved against itself and against `List`, must let the plugin's loader be collected.

    The test fails on the previous code on JDK 21 and JDK 8, and again with either half of the fix removed: without counting a `Type` object's class, the plugin case fails; without counting `TypeUtilities.class`, the java-util case fails. Found by the review of `TypeUtilities` (its TU-2 and TU-P). Full suite: 21,299 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`TypeUtilities` resolves a binding that is itself built from another type variable.** With `Mid<X> extends Parent<List<X>>` and `Child extends Mid<Integer>`, `Parent`'s `T` seen from `Child` is `List<Integer>`, but `resolveType` and `resolveTypeUsingInstance` answered `List<X>`: only a bare variable found as a binding was resolved further. Any binding that still contains a variable is now resolved in turn. Cost: on par (hits on a `Class` root 10.5 vs 10.4 ns on JDK 21; fresh resolution within noise on both JDKs).
  * **Tests.** `TypeUtilitiesBehaviorTest` (+1): `Parent`'s `T` must be `List<Integer>` from `Child`, by class and by instance. Fails on the previous code. Found by the review of `TypeUtilities` (its TU-3). Full suite: 21,300 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`TypeUtilities`' own `ParameterizedType` and `WildcardType` hash as the JDK's do, keeping the `equals`/`hashCode` contract with them.** `equals` already held both ways between `TypeUtilities`' `Map<String, Integer>` and the JDK's, but the hashes differed (-1538074747 against 1013785805), so a `HashSet` or `HashMap` holding the JDK's type did not find ours. They now use the JDK's formulas: for a parameterized type, the XOR of the hashes of its arguments array, owner and raw type; for a wildcard, of its lower- and upper-bounds arrays. Its `GenericArrayType` already matched. Cost: on par (hits 10.7 vs 10.5 ns on JDK 21, 11.7 vs 11.9 on JDK 8).
  * **Tests.** `TypeUtilitiesBehaviorTest` (+1): a resolved `Map<String, Integer>` and `? extends Integer` must equal the JDK's both ways and hash alike, and a hash set of the JDK's type must contain ours. Fails on the previous code. Found by the review of `TypeUtilities` (its TU-5). Full suite: 21,301 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`getActualTypeArguments()` on a `TypeUtilities` parameterized type hands out a copy, as the JDK's does, so one caller can no longer change the answer every later caller gets.** It returned the instance's internal array, and resolved types are shared through the cache. After one caller wrote into the array, `resolveType(ConcreteMap.class, Map<String, T>)` answered `Map<String, Thread>` for every later caller in the JVM; in the test run, the next test class got the poisoned answer too. `TypeUtilities`' own reads of type arguments (seven places) use the array directly, without a copy. Cost: each outside call copies the array, as calls on the JDK's types already do. json-io reading a bean of nested parameterized fields takes 4.74 vs 4.59 µs on JDK 21 (+3%) and 4.73 vs 4.54 on JDK 8 (+4%). `TypeUtilities`' own resolution is unchanged.
  * **Tests.** `TypeUtilitiesBehaviorTest` (+1): writing into the array returned for a cached answer must change neither the next answer nor the array that answer holds. Fails on the previous code. Found by the review of `TypeUtilities` (its TU-6). Full suite: 21,302 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`TypeUtilities` no longer caches a `Type` object of a class it does not know, which let the cache grow without bound.** A caller's own `Type` implementation can lack value equality. Built fresh for each call, such an object added one cache entry per call that was never removed: about 290 bytes a call in the review's probe, and 1,000 calls added 1,000 entries in the test. Only the JDK's `Type` classes and `TypeUtilities`' own are known to have value equality and to outlive nothing. A `Type` object of any other class is now resolved afresh and kept nowhere. This also replaces the previous entry's handling of a plugin's own `ParameterizedType`, which is now not kept at all. Cost: on par (hits 10.4 vs 10.4 ns on JDK 21, 11.9 vs 11.8 on JDK 8; fresh resolution on par or faster).
  * **Tests.** `TypeUtilitiesBehaviorTest` (+1): resolving 1,000 fresh identity-equality `ParameterizedType` objects against a class must add no cache entries. Fails on the previous code. `TypeUtilitiesClassLoaderLeakTest`'s plugin case still passes. Found by the review of `TypeUtilities` (its TU-4). Full suite: 21,303 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`TypeUtilities.inferElementType` follows a container's own type arguments to `Map`'s value type or `Collection`'s element type, and gives an array's component type, as its Javadoc says.** It took the container's type arguments by position, which only works when they line up with `Map`'s or `Collection`'s:
  * `StringMap<Integer>` (`StringMap<V> extends Map<String, V>`) gave `null`.
  * `MultiMap<String, Integer>` (`extends HashMap<K, List<V>>`) gave `Integer`, not `List<Integer>`.
  * `IdList<Long, String>` (`IdList<I, E> extends ArrayList<E>`) gave `Long`.
  * An array such as `Point[]` or `List<String>[]` gave `null`.

  These now give `Integer`, `List<Integer>`, `String`, `Point` and `List<String>`. The arguments are followed up the hierarchy by substitution only, so a variable no argument binds stays that variable (`Map<String, T>` still gives `T`, never its bound). How a container class's parameters reach `Map` or `Collection` is worked out once per class: walking the generic supertypes on each call cost 40 to 80 ns. Cost: 6.2 → 7.6 ns on JDK 21 and 2.6 → 5.0 on JDK 8 for a `Map`, `HashMap` or `ArrayList`, for the per-class lookup. json-io calls it once per field when it first plans how to write a class.
  * **Tests.** `TypeUtilitiesBehaviorTest` (+1): the review's containers must give their element types. Ordinary containers, an unresolved variable, a non-container and a raw class must answer as before. Fails on the previous code. Found by the review of `TypeUtilities` (its TU-7). Full suite: 21,304 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`UniqueIdGenerator` no longer stalls every caller for as long as the wall clock stepped back.** IDs are never issued for a time before IDs already issued. Once a millisecond's capacity was used (1,000 IDs for `getUniqueId`, 10,000 for `getUniqueId19`), the generator waited for the wall clock to pass the last issued millisecond. After the clock stepped back (an NTP correction, a VM snapshot restored), every caller therefore blocked for the size of the step: 1,100 IDs after a 3-second step took 3,002 ms, and an hour's step would block for an hour. Now, after one real millisecond (measured by `System.nanoTime()`) with the wall clock still behind, the generator moves on by one millisecond of its own. It still issues no more than one millisecond's capacity per real millisecond. Those 1,100 IDs now take a few milliseconds. Normal operation is unchanged:
  * Uncapped cost per ID is on par (50 to 53 vs 57 to 59 ns on JDK 21, about 49 on JDK 8).
  * Under saturation, IDs still come at exactly the capacity (10.0 million per second for `getUniqueId19` on one thread, 1.0 million for `getUniqueId` on eight threads), with no duplicates and strictly increasing per thread, on both JDKs.
  * **Tests.** `UniqueIdGeneratorBehaviorTest` (1): a fresh copy of the generator, in its own class loader so the rest of the JVM's IDs are not disturbed, is set as if the clock had stepped back 3 s. It must then hand out 1,100 strictly increasing IDs in under a second. Fails on the previous code (3,002 ms on JDK 21, 3,001 on JDK 8). Found by the review of `UniqueIdGenerator` (its UID-1). Full suite: 21,305 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`UniqueIdGenerator` reads a hostname ending in `-digits` as a Kubernetes pod ordinal only inside Kubernetes.** The server ID (the IDs' last two digits) came from `HOSTNAME`'s last dash-separated number on any host. EC2 hosts `ip-10-0-1-23`, `ip-10-0-2-123` and `ip-172-31-40-223` all got server ID 23, and `db-prod-01` and `app-prod-101` both got 1, so a fleet's hosts shared server IDs and could issue the same IDs. The ordinal rule now applies only when `KUBERNETES_SERVICE_HOST` is set, as it is in every Kubernetes pod. Elsewhere the hostname is hashed, as the existing later step already did for other hostnames. The class Javadoc now says so, and also describes the previous entry's behavior after the clock steps back. Only the startup choice of server ID changes.
  * **Tests.** `UniqueIdGeneratorBehaviorTest` (+1): outside Kubernetes, those hostnames must get their hash-based server ID; inside, a pod's ordinal (modulo 100). Each case runs a fresh copy of the generator. The test needs an environment that does not set these variables itself; it is skipped otherwise. Fails on the previous code. Found by the review of `UniqueIdGenerator` (its UID-2). Full suite: 21,306 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **DOCUMENTATION**: **`UniqueIdGenerator`'s Javadoc and the user guide no longer call its IDs "guaranteed unique" without qualification.** They are unique across processes only if each process has its own server ID. Two JVMs on one host with the same environment derive the same server ID and issue nearly the same IDs: 498,622 of 500,000 were identical in the review's probe. The docs now say so and advise setting `JAVA_UTIL_CLUSTERID` per process when several run on one host. Found by the review of `UniqueIdGenerator` (its UID-3). Full suite: 21,306 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`Converter`'s `Double`/`Float` → `BigInteger` works under any default locale, and is 3x to 6x faster.** It printed the value's whole part with `String.format("%.0f")` and parsed the text back. That uses the default locale's digits, so under `ar-SA` on JDK 9+ it printed `١٢٣٤٥` and the parse threw `NumberFormatException: illegal syntax`. It now takes `BigDecimal.valueOf(d).toBigInteger()`. That is the same whole part of the value as Java prints it, which is what `%.0f` printed (Java's `%f` pads the shortest digits with zeros, so `1e300` stays exactly 10^300), and involves no locale. 12345.678 → `BigInteger`: 221 → 36 ns on JDK 21, 240 → 80 on JDK 8. 1.23e30: 369 → 78 ns, 612 → 284.
  * **Tests.** `ConverterDoubleToBigIntegerTest` (2): under `ar-SA`, a `double` and a `float` must convert. 200,000 random doubles across bit patterns, magnitudes and fractions, plus the edge values (±0, `Double.MAX_VALUE`, `Long.MAX_VALUE`, 1e22, 1e23), must give exactly what the previous algorithm gives with the locale pinned. The locale case fails on the previous code on JDK 21; JDK 8's locale data uses ASCII digits there. Found by the review of the `convert` package (its H5 and P3). Full suite: 21,308 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`Converter` keeps a `Timestamp`'s nanoseconds in `Timestamp` → `Timestamp`, → `ZonedDateTime`, and the conversions that go through `ZonedDateTime` (such as → `LocalTime`).** These were registered with `java.util.Date`'s conversions, which read `getTime()` (milliseconds): `2024-01-15T10:20:30.123456789Z` came back as `.123`. Conversions to `Instant`, `LocalDateTime`, `OffsetDateTime`, `String` and `BigInteger` already kept `.123456789`. `Timestamp` now has its own: a copy with `setNanos`, and `toInstant().atZone(zone)`. Cost: on par (`Timestamp` → `ZonedDateTime` 19.9 vs 19.0 ns on JDK 21, a median of 95.6 vs 97.9 on JDK 8; → `Timestamp` +0.6 ns on JDK 8 for the nanoseconds).
  * **Tests.** `ConverterTimestampNanosTest` (1): a `Timestamp` with nanoseconds must keep them through `Timestamp`, `ZonedDateTime`, `LocalTime`, `Instant`, `LocalDateTime` and `OffsetDateTime`, and round-trip through `ZonedDateTime`; the `Timestamp` result must be a copy. Fails on the previous code. Found by the review of the `convert` package (its H1). Full suite: 21,309 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **With `modern.time.long.precision = nanos`, `ZonedDateTime`, `OffsetDateTime`, `LocalDateTime` and `LocalDate` convert to `long` in nanoseconds, as they already did from `long`, and an instant too late for a `long` of nanoseconds throws instead of wrapping.**
  * **The setting was ignored in one direction.** These types converted to `long` in milliseconds whatever the setting (`LocalDateTime` and `LocalDate` go through `ZonedDateTime`). Converting back from `long` honored the setting, so `ZonedDateTime` → `long` → `ZonedDateTime` landed in 1969 and `LocalDate` → `long` → `LocalDate` gave `1969-12-31`. They now convert as `Instant` does.
  * **Overflow wrapped silently.** In nanoseconds mode, an `Instant` after 2262-04-11 wrapped: 2300-01-01 became a long that converts back to 1715. It now throws `ArithmeticException`.

  Cost: `ZonedDateTime`, `OffsetDateTime` and `LocalDateTime` → `long` now look the setting up on each call, as `Instant` → `long` always did: about +5 ns on JDK 21 (10 → 15), mixed on JDK 8 (`OffsetDateTime` +4.6 ns, `ZonedDateTime` faster). `Instant` → `long` is on par. Reading the setting once per `Converter` would remove that cost; it is listed under the open items, because it would change when a newly set system property takes effect.
  * **Tests.** `ConverterLongPrecisionTest` (2): in nanoseconds mode each type must convert to the instant's nanoseconds and round-trip, and an instant in 2300 must throw for `Instant` and `ZonedDateTime`. Both fail on the previous code. Found by the review of the `convert` package (its H7). Full suite: 21,311 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **A digit-only `String` converts to `MonthDay` and `YearMonth` as the same number does, so these values survive a round trip through `int` and `String`.**
  * **MonthDay.** `"1225"` gave `--12-05`, because the `MM.DD` pattern's separator was `.`, which matches any character, so it read 12, 2, 5. `"1231"` gave `--12-01`. The `Integer` 1225 gives `--12-25`, so `MonthDay` → `int` → `String` → `MonthDay` failed.
  * **YearMonth.** `"202401"` gave `1969-12`, because the date parser read the digits as epoch milliseconds. The `Integer` 202401 gives `2024-01`.

  Now a 3- or 4-digit string reads as `MMDD`, and a 5- or 6-digit one as `YYYYMM`, as the numbers do. The pattern's separator is literal (`-`, `/`, `.` or a space), so `"12/25"`, `"12-25"`, `"12.25"` and `"1 5"` read as before. Shorter digit strings and every other form take the same path as before. Digit strings are now about 30x faster, since they no longer go through a failed parse (`"1225"` 329 → 10.6 ns on JDK 21, 353 → 13.2 on JDK 8). Other forms are on par. Not changed, and open: a digit string converts to `LocalTime` and `Duration` by a different rule than the same `long` does (`"1500"` → `Duration` is 25 minutes, the `long` 1500 is 1.5 s).
  * **Tests.** `ConverterDigitStringTest` (2): the review's digit strings must convert as the numbers do and round-trip through `int` and `String`; the separated and ISO forms must read as before. Both fail on the previous code. Found by the review of the `convert` package (its H2). Full suite: 21,313 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **Converting an object with no conversion of its own to a `Map` keeps its values, keeps objects reached twice, ends cycles instead of overflowing the stack, and lets a subclass field hide its superclass's.** The fallback that turns an object's fields into a `Map` had four faults:
  * **Values were walked as objects.** Fields of type `BigDecimal`, `Date`, `LocalDate`, `UUID` or an enum became `{}` on JDK 17+, where their fields cannot be read. On JDK 8 they became internals (`salary={scale=2}`, `id={mostSigBits=…}`).
  * **An object reached twice became null the second time.** It was null in the second place, not only where it would have closed a cycle: a person whose `home` and `work` were one `Address` got `work=null`.
  * **A cycle through a collection overflowed the stack.** Each element was converted afresh, with no record of its ancestors.
  * **A superclass field overwrote a subclass field of the same name.** In addition, a container inside a container (a `List` of `List`s) was walked as an object.

  The fields are now walked depth first, without recursion, so a 100,000-deep chain converts:
  * A value stays a value. That covers a primitive or wrapper, `String`, an enum, a type the `Converter` treats as simple, and a JDK class that is not a container.
  * An object, `Collection`, `Map` or array becomes a `Map` or `List`.
  * An object reached again is the same `Map` in both places, and one reached from inside itself is `null`.
  * Of two fields with one name, the subclass's is kept, as Java resolves it.

  Which fields a class maps is worked out once per class. Cost: an object with value fields is much faster, since walking their internals threw for every unreadable field: 4,122 → 256 ns on JDK 21, 483 → 226 on JDK 8. A plain object is on par or faster (163 vs 176 ns on JDK 21, 199 vs 201 on JDK 8).
  * **Tests.** `ConverterObjectToMapTest` (6): value fields must stay values; an object shared by two fields must be the same `Map` in both; a cycle through a `List` must end in `null`; a subclass field must win. Containers inside containers must convert element by element, and a 100,000-deep chain must convert. The first five fail on the previous code. Found by the review of the `convert` package (its H4). Full suite: 21,319 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **PERFORMANCE**: **`Converter`'s `Timestamp` → `String` is 2.3x faster on JDK 21 and 2.5x on JDK 8.** It built a `DateTimeFormatter` from its pattern on every call, which cost more than the formatting itself. The two formatters (3 decimals for whole milliseconds, 9 otherwise) are now built once. Their fields are all numeric, so the default locale does not change the text. Per call: 228 → 100 ns on JDK 21, 435 → 173 on JDK 8; with nanoseconds, 234 → 110 and 386 → 165.
  * **Tests.** `ConverterTimestampToStringTest` (1): 20,000 random timestamps, half with nanoseconds, under four default locales (including th-TH and ar-SA), must give exactly the text a formatter built per call gives. It passes on the previous code. A mutant that swaps the two formatters fails it. Found by the review of the `convert` package (its P2). Full suite: 21,320 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **SECURITY**: **A short `String` with a huge or tiny exponent no longer holds a thread for seconds to minutes when converted to `Byte`, `Short`, `Integer` or `Long`.** Text that is not an integer literal is parsed as a `BigDecimal` and truncated to a whole number before the range check. Truncating builds the whole number: `"1e9999999"` → `Integer` took 1.5 s on JDK 21 (1.8 s on JDK 8), and each digit added to the exponent multiplies that by about ten. So an 11-character string such as `"1e999999999"` held a thread for minutes, and so did tiny values such as `"1e-999999999"`. Any caller that converts untrusted text to an integer type, such as json-io reading a string into an `int` field, was exposed. Out of range, or below 1 in magnitude (which truncates to 0), is now settled from the number's digit count before truncating. Answers are unchanged: out of range still throws `IllegalArgumentException`, and below 1 is still 0. Ordinary decimal and integer strings are on par (`"12345.0"` → `Integer` 282 vs 281 ns on JDK 21). Found while writing the test for the next item: its random strings included such exponents and the run did not finish.
  * **Tests.** `ConverterHugeExponentTest` (1): huge and tiny exponents (10 million, then a billion) must convert to each of the four types within 500 ms (out of range, or 0), and ordinary values must convert as before. Fails on the previous code (11.4 s for the first eight conversions). Full suite: 21,321 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **PERFORMANCE**: **`Converter`'s `String` → `Byte`, `Short`, `Integer` and `Long` read decimal text such as `"12345.0"` 10x to 14x faster.** Each tried `valueOf()` first and, when that threw, read the text as a decimal. Building that exception was nearly the whole cost: `"12345.0"` → `Integer` took 284 ns on JDK 21 and 275 on JDK 8. An integer literal is now read in one pass, exactly as `Long.parseLong` reads it (an optional sign, then decimal digits, Unicode digits included, within the type's range). Any other text goes straight to the decimal path, so no exception is built. That gives 20.4 ns on JDK 21 and 26.2 on JDK 8. A plain integer string is within a nanosecond (`"12345"` → `Integer` 8.7 → 9.5 ns on JDK 21, 11.1 → 11.5 on JDK 8). Answers are unchanged; when a conversion fails, the `IllegalArgumentException` no longer carries the `NumberFormatException` from `valueOf()` as its cause.
  * **Tests.** `ConverterStringToIntegerTest` (1), for all four types: fixed cases (signs, range edges, 20-digit and longer numbers, exponents, Unicode digits, blanks, junk) and 100,000 random strings of digits, points, exponents and signs. Each must give exactly the value or failure of the previous algorithm, reimplemented in the test. It passes on the previous code. A mutant without the overflow guard fails it (`"99999999999999999999"` became 7,766,279,631,452,241,919). Found by the review of the `convert` package (its P1). Full suite: 21,322 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **SECURITY**: **A value below 1 in magnitude with a tiny exponent converts to `BigInteger`, `UUID` and `BitSet` at once.** These conversions take the whole part through `BigDecimal.toBigInteger()`, which divides by 10 to the power of the scale. A `String` or `BigDecimal` such as `1e-9999999` took 1.5 to 2 s to produce 0, on JDK 21 and on JDK 8, ten times longer for each digit added to the exponent. So a short string, or a number parsed from untrusted input, could hold a thread for minutes. Values below 1 in magnitude are now recognized from their digit count and give 0 at once. Others are unchanged: on par on JDK 21 (`BigDecimal` → `BigInteger` 8.1 vs 8.0 ns, `String` +1 ns), faster on JDK 8 (19.2 → 10.5, 32.4 → 19.9). Not changed, and open: huge exponents (`1e999999999`) to `BigInteger`, or a `BigDecimal` to its plain `String`, still build the requested number in full.
  * **Tests.** `ConverterTinyExponentTest` (1): `1e-9999999` and `-1e-999999999`, as `String` and `BigDecimal`, must convert to `BigInteger` 0, the zero `UUID` and an empty `BitSet` within 500 ms; ordinary values must convert as before. Fails on the previous code (5.9 s). Full suite: 21,323 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`Double` → `Duration`, `LocalTime` and `OffsetTime` round to the nearest nanosecond, as `Double` → `Instant` already did, so a millisecond value survives a round trip through `double`.** They truncated the fraction. 1.001 is slightly below 1.001 as a `double`, so `Duration.ofMillis(1001)` → `double` → `Duration` came back as `PT1.000999999S`, and about half of all millisecond values came back a nanosecond short (6.1 of 12.3 million in the review's probe). A value within half a nanosecond of midnight becomes the day's last nanosecond (`LocalTime.MAX`), not an invalid 24:00. Cost: +0.2 to +0.9 ns for the rounding (`Double` → `Duration` 6.8 → 7.0 ns on JDK 21).
  * **Tests.** `ConverterDoubleToTimeTest` (1): every 7th millisecond value up to 200 seconds must survive `Duration` → `double` → `Duration` and `LocalTime` → `double` → `LocalTime`; 1.001 must give 1.001 s for all three types; just under midnight must give `LocalTime.MAX`. Fails on the previous code. Found by the review of the `convert` package (its M1). Full suite: 21,324 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`String` → `Locale` reads `"en_US"`, the form `Locale.toString()` prints.** It gave the undetermined locale (an empty `Locale`) without complaint, because `Locale.forLanguageTag` reads only `-` between the parts and answers `und` for anything it cannot read. `_` between the parts is now read as `-`, so `"en_US"`, `"fr_FR"` and `"zh_Hant_TW"` read, `Locale` → `toString()` → `Locale` round-trips, and `"en-US"` reads as before. Cost: one scan of the string for `_`.
  * **Tests.** `ConverterLocaleTest` (1): underscore- and hyphen-separated tags must give their locales, and four locales must round-trip through both `toString()` and the `Converter`'s own `String`. Fails on the previous code. Found by the review of the `convert` package (its M4). Full suite: 21,325 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`String` → `TimeZone` reads an offset such as `"+05:00"`, `"-03:30"` or `"Z"`.** It gave GMT (offset 0) without complaint, because `TimeZone.getTimeZone` does not read a bare offset and answers GMT for any ID it does not know. When `getTimeZone` falls back to GMT for something other than `"GMT"`, the string is now read with `ZoneId.of`, so `"+05:00"` gives `GMT+05:00`. An ID neither `TimeZone` nor `ZoneId` knows still gives GMT, as `getTimeZone`'s Javadoc says and `ConverterEverythingTest` pins; whether it should throw instead, as `ZoneId` does, is left open. Zone IDs `TimeZone` knows cost one more `String` comparison.
  * **Tests.** `ConverterTimeZoneTest` (1): `"+05:00"`, `"-03:30"` and `"Z"` must give their offsets; region IDs, `GMT`, `UTC`, `GMT+5` and `EST` must read as before, and a misspelled ID must still give GMT. Fails on the previous code. Found by the review of the `convert` package (its M5). Full suite: 21,326 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **Text that states an offset and then names its zone is read at that offset: a `ZonedDateTime` or `Calendar` converted to `String` and back keeps its instant in the hour a fall-back repeats.** `"2025-11-02T01:30:00-05:00[America/New_York]"` is the form `ZonedDateTime.toString()` and the `Converter` write. `DateUtilities` dropped the `-05:00` and read 01:30 by New York's rule alone, which answers the earlier of the two 01:30s (−04:00): an hour off. The `Converter`'s own round trip lost the hour for every zone that keeps daylight time — 1,644 of 8,642 instants tried, for `ZonedDateTime` and for `Calendar` alike, and so for anything stored as that text. The abbreviation rule above added four more: `CET`, `EET`, `MET` and `WET` are zone IDs that keep summer time as well as abbreviations, so `"2026-07-01T12:00:00+02:00[CET]"`, which `ZonedDateTime.toString()` writes, read as the abbreviation `CET` (+01:00). The stated offset now decides whenever the zone has that offset at that local time. When it does not, the time is read as before: an offset truncated to the minute still yields to the zone's local mean time (−04:56:02 in 1850 New York), an abbreviation still means its own offset, and text whose offset contradicts its zone (`"12:00+05:00[Europe/Berlin]"`) still reads by the zone. Cost: on par on JDK 21; on JDK 8, +16 ns (2%) on such text and +130 ns in the repeated hour itself.
  * **Tests.** `DateUtilitiesStatedOffsetTest` (4): both 01:30s of New York's fall-back and Berlin's must read at their stated offsets; `[CET]`, `[EET]`, `[MET]` and `[WET]` in July must read at their summer offsets; `EST` with and without `-05:00`, and the 1850 local mean time, must read as before; and every zone, around each of its 2025–2026 transitions and in mid-January and mid-July, must convert `ZonedDateTime` and `Calendar` to `String` and back to the same instant. Three fail on the previous code (3,343 wrong round trips); two mutants (always taking the stated offset, and taking it without checking it against the zone) each fail the fourth. Full suite: 21,330 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **A `Calendar` in GMT converts to a `String` (and a `Map`) in GMT.** `Calendar` → `String` rewrote a zone ID without a `/` as the region the abbreviation table names, so that the text reads back to the same instant, and the table names `Europe/London` for `GMT`. London keeps summer time, so noon GMT on July 1 was written `13:00:00+01:00[Europe/London]`; read back, the `Calendar` was in London and its hour of day was 13. Reading text, `DateUtilities` takes `GMT` as GMT, not London, so `GMT` is now written as itself: `2026-07-01T12:00:00Z[GMT]`. The other rewrites stay (`CET` → `Europe/Berlin`, `EET` → `Europe/Kyiv`, `WET` → `Europe/Lisbon`), because the text is read back as those regions and they once kept other offsets (EET and Kyiv in 1960). `ConverterTest.testString_fromCalendar`, which pinned `[Europe/London]` for a January GMT `Calendar`, now expects `[GMT]`. Cost: one `String` comparison.
  * **Tests.** `ConverterCalendarZoneTest` (2): a July GMT `Calendar` must write `[GMT]` and read back at hour 12 with offset 0; eight zones, in January and July of 1960 and 2026, must convert `Calendar` → `String` → `Calendar` to the same instant. The first fails on the previous code; dropping every rewrite fails the second (EET in 1960). Found by the review of the `convert` package (its M3). Full suite: 21,332 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **An enum converted to a `Map` converts back to that enum.** Enum → `Map` gives `{"name": "SECONDS"}`, but `Map` → enum read every `Map` as the names of an `EnumSet`'s members, so the way back failed with `No enum constant java.util.concurrent.TimeUnit.name`. A `Map` whose one entry is `"name"` with a `String` value now gives that constant; any other `Map` still gives the `EnumSet` its keys name. Cost: a size check on `Map` → enum.
  * **Tests.** `ConverterEnumMapTest` (2): every `TimeUnit` must round-trip through its `Map`, and `{"name": "DAYS"}` must give `DAYS`; a two-key `Map`, and `{"HOURS": "name"}`, must still give their `EnumSet`s. The first fails on the previous code; a mutant that ignores the key fails the second. Found by the review of the `convert` package (its M6). Full suite: 21,334 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`String` → `Color` reads color names under a Turkish default locale.** The name was lowered with the JVM's default locale, and Turkish lowers `I` to a dotless `ı`: `"WHITE"` became `"whıte"`, which no name matched, so `"WHITE"`, `"PINK"` and `"LIGHT_GRAY"` failed with `Unable to parse color from string`. It is now lowered in `Locale.ROOT`, as `DateUtilities` and `IOUtilities` already do.
  * **Tests.** `ConverterColorLocaleTest` (1): under `tr-TR`, `"WHITE"`, `"PINK"`, `"LIGHT_GRAY"`, `"RED"` and `"RGB(10, 20, 30)"` must give their colors; the default locale is restored afterwards. Fails on the previous code. Found by the review of the `convert` package (its M9). Full suite: 21,335 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **SECURITY**: **`ReflectionUtils`' sensitive-field check and `UrlUtilities`' cookie-domain check hold under a Turkish default locale.** Both lowered names with the JVM's default locale, and Turkish lowers `I` to a dotless `ı`.
  * **`ReflectionUtils`.** With sensitive-field validation on, a field named `API_KEY` lowered to `"apı_key"`, which the pattern `api_key` does not match, so under `tr-TR` the check let `API_KEY`, `PRIVATE_KEY` and `DB_CREDENTIAL` through. A pattern set by property with an `I` (`PIN`) was lowered to `"pın"` and matched nothing.
  * **`UrlUtilities`.** Cookie attribute names were lowered the same way: `DOMAIN=` became `"domaın"`, which is not `"domain"`. With strict cookie domains on, the domain check was skipped and a cookie for another site kept its domain, stored under the mangled name. A domain such as `WIKI.EXAMPLE.COM` lowered to `"wıkı.example.com"` and was rejected for its own host.
  * All of these, and the reserved-prefix check on cookie names, now lower in `Locale.ROOT`, as `DateUtilities` and `IOUtilities` already do. Found by the scan that followed the `String` → `Color` fix.
  * **Tests.** `ReflectionUtilsTurkishLocaleTest` (1): under `tr-TR`, `API_KEY`, `PRIVATE_KEY`, `DB_CREDENTIAL`, and `userPin` with the pattern `PIN`, must be blocked. `UrlUtilitiesCookieLocaleTest` (1): under `tr-TR`, a cookie's `DOMAIN` and `PATH` must be stored as `domain` and `path`; no cookie may keep the domain `evil.com` from `wiki.example.com`; and `Domain=WIKI.EXAMPLE.COM` must be kept. Each fails on the previous code; the default locale and the properties are restored afterwards, and no connection is opened. Full suite: 21,337 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **SECURITY**: **A cookie that `UrlUtilities`' strict domain check rejects is no longer stored.** `getCookies` put a cookie in the store as soon as it had read its name and value, and checked its attributes afterwards. When the domain check rejected it — `Set-Cookie: hijack=2; domain=evil.com` from `www.example.com` — it logged `Rejecting dangerous cookie`, but the cookie was already stored and `setCookies` sent it back on the next request. RFC 6265 (section 5.3) has a user agent ignore such a cookie entirely. It is now stored only once all its attributes pass, which also means another thread can no longer see a cookie whose attributes are still being read. With the strict check off (the default), nothing changes.
  * **Tests.** `UrlUtilitiesCookieRejectTest` (2): with the strict check on, a cookie for `evil.com` from `www.example.com` must not be stored while its neighbor for `example.com` is; with it off, the cookie must be stored with its domain, as before. The first fails on the previous code. No connection is opened. Full suite: 21,339 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`TestUtil`'s case-insensitive search and `CompactMap`'s reading of a map type's name no longer depend on the default locale.** Both lowered with it, and Turkish lowers `I` to a dotless `ı`. Under `tr-TR`, `TestUtil.checkContainsIgnoreCase("Invalid ID: 42", "invalid", "id")` answered `false` (and `assertContainsIgnoreCase` failed), because `"Invalid ID"` lowered to `"ınvalıd ıd"`. A `CompactMap` built with a sorted map type named `...DESCENDING...` came out ascending instead of reversed. Both now lower in `Locale.ROOT`, as do `CompactMap`'s legacy comparator-name check and its template-class hash, which were unaffected only by luck. With this, no `toLowerCase()` or `toUpperCase()` without a locale remains in java-util's main code.
  * **Tests.** `LocaleIndependentCaseTest` (2): under `tr-TR`, both `TestUtil` searches must find `invalid` and `id` in `"Invalid ID: 42"`, and a `CompactMap` over a `TreeMap` subclass named `DESCENDINGTreeMap` must iterate `c, b, a`. A compact size no other test uses makes `CompactMap` generate the template class under the Turkish locale. Each fails on the previous code. Full suite: 21,341 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **IMPROVED**: **An unsupported conversion's message shows the first 100 characters of the source, not all of it.** The message embedded the source's whole `toString()`: converting a list of 20,000 numbers to `UUID` gave a 128,959-character message, written into every log that records the exception, along with whatever data the source held. The value is now cut to 100 characters followed by `...`, never splitting a character outside the BMP; a shorter value reads exactly as before.
  * **Tests.** `ConverterUnsupportedMessageTest` (3): the 20,000-number list must give a message under 200 characters that starts with its first elements and ends `...)] target type 'UUID'`; an emoji straddling the cut must not be split (a mutant cutting at exactly 100 fails); a one-element list must read as before. The first fails on the previous code. Found by the review of the `convert` package (a LOW item). Full suite: 21,344 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **DOCS**: **`computeIfAbsent`'s comment in the null-safe concurrent maps no longer promises the function runs at most once.** That holds for `ConcurrentHashMapNullSafe`, whose `ConcurrentHashMap.compute()` is atomic. `ConcurrentNavigableMapNullSafe` is backed by `ConcurrentSkipListMap`, whose `compute()` may apply the function more than once when another write races, as its Javadoc says. Measured under contention: 25,084 calls for 20,000 absent keys. The comment now says so. Comment only; found by the review of the concurrent collections (its L3). Full suite: 21,344 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **STYLE**: **Lines added for 4.113.0 are wrapped at 120 characters**: 152 lines in 53 files (45 in main code), formatting only. `javap -c -p` output is identical for all 1,809 main and test classes before and after. Full suite: 21,344 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,740 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BEHAVIOR CHANGE**: **`String` → `byte[]` (and `ByteBuffer`) gives the String's bytes in the Converter's charset again; nothing is guessed from what the text looks like.** Since 4.103.0 it decoded text that looked like a list of numbers (`"[1, 2, 3]"`), spaced or unspaced hex (`"de ad be ef"`, `"deadbeef"`) or Base64 (16+ letters and digits, or padded). That broke round trips both ways:
  * **Text read as binary:** the bytes of `"deadbeef"`, `"12345678"`, `"ThisIsAPlainText"`, `"AccountNumber123"`, `"[1, 2, 3]"` or `"ab cd ef"` did not survive `byte[]` → `String` → `byte[]`.
  * **Base64 read as text:** the Base64 of 112 of 2,050 random arrays of 0–40 bytes came back different. The Base64 of 3 bytes is 4 letters with no padding (`"lAfp"`), which was taken for text; Base64 made only of hex digits was taken for hex.
  * **Now:** `String` → `byte[]` is the reverse of `byte[]` → `String`. Base64 is read from the Map form, `{"value": "<base64>"}` (or `_v`), as json-io writes a `byte[]`: 0 of the 2,050 went wrong there. In that form, a value that is not Base64 gives its own bytes rather than a guessed format. To read hex, call `ByteUtilities.decode`; to read Base64, call `java.util.Base64`. json-io 4.113.0 reads a JSON string into a `byte[]` as Base64 itself, as Jackson does, so it no longer relies on the guess. Cost: none; the regular-expression checks are gone.
  * **Tests.** `ConverterStringToByteArrayTest` (3): 13 texts that looked like hex, Base64 or number lists must survive `byte[]` → `String` → `byte[]` and `String` → `ByteBuffer`; the Converter's charset must decide the bytes (ISO-8859-1 `"é"` is one byte); the Map form must read Base64 and give a non-Base64 value its bytes. The first and third fail on the previous code. `ConverterEverythingTest`'s `String` → `byte[]`/`ByteBuffer` and `Map` → `byte[]`/`ByteBuffer` entries now expect the text's bytes (18 of the 25 `String` → `byte[]` inputs kept; the other seven repeated a shape). Found by the review of the `convert` package (its H3). Full suite: 21,333 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,746 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BEHAVIOR CHANGE**: **A `BitSet` converts to and from its binary text, the integer types it fits, `BigInteger` and `BigDecimal`, `Boolean` as one bit, and its byte, index and boolean arrays — and nothing else.** Every other conversion went through its binary text, read back as something else: `BitSet` {0, 2} (`"101"`) gave `101.0` as a `Double`, `PT1M41S` as a `Duration`, 101 ms after 1970 as a `Date`, `'e'` as a `Character`, and a file named `101`. The other way, any character but `1` counted as 0: `"hello"`, `5.0` and `Year` 2024 gave `{}`, `"102"` gave {2}, and `Date(0)` gave {14, 17, 23}. Now:
  * **Binary text** (`String`, `StringBuilder`, `StringBuffer`, `CharSequence`, `CharBuffer`, `char[]`, `Character[]`): only `0` and `1`, after trimming; `""` is the empty set. Any other character is an `IllegalArgumentException` naming it and its index (`Unable to convert '10x1' to BitSet: only 0 and 1 are allowed, found 'x' at index 2`), with the input cut at 100 characters.
  * **Integer types** (`long`, `int`, `short`, `byte`, their wrappers, `AtomicLong`, `AtomicInteger`, `LongAdder`): a two's-complement pattern of the type's width, as before, so `-1L` ↔ 64 set bits. A set bit beyond the width was dropped without a word (bit 40 into an `int`); it is now an `IllegalArgumentException`.
  * **`BigInteger`, `BigDecimal`**: a non-negative whole number of any size, as before. A `BigDecimal` with a fraction (5.5) became 5; it is now an `IllegalArgumentException`, and the message shows `1E-9999999` rather than ten million digits.
  * **`Boolean`, `AtomicBoolean`**: one bit. `{}` is `false` and `{0}` is `true`; any other set bit was `true`, and is now an `IllegalArgumentException`.
  * **`ByteBuffer`** now holds the same bytes as `byte[]` ({0, 2} is {5}; it held the 3 text bytes of `"101"`). **`Map`** is `{"value": "101"}`, which `Map` → `BitSet` reads back (it gave an empty `Map`). `null` → `BitSet` is `null` (it threw a `NullPointerException`).
  * **Unsupported:** floating-point types, every date and time type, `Duration`, `Year`, `Character`, `File`, `Path`, `URI`, `Pattern`, `Locale` and `TimeZone`, both ways. The `BitSet` ↔ `String` surrogate pairs that produced them are gone.
  * **Tests.** `ConverterBitSetTest` (8): of every class the Converter knows, exactly these partners must convert each way; the text, width, whole-number, one-bit, `ByteBuffer`, `Map` and `null` rules; the removed types must throw. All but the `null`/`Map` test fail on the previous code. `ConverterEverythingTest`'s `BitSet` ↔ `double`/`float` entries are gone and its `Boolean` entries expect one bit; `ConverterTinyExponentTest` now expects a tiny `BigDecimal` → `BitSet` to be refused at once. Found by the review of the `convert` package (its H6). Full suite: 21,331 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,746 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`Map` → `BitSet` reads the indices of the set bits, `{"value": [0, 2]}`, as older json-io wrote a `BitSet`.** That form failed with `Unsupported conversion, source type [Object[]]` (json-io's `BitSetFactory`, which knew it, was never reached), so such JSON could not be read. A JSON array of indices (`Object[]` or a `Collection`) is now read as the set bits; `{"value": "101"}`, the binary string, reads as before. An index must be a whole number from 0 to 2^24 − 1, else an `IllegalArgumentException`: a `BitSet`'s memory follows its highest bit, so without a cap twelve characters of input (`[2000000000]`) could take 250 MB. A larger `BitSet` comes as its binary string, whose size follows the input's.
  * **Tests.** `ConverterBitSetTest.theMapFormTakesBitIndices`: `[0, 2]` as `Object[]` and as a `List`, an empty list, and index 2^24 − 1 must read; −1, 2^24, 2,000,000,000, 2.5, `"2"`, `null` and `true` must each be refused, naming the index. Fails on the previous code. Full suite: 21,332 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,749 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BEHAVIOR CHANGE**: **A number converts to a narrower type only if it fits; otherwise an `IllegalArgumentException` names the value and the range, whatever the source.** A `String` source was already refused that way. Every other source failed silently, each in its own way:
  * **Wrapping:** `Long` and `BigInteger` wrapped like a cast. `4294967297L` → `int` gave 1, `BigInteger` 2^64+5 → `long` gave 5, `BigDecimal` 1e20 → `int` gave 1661992960.
  * **Clamping:** `double` clamped, then wrapped. 1e10 → `int` gave 2147483647, and → `short` gave −1.
  * **NaN:** gave 0, the epoch, `00:00`, or an enum's first constant.
  * **Enum by ordinal:** wrapped into range. `4294967297L` gave `GREEN`, alone and inside a collection.
  * **Dates:** wrapped or clamped. `BigInteger` 2^64+1000 ms → `Date` gave 1970-01-01T00:00:01; 1e300 seconds → `Date` gave the year 292278994. `"99999999999999999999"` → `Duration` wrapped.
  * **char and calendar fields:** `'é'` → `byte` gave −23; `(byte) −1` → `char` gave `'￿'`; `YearMonth` 2024-01 → `short` gave 5793, as does every `YearMonth` past the year 327.
  * **Floating overflow:** a finite value too large for `float` or `double` became infinite (`1e300` → `float`, `BigDecimal` 1e400 → `double`, `"1e300"` → `float`).

  Now one rule applies to every source. A fraction is still truncated toward zero (`3.7` → 3, `−3.7` → −3). A value beyond the target's range, `NaN` or infinity is an `IllegalArgumentException`, such as `Unable to convert 4294967297 to int: it is outside -2147483648 to 2147483647`. That covers `int`, `long`, `short`, `byte`, `char`, their wrappers, `AtomicInteger`, `AtomicLong`, enums by ordinal, and the dates and times built from a number (`Date`, `java.sql.Date`, `Timestamp`, `Calendar`, `Instant`, `Duration`, `LocalTime`, `LocalDate`, `OffsetTime` and the rest). A finite value beyond `float` or `double` is refused too, while `NaN` and infinity themselves still convert to `float` and `double`. A huge `BigDecimal` (1e999999999) is refused by counting its digits, at once, and a huge `BigInteger` appears in the message by its size, not its digits. Cost: at most 0.6 ns on the measured conversions on JDK 21 (`Double` → `int` 6.1 → 6.6 ns, `Long` → `short` 8.1 → 8.7 ns; `BigDecimal` → `int` on par), and on JDK 8 within 0.5 ns except `BigDecimal` → `int`, 11.6 → 13.8 ns.
  * **Tests.** `ConverterNarrowingTest` (8): every source against `int`, `long`, `short`, `byte`, `char` and the atomics; `NaN` and infinity against 18 targets; `float`/`double` overflow from `double`, `BigDecimal`, `BigInteger` and `String`; dates and times out of range; `char`, `Year`, `YearMonth`, `MonthDay`; enum ordinals alone and in a collection; truncation kept; the message, and a huge `BigDecimal` refused within 500 ms. Seven fail on the previous code. `ConverterEverythingTest`'s 51 entries that pinned the wrapping (most marked "wrap around") now expect the refusal, its char messages the new wording, and `Date(Long.MAX_VALUE)` → `double` is no longer checked in reverse (the double rounds past a long); `ConverterTest`'s precision-loss tests are split into precision loss (kept) and overflow (refused). Found by the review of the `convert` package (its M7). Full suite: 21,336 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,753 + 165 + 28 (with its ReturnValueTest updated), 0 failures, against it.
* **BEHAVIOR CHANGE**: **Text that is a number converts to a date or time type as that number would.** Integer text (`"1500"`) converts as a `Long` would: milliseconds, or nanoseconds under the `*.long.precision` setting. Decimal text (`"1.5"`, `"1500.0"`) converts as a `BigDecimal` would: seconds. Before, the text followed no single rule:
  * **`Duration`:** `"1500"` was 1,500 seconds (25 minutes), while the `Long` 1500 is 1.5 seconds.
  * **`LocalTime`:** `"1500"` was the time of day of the instant 1.5 s after 1970, in the Converter's zone (19:00:01.500 in New York), while the `Long` 1500 is 00:00:01.500.
  * **`Instant`, `Date` and other date types:** `"1500"` was milliseconds, but decimal text was refused.
  * **The precision setting** did not reach text at all.

  Now `"1500"` is 1.5 s as a `Duration` and 00:00:01.500 as a `LocalTime`, whatever the zone. `"1.5"` converts to `Instant`, `Date`, `LocalTime` and the rest as `BigDecimal` 1.5 does. Integer text that does not fit a `long` is refused. This applies to `Duration`, `LocalTime`, `OffsetTime`, `java.sql.Time`, `Instant`, `Date`, `java.sql.Date`, `Timestamp`, `Calendar`, `ZonedDateTime`, `OffsetDateTime`, `LocalDateTime` and `LocalDate`. Eight digits that make a date are still that date for the date types (`"20240115"` → 2024-01-15). Every other text — ISO durations, times, dates — is parsed as before, and `Year`, `YearMonth` and `MonthDay` are unchanged. `Integer`, `Short` and `Byte` still do not convert to these types; an `int` cannot hold enough (John). Cost:
  * **Digit text is faster:** → `Date` 95 → 54 ns on JDK 21 and 143 → 49 ns on JDK 8.
  * **ISO durations are faster:** `"PT1M30S"` → `Duration` 209 → 146 ns on JDK 21 and 213 → 177 ns on JDK 8.
  * **Other date text pays 3 to 6 ns** to be told from number text, on the fastest paths: `"2024-01-15"` → `LocalDate` 24.6 → 27.7 ns, and `"2024-01-15 10:30:00"` → `Date` 41 → 47 ns. The rest are within noise.
  * **Tests.** `ConverterNumberTextTest` (6): integer and decimal text must convert to all 13 types exactly as the `Long` or `BigDecimal` does (or be refused when it is); `"1500"` → `LocalTime` must not depend on the zone; the nanosecond precision setting must reach integer text; `"20240115"`, ISO durations, times and dates must read as before; integer text beyond a `long` must be refused; and a `Map`'s value must convert by its own type. Three fail on the previous code. `ConverterEverythingTest`'s three `Map` → `Duration` entries with an integral `BigDecimal` now use `123.0` and `Long.MIN_VALUE + ".0"`. Its json-io round trip runs on the test-scope json-io, which before 4.113.0 hands a typed `BigDecimal` inside a `Map` to the Converter as its text; json-io 4.113.0 keeps the type. Found by the review of the `convert` package. Full suite: 21,340 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,753 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUILD**: **Tests run against json-io 4.112.0** (test scope), up from 4.111.0, now that it is released; java-util's test-scope json-io trails by one release. `ConverterEverythingTest` round-trips every conversion through it. The `Charset`, `InetAddress`, `LongAdder` and `DoubleAdder` tests stay in their own classes: json-io's support for them ships in 4.113.0 (4.112.0 still cannot read them back), so they move into `ConverterEverythingTest` when the test scope reaches json-io 4.113.0. Full suite: 21,340 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,753 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **TESTING**: **`java.sql.Time` is in `ConverterEverythingTest`**, now that the test-scope json-io is 4.112.0, which round-trips it. Every pair is there: `Time` → `String`, `CharSequence`, `StringBuilder`, `StringBuffer`, `LocalTime`, `Map` and `Time`; and `Long`, `long`, `AtomicLong`, `Double`, `double`, `BigInteger`, `BigDecimal`, `Date`, `Timestamp`, `java.sql.Date`, `Instant`, `Calendar`, `ZonedDateTime`, `OffsetDateTime`, `LocalDateTime`, `LocalDate`, `Duration`, `LocalTime`, `String`, `CharSequence`, `Map` and `null` → `Time`. Each is checked directly, through json-io, and in reverse where it reverses. `LongAdder` and `DoubleAdder` sources wait for json-io 4.113.0. `SqlTimeConversionsTest` keeps the checks a pair cannot state. Full suite: 21,438 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,753 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **SECURITY**: **A number with a huge exponent is no longer written out or built in full.** `"1e999999999"` is 11 characters. Converted to a `BigInteger` it is about 415 MB, and to a `String` (`toPlainString()`, a billion digits) it is a 1 GB string; either exhausted the heap. Untrusted text or JSON reached both. So did `LocalTime`/`OffsetTime`'s error message, which wrote the value out in full, and `DeepEquals`' difference report. Below 1e-999999999 the same held for the leading zeros of `"0.000…1"`. Now one limit, `ConverterOptions.getMaxPlainDigits()`, default 1,000, caps how many digits a number may be written out in, ignoring the decimal point, beyond the digits it already has:
  * **Plain text** (`BigDecimal` → `String`, its `Map` form, error messages, `DeepEquals`): a value whose plain form fits — every amount of money (999 trillion to the cent is 17 digits), `9.99E14` (`"999000000000000"`), `1E18`, a 1,234-digit number spelled out in full — is written in plain digits as before. Past the limit it is written with its exponent, `"1E+1000"`, which reads back as the same `BigDecimal`.
  * **Whole numbers** (`BigDecimal` or text → `BigInteger`, `UUID`, `BitSet`): past the limit, an `IllegalArgumentException` naming the digit count, decided from the precision and scale without building anything. `"1e999"` is still 10^999; a number spelled out in full converts whatever its length.
  * **New helpers:** `MathUtilities.plainDigits(BigDecimal)`, `fitsPlainDigits(BigDecimal, int)` and `toPlainString(BigDecimal, int)` apply the rule, for json-io and other callers.
  * **Cost:** `BigDecimal` → `String` stays on par on JDK 21 and JDK 8 (the common case checks the scale alone; `12345.67` 16.9 → 17.1 ns on JDK 8). → `BigInteger` is within 1 ns.
  * **Tests.** `ConverterPlainDigitsTest` (5): money, `9.99E14`, `1E18`, 1,000-digit and 1,234-digit values must stay plain; `1E+1000` and `1E-1000` must use the exponent and read back; `1E999999999` must be written at once; `1E999` must build 10^999 while `1E1000`, `"1e1000"` and `1E999999999` are refused for `BigInteger`, `UUID` and `BitSet` at once; a limit of 50 must apply; the `LocalTime` error and `DeepEquals`' report must stay short. On the previous code the test JVM ran out of memory. Found by the review of the `convert` package (its huge-exponent item), settled with John: 1,000 digits, counted regardless of the decimal point. Full suite: 21,443 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21 and on JDK 8; json-io 4,757 + 165 + 28, 0 failures, against it.
* **BUG FIX**: **A `Pattern` converted to a `String` keeps its flags, written inline: `Pattern.compile("abc", CASE_INSENSITIVE)` is `"(?i)abc"`.** The text was `pattern()` alone, so the `Pattern` read back from it was case-sensitive, and the same for `MULTILINE`, `DOTALL`, `COMMENTS` and the others. A `Pattern` without flags is written as before.
  * **Form:** Java's own inline syntax, letters in the order `idmsuxU`. `LITERAL` is written as the quoted text, `\Q...\E`. `CANON_EQ` has no inline form and is left out; the `Map` form keeps it, as `"flags"`.
  * **Flags the regex sets itself are not written twice.** `Pattern.flags()` also reports the flags a regex turns on at its top level: `"(?i)abc"` compiled without flags reports `CASE_INSENSITIVE`. Only the flags the `Pattern` was compiled with are written, so `"(?i)abc"` stays `"(?i)abc"` and does not grow on each round trip, and `"a(?i)b"` does not become `"(?i)a(?i)b"`, which would fold the case of the `a`. When the two cannot be told apart, nothing is added that the regex does not already say.
  * **The `Map` form had the same defect.** `"flags"` held what `flags()` reports, so `"a(?i)b"` read back from its `Map` matched `"Ab"`. It now holds the flags the `Pattern` was compiled with.
  * **`String` → `Pattern` no longer trims.** Whitespace is part of a regex: `" x "` became `/x/`. The `Map` form follows.
  * **Tests.** `ConverterPatternTextTest` (5): flags written inline; seven flags plus `LITERAL` round-trip, matching what they matched; inline flags in the regex are not repeated, including under `COMMENTS`, where `#(?i)` is a comment; whitespace is kept through the `String` and the `Map` forms; the `Map` form keeps the flags. All 5 fail on the old code, and a mutant that puts the raw `flags()` back in the `Map` form fails the `Map` assertion. `PatternConversionsTest`'s trim and flag expectations are updated.
* **BUG FIX**: **A zone ID converts to `TimeZone` and `ZoneId` in any case, and an ID that names no zone is refused.** `String` → `TimeZone` gave GMT for an ID it did not recognize, as `TimeZone.getTimeZone` does, so `"Amercia/New_York"` and `"america/new_york"` both moved every time computed with them by 4–5 hours, without a word. `String` → `ZoneId` refused both.
  * **Any case:** `"america/new_york"` and `"AMERICA/NEW_YORK"` are `America/New_York`, with its daylight saving time. No two IDs the JDK knows (632 on JDK 21 and JDK 8) differ only in case. Short IDs, prefixes and offsets too: `"utc"`, `"est"`, `"z"`, `"gmt+5"`, `"utc+05:00"`. Only ASCII is folded: no zone ID has another letter, and a Kelvin sign would fold to `k`.
  * **Unknown:** an ID that names no zone in any case is an `IllegalArgumentException`, `Unknown time-zone ID: 'Amercia/New_York'`, from both `TimeZone` and `ZoneId`, as `DateUtilities` and `String` → `ZonedDateTime` already did. The `Map` forms follow.
  * **JDK 8:** `"UTC+05:00"` and `"UT+5"` gave GMT on JDK 8, whose `TimeZone.getTimeZone(ZoneId)` does not read those prefixes; they are `GMT+05:00`, as on JDK 9+.
  * **Unchanged:** every other ID that read before reads the same, exactly as before; the lookup by lower case runs only after the exact lookup fails, from a table built the first time. Speed on par on JDK 21 and JDK 8 (`String` → `ZoneId` 12.7 ns JDK 21, 16.4 ns JDK 8).
  * **Tests.** `ConverterZoneIdCaseTest` (4): any case names the zone, with its offsets in January and July; short IDs, prefixes and offsets in any case; exact IDs unchanged for both targets; unknown IDs (a misspelling, `"bogus"`, a Kelvin sign, `"gmt+99"`) refused by both, and through the `Map` form. 3 fail on the old code. `ConverterEverythingTest`: `"American/FunkyTown"` is refused (it pinned GMT) and `"america/new_york"` reads; its BitSet coverage builder made a `TimeZone` from `"101010"`, which only worked as GMT, and now uses UTC. `ConverterTimeZoneTest`'s GMT pin now expects the refusal.
* **BUG FIX**: **On JDK 8, a zone written `UTC+05:00` or `UT+5` is that offset, not GMT, wherever the Converter makes a `TimeZone`.** JDK 8's `TimeZone.getTimeZone(ZoneId)` does not read the prefixes `UTC` and `UT` before an offset and answers GMT; JDK 9+ reads them. On JDK 8, `ZoneId.of("UTC+05:00")` → `TimeZone` was GMT, and with such a zone in `ConverterOptions` every `Calendar` the Converter made was in GMT (`getTimeZone()` is built from `getZoneId()`), as was `"2024-07-01T12:00 UTC+5"` → `Calendar`.
  * **Where:** `ZoneId` → `TimeZone`, `ConverterOptions.getTimeZone()`, `java.sql.Date` → `Calendar` and `String` → `Calendar` go through the one rule JDK 9+ applies (`ZoneIdConversions.timeZone`). JDK 9+ results are unchanged.
  * **Tests.** `ConverterUtcPrefixZoneTest` (3): `ZoneId` → `TimeZone` for `UTC+05:00`, `UT-03:30`, `UT`, `UTC` and a region; the Converter's own zone, through `Long` and `java.sql.Date` → `Calendar`; text in that zone → `Calendar`. All 3 fail on JDK 8 with the old code and pass on JDK 21 with both.
* **BUG FIX**: **`String` → `Calendar` makes the same kind of `Calendar` as every other conversion: the calendar system of the default FORMAT locale, as `Calendar.getInstance` does.** It alone took the general default locale, so where an application set the two apart (`Locale.setDefault(Locale.Category.FORMAT, ...)`), text could become a `GregorianCalendar` while a `Long` became a `BuddhistCalendar`. The Converter keeps the JDK's (and Jackson's) rule: a `Calendar` follows the locale's calendar system, the instant is exact either way.
  * **Tests.** `ConverterCalendarLocaleTest` (1): with the FORMAT locale `th_TH` and the general default `en_US`, `String` and `Long` → `Calendar` must both be `Calendar.getInstance()`'s class, at the same instant. Fails on the old code.
* **BUG FIX**: **`Calendar` → `LocalTime` is the Calendar's own time of day, as its other conversions are.** 4.93.0 made every `Calendar` conversion use the Calendar's own zone, by registering `Calendar` → `Year`, `YearMonth`, `MonthDay` and `java.sql.Date` directly; `LocalTime` still went through `ZonedDateTime`, which converts to the Converter's zone. With the Converter in New York, a Tokyo `Calendar` at 05:30 on July 1 gave `LocalTime` 16:30 while its `LocalDateTime` was `2024-07-01T05:30`.
  * **Cost:** faster, as it no longer changes zone: 33 → 17 ns on JDK 21, 125 → 112 ns on JDK 8 (medians).
  * **Unchanged:** `ZonedDateTime` and `OffsetDateTime` still convert to local types in the Converter's zone.
  * **Tests.** `ConverterCalendarZoneTest.everyLocalTypeIsTheCalendarsOwnLocalTime`: a Tokyo `Calendar` with the Converter in New York must give its own `LocalTime`, `LocalDateTime`, `LocalDate`, `java.sql.Date`, `YearMonth`, `MonthDay` and `OffsetDateTime`. `LocalTime` fails on the old code; the others pin 4.93.0's rule.
* **CHANGE**: **A source that carries a zone keeps its own wall clock. The Converter's zone applies only where the source has none (`Date`, `Instant`, `Long`, `Timestamp`, numbers), and when a local value is turned into an instant (`LocalDate` → `Instant`).** `ZonedDateTime` and `OffsetDateTime` were first moved to the Converter's zone: with the Converter in Los Angeles, `2024-07-01T05:30+09:00[Asia/Tokyo]` as a `ZonedDateTime` gave `LocalDate` 2024-06-30 and `LocalTime` 13:30, while the same moment as a Tokyo `Calendar`, or as text, gave 2024-07-01 and 05:30.
  * **What changes:** `ZonedDateTime` and `OffsetDateTime` → `LocalDate`, `LocalTime`, `LocalDateTime`, `java.sql.Date`, `Year`, `YearMonth`, `MonthDay` give the date and time they show (`zdt.toLocalDate()`, as java.time does); `ZonedDateTime` → `Calendar` is a `Calendar` in its zone; `OffsetDateTime` → `ZonedDateTime` and `Calendar` are at its offset (`odt.toZonedDateTime()`; an offset has no region to give back). Only where the value's zone differs from the Converter's. Conversions to instants (`Instant`, `Date`, `Long`, `Timestamp`) do not change.
  * **The application's zone is still one step away:** convert through `Instant`, which has no zone: `converter.convert(zdt.toInstant(), LocalDate.class)`.
  * **Agrees with** `Calendar` (4.93.0) and text with a zone, java.time, the `getZoneId()` Javadoc ("when one is not provided", now spelled out), and n-cube's DATE rule (the day a value prints in its own zone). The user guide states the rule under "Which zone applies".
  * **Faster:** no zone change. `ZonedDateTime` → `LocalDate` 22 → 4 ns on JDK 21, about 80–120 → 4 ns on JDK 8; `OffsetDateTime` → `LocalDate` 26 → 4.5 ns and about 120 → 4.4 ns.
  * **Also fixed:** `OffsetDateTime` → `Calendar` set the Calendar's milliseconds from `toLong()`, which follows `modern.time.long.precision`, so under `nanos` it gave a Calendar in the year 54,499,609.
  * **Tests.** `ConverterWallClockTest` (4): a `ZonedDateTime`, `OffsetDateTime`, `Calendar`, and text with a zone or an offset keep their own wall clock for all seven local types; zoned targets keep the source's zone; `Instant`, `Date`, `Timestamp` and `Long` use the Converter's zone; a local value becomes an instant in the Converter's zone. The first two fail on the old code. `ConverterTest.testLocalDateTimeToZonedDateTime` now expects the own wall clock, and the Converter's through `Instant`. `ConverterEverythingTest`: `ZonedDateTime` → `OffsetDateTime` no longer round-trips back to the region (an offset has none); `OffsetDateTime` → `ZonedDateTime` entries expect the offset zone. `OffsetDateTimeConversionsTests`' `java.sql.Date` cases expect the day each value shows (they expected the JVM zone's day for six offsets of one instant).
