* **BUG FIX**: **With `modern.time.long.precision` set to `nanos`, `ZonedDateTime`, `OffsetDateTime`, `LocalDateTime`, `LocalDate` and `Calendar` → `Date` gave the year 54,499,609.** `ZonedDateTime` and `OffsetDateTime` → `Date` built the `Date` from `toLong()`, which counts nanoseconds under that setting; a `Date` counts milliseconds. `Calendar`, `LocalDateTime` and `LocalDate` reach `Date` through `ZonedDateTime`. Now milliseconds whatever the setting; `→ long` still follows it.
  * **Faster:** the setting is no longer read (a system property and an option) on every call: `ZonedDateTime` → `Date` 13.7 → 8.2 ns on JDK 21, 14.4 → 9.4 ns on JDK 8; `OffsetDateTime` the same.
  * **Tests.** `ConverterNanosPrecisionDateTest` (2): under `nanos`, the five sources and `Instant` → `Date`, and `ZonedDateTime` → `Calendar`, must be the moment to the millisecond (fails on the old code); `ZonedDateTime` and `OffsetDateTime` → `long` must still be nanoseconds, and read back.
* **CHANGE**: **Text with an offset and a zone that disagree is read at the offset, as java.time reads it: `"2024-07-01T12:00+05:00[Europe/Berlin]"` is 07:00Z, shown as 09:00+02:00 in Berlin.** The zone won (10:00Z). The case that matters is text written before a zone's rules changed: `"2019-11-15T12:00-02:00[America/Sao_Paulo]"`, written for a daylight time Brazil abolished in 2019, read back an hour off (15:00Z); it now keeps the instant the text states (14:00Z). Applies to `DateUtilities` and every `String` → date conversion, the same on every JDK: java.time reads it this way on JDK 9 and later, while JDK 8's `ZonedDateTime.parse` lets the zone win, even in the repeated hour. Text whose offset agrees with its zone reads as before, including the hour that repeats when clocks go back.
  * **Exception:** the zone's own offset cut to whole minutes means the zone's exact offset. Until 1972 some zones kept local mean time with seconds (Berlin `+00:53:28` until 1893, Liberia `-00:44:30` until 1972), and `XXX` patterns write it as `+00:53`; that text keeps reading as the zone reads it.
  * **The hour clocks skip:** the offset is applied to the time as written (`02:30+01:00` in Berlin on 2024-03-31 is 01:30Z, `02:30+02:00` is 00:30Z, as java.time reads them). The zone had already moved the time forward an hour.
  * **`Calendar` → `String` writes the seconds of an offset when it has any:** `1880-01-01T12:00:00+00:53:28[Europe/Berlin]` (it wrote `+00:53`). Dates since 1972 are written as before.
  * **Cost:** parsing `"…+02:00[Europe/Berlin]"` on par on JDK 21 and JDK 8.
  * **Tests.** `ConverterOffsetVersusZoneTest` (3): ten texts (a contradictory offset, a winter offset in July, Brazil 2019, both offsets in the skipped and the repeated hour, a consistent one, a `UTC` zone, local mean time in full) must read at the instants their offsets state, which `ZonedDateTime.parse` also gives on JDK 9+ (checked there); the zone's offset cut to minutes (Berlin 1880, Monrovia 1960, New York 1880) is the zone's; a `Calendar` is written with its offset's seconds and reads back, and a modern one as before. The first and third fail on the old code.
* **IMPROVED**: **`CompactSet.getConfig()` reports the backing map type (`CompactMap.MAP_TYPE`), as `CompactMap.getConfig()` does.** It left it out, so a reader of the config could not keep it: json-io read a `CompactSet` built on `ConcurrentHashMap` back on `HashMap`, no longer thread-safe, and one on `ConcurrentSkipListMap` back on `TreeMap`. `withConfig(...)` is unchanged: it keeps the set's own backing map (unless the ordering changes) and ignores one in the config it is given.
  * **Tests.** `CompactSetMapTypeConfigTest` (2): the config names a custom backing map and each ordering's default (`HashMap`, `TreeMap`, `LinkedHashMap`); `withConfig(getConfig())` keeps a `ConcurrentHashMap`. Both fail on the old code. `CompactSetTest` pinned three keys and asserted the map type was hidden; it now expects four, and that `withConfig` ignores a different map type handed to it.
* **BUG FIX**: **`Traverser` reaches objects held in `Optional`, `AtomicReference`, `AtomicReferenceArray` and a standalone `Map.Entry`, and the outer object and captured variables of inner, anonymous and local classes.** Its Javadoc promises "all object reference fields"; these were never reached, on JDK 8 or 21, so `GraphComparator` did not see a `Customer` held in an `Optional` or an `AtomicReference`, and could not report it changed or removed.
  * **JDK holders, through their public methods**, so every JDK still gives the same graph: an `Optional`'s value, an `AtomicReference`'s, an `AtomicReferenceArray`'s elements, the key and value of a JDK `Map.Entry`. Any other JDK class stays a leaf (4.113.0 made them leaves). `AtomicReference.get()` and `AtomicReferenceArray.get()` are final, so a user subclass runs no user code; a `Map.Entry` is read only when its class is the JDK's. An `AtomicReferenceArray` honors `traverser.max.array.length`.
  * **Compiler-made references:** an inner object's outer object (`this$0`) and an anonymous or local class's captured variables (`val$name`) are walked, and reported by `getFields()`. Other synthetic fields are still skipped. To stop at a large outer object, list its class in `classesToSkip`.
  * **Cost:** a traversal of 131,071 plain objects is on par on JDK 8 (45.4 ns per object) and about 1 ns per object slower on JDK 21 (45.3 → 46.2 ns), for the holder checks.
  * **Docs:** the class Javadoc and the user guide list what is followed.
  * **Tests.** `TraverserHeldReferencesTest` (5): the holders, an entry in a `List`, an `AtomicReference` subclass, an inner class's outer object, anonymous and local classes' captured variables, each object visited once, and `classesToSkip` stopping at the outer object. Three fail on the old code. `TraverserTest` and `TraverserBehaviorTest` declared local and anonymous classes in instance methods, which now also reach the test object; they are declared statically, keeping what those tests check.
* **BUG FIX**: **`TypeUtilities.resolveType` gives the right answer in three cases it got wrong.**
  * **A raw root:** with no type arguments known (`Box.class`, `T extends Number`), a bare `T` came back as `T` while the `T` in `List<T>` came back as its bound (`List<Number>`). Both are now the bound, which is what a raw type means; `resolveTypeUsingInstance` already applied it. A root that binds the variable still gives the binding.
  * **A variable of an enclosing class:** in `OC<String>.Inner`, OC's `T` used inside `Inner` gave `Object`; it is now `String`, taken from the owner type (after the type's own hierarchy, which binds an inherited field's variable first).
  * **Arrays:** `T[]` with `T` resolved to a class came back as a `GenericArrayType` that printed `Integer[]` but was not equal to `Integer[].class`; it is now `Integer[].class`, as reflection gives a non-generic array. A `GenericArrayType` remains only while the component is itself generic (`List<String>[]`).
  * **json-io:** 60 generic reads compared before and after: none that worked changed. Four that failed still fail, now on the right field (an `OC<String>.Inner` value is known to be a `String`; `G<Integer>.array` is known to be an `Integer[]`, though json-io does not yet convert its elements: "java.lang.Long" where it said "Unresolved type: T[]"). json-io does not yet pass a field's own type arguments down, so it does not benefit for an inner class's scalar or a `G<Integer>` field; that is a json-io change.
  * **Cost:** resolving with a `Class` root (cached) on par or faster; `T[]` against `G<Integer>` 26.1 → 24.9 ns on JDK 21, 43.2 → 45.3 ns on JDK 8 (the cached array class costs more than the old wrapper there; `Array.newInstance` measured far worse); an inner class's outer variable 25.5 → 32.1 ns on JDK 21, 29.2 → 39.8 ns on JDK 8, the price of finding the answer instead of giving `Object`.
  * **Tests.** `TypeUtilitiesRawOwnerArrayTest` (3), one per flaw, each failing on the old code. Pins of the old answers updated: `TypeUtilitiesBehaviorTest` (an unbound variable is its bound, still not the class's own supertype), `TypeUtilitiesTest` (`T[]` is `Integer[].class`; a raw root gives the bound), `GenericArrayTypeImplEqualsHashCodeTest` (now on `List<T>[]`, which stays generic). The user guide states the three rules.
* **BUG FIX**: **`IntervalSet` and `IntervalSet.Interval` are equal by value: `[1, 2.0)` equals `[1, 2.00)`, with the same hash code.** Every other operation compares bounds with `compareTo`, for which `2.0` and `2.00` are one number; `equals` used `BigDecimal.equals`, which also compares the number of decimal places. So `{[1, 2.0)}` and `{[1, 2.00)}` were unequal while their `difference` was empty, a `HashSet` holding one did not find the other, and a set's equality depended on which spelling of a bound it had stored first (adding `[1, 2.00)` then `[1.0, 2.0)` kept `[1-2.00)`). The JDK's `TreeSet` calls `{2.0}` and `{2.00}` equal but gives them different hash codes; here both agree.
  * **Rule:** two bounds are the same value when `equals`, or of one class and equal by `compareTo`; bounds of different classes are not compared (an `Integer` interval is not a `Long` one, and no exception). The hash of a `BigDecimal` bound is taken without its trailing zeros, every zero alike; other types hash as before. A bound type whose `equals` disagrees with its `compareTo` in some other way gets the same equality but not a hash to match.
  * **Cost:** 1,000-interval sets: `equals` and `hashCode` on par for `Integer` bounds; `equals` on par or faster for `BigDecimal`; `hashCode` with `BigDecimal` bounds 6.7 → 7.6 ns per interval on JDK 21, 6.3 → 7.1 on JDK 8 (removing trailing zeros).
  * **Tests.** `IntervalSetScaleEqualityTest` (3): sets and intervals equal across scales, with equal hash codes and `HashSet` lookup, including zero and `1E+1`; the order of adding does not matter; different values, and `Integer` against `Long`, stay unequal. The first two fail on the old code.
* **BUILD**: **`module-info.class` moves to `META-INF/versions/9` of a multi-release jar.** It sat at the root of the jar as a Java 9 class file (version 53), the one class in a JDK 8 library that JDK 8-era tools reading every class in a jar (bytecode scanners, shrinkers, older Android tooling) could not read. Only JDK 9 and later look under `META-INF/versions`, and they find the same module: name, `requires` and `exports` unchanged.
  * **Nothing changes for users:** JDK 8 ignores the multi-release marker and `META-INF/versions` and runs the same classes; JDK 9+ reads the same module descriptor; classpath users on any JDK see no difference. The OSGi manifest is unchanged apart from `Multi-Release: true` (moditect's `jvmVersion` 9, which adds it).
  * **Verified on the built jar:** `--describe-module` on JDK 11, 17, 21 and 25; a module requiring `com.cedarsoftware.util` runs on JDK 11, 21 and 25; classpath use on JDK 8 and 21.
  * **Tests.** New integration tests (`maven-failsafe-plugin`, `*IT`, after `package`, against the packaged jar rather than `target/classes`): `JarLayoutIT` (3) checks that every class outside `META-INF/versions` is a JDK 8 class file, that `module-info` is under `versions/9` of a `Multi-Release` jar, and that the OSGi headers and exports are kept. The first two fail on the old layout.
* **BUG FIX**: **On JDK 21+, `ConcurrentList.reversed()` works however the list is held.** JDK 21 gave both `List` and `Deque` a `reversed()`, and `ConcurrentList` is both; compiled for JDK 8, it could not define its own. Held as a `SequencedCollection`, the JVM found two and threw `AbstractMethodError`; called on a `ConcurrentList` variable, javac reported `reversed()` as ambiguous. Held as a `List` or a `Deque` it worked, and still does the same.
  * **How:** `ConcurrentList` now extends a package-private `ConcurrentListBase`, empty in the JDK 8 classes; its JDK 21 version (`src/main/java21`, compiled with `--release 21` into `META-INF/versions/21` of the multi-release jar) defines `reversed()` as `List`'s live reversed view, and javac adds the `SequencedCollection` bridge. JDK 8 to 20 load the empty superclass; nothing else changes. The jar still targets and runs on JDK 8+; only the build of a release needs JDK 21+, and a release build (`-DperformRelease=true`) on an older JDK now stops with a message saying so (`maven-enforcer-plugin`, release profile only; ordinary builds on JDK 8+ are unaffected).
  * **Compatible:** a `ConcurrentList` serialized with the previous jar reads with this one (JDK 8 and 21), and the reverse; the superclass is not `Serializable` and adds no state. bnd writes `META-INF/versions/21/OSGI-INF/MANIFEST.MF`, the OSGi R7 supplement for JDK 21 (the same imports, `JavaSE 21`); the main manifest still requires `JavaSE 1.8`. The module (in `versions/9`) loads on JDK 11, 21 and 25.
  * **Verified:** through `SequencedCollection`, `List`, `Deque` and a `ConcurrentList` variable on JDK 21 and 25.
  * **Tests.** `ConcurrentListSequencedIT` (2, failsafe, against the packaged jar): on JDK 21+, `reversed()` through `SequencedCollection`, `List` and `Deque` gives `[3, 2, 1]`, and the view is live; below JDK 21 the superclass declares nothing. The first fails on the old jar with `AbstractMethodError`. The JaCoCo report skips `META-INF/**` (the two versions of the class share a name).
* **IMPROVED**: **`computeIfAbsent` in `LRUCache` (LOCKING, the default) and `TTLCache` loads single-flight per key, outside the cache's lock.** The function ran under the one lock of the cache, so loads ran one at a time even for different keys, every other operation waited for them, and, as all TTLCaches share one purge thread, a slow load in one stalled purging in all. Eight threads loading eight keys with a 50 ms function took about 470 ms, and an unrelated `put` waited about 460 ms; now about 60 ms and 0 ms (JDK 21 and JDK 8), as `ConcurrentHashMap` and the THREADED strategy.
  * **Rule:** a key's function runs once however many threads ask for it at once; the others wait for its value. Loads of different keys run in parallel. Nothing else waits for a function. The cache's lock is taken only to write the result. (`com.cedarsoftware.util.internal.KeyedLoads`.)
  * **What a caller can see change:**
    * **Exceptions:** threads that waited for a load that threw get the same exception, and nothing is cached; each of them ran the function again in turn before.
    * **A function that asks for its own key again** throws `IllegalStateException` (it would wait for itself), as `ConcurrentHashMap` does; before, the lock was reentrant and the nested call ran the function again.
    * **Functions on different threads that need each other's keys** (A loads k1, which needs k2, while B loads k2, which needs k1) fail with `IllegalStateException` ("Cyclic load"): the wait that would close the loop is detected, across caches too, where `ConcurrentHashMap`, Guava and Caffeine hang. Such a loop never finishes even on one thread (it overflowed the stack before; it throws `IllegalStateException` now). A function may load other keys; dependencies without a loop never wait on each other in a cycle.
    * **A value written for the key while its function runs** (a `put` from another thread, or, against the Map contract, from the function itself) is kept and returned; the computed value is dropped. In either order of the two, the `put` is the last write.
  * **Cost:** hits on par (LRU 12.2 → 12.6 ns on JDK 21, 10.1 → 12.0 on JDK 8; TTL on par); with a trivial function, a miss costs about 16 ns more in LRU and 5 ns more in TTL, and a 4-thread mix with half misses runs 50–75% (LRU) and 20–45% (TTL) slower: the bookkeeping of in-flight loads. Any function that takes time, and any contention on slow loads, is far faster. n-cube's one LOCKING `computeIfAbsent` (path segments, mostly hits) is on par on JDK 21.
  * **Tests.** `CacheLoadSingleFlightTest` (10), each over LOCKING, size-limited TTL and unbounded TTL: different keys load in parallel (eight loaders that must all run at once); a key loads once for eight threads; other operations do not wait for a loader; a failed load reaches its waiters, caches nothing, and is retried by the next call; a recursive load of the same key throws, a nested load of another key works; a `put` while loading is kept; a slow load does not stall purging of another TTLCache; nulls and hits; the size limit holds under 32 concurrent loads; loads that need each other's keys on two threads, in one cache and across two, fail instead of hanging. Seven fail on the old code (the last hangs). Repeated 15 times on JDK 21 and JDK 8 without a failure. `CacheAtomicityTest`'s self-writing `computeIfAbsent` now keeps the function's write (still one entry, within capacity).
* **PERFORMANCE**: **`LRUCache`'s LOCKING strategy (the default) evicts by CLOCK (second chance), so a read takes no lock.** A `get` took the cache's lock (by `tryLock`) to move the entry to the front, so every read was a write that contended on one lock, and a read that found the lock held lost its use. Now a read only sets the entry's "used" flag; eviction walks from the oldest entry, clears a flagged entry's flag and passes it over once (moving it to the front), and evicts the first entry not used since. Writes, removal and `compute` are unchanged, as is the unlinking that keeps removed nodes from holding their neighbours (GC nepotism).
  * **How close to LRU:** simulated against exact LRU over seven request patterns (Zipf 0.7, 0.99, 1.2; Zipf with scans; uniform; a shifting working set; a scan larger than the cache), CLOCK's hit rate was never lower and up to 1.3 points higher; 87–100% of the entries the two kept were the same; on skewed traffic 97–100% of CLOCK's victims were among the least recently used tenth of the cache. Which entry is evicted next can differ from strict LRU (a test pins one such sequence). Under load, no read is lost any more.
  * **Faster** (millions of operations per second, JDK 21 / JDK 8): reads, 1 thread 55 → 166 / 72 → 148; 8 threads 118 → 1,093 / 186 → 1,101; 100 hot keys, 8 threads 397 → 1,908 / 363 → 1,897; 90% reads and 10% writes with eviction, 1 thread 57 → 75 / 58 → 73, 8 threads 32 → 57 / 32 → 47.
  * **Tests.** `LRUCacheClockTest` (3): a read made while another thread holds the lock still saves the entry from eviction; the second-chance rule on a sequence where it differs from exact LRU (both fail on the old code); on a Zipf stream the cache's hit rate is within half a point of `LinkedHashMap`'s exact LRU.
* **IMPROVED**: **`ConcurrentHashMapNullSafe`, `ConcurrentNavigableMapNullSafe` and `ConcurrentNavigableSetNullSafe` are `Serializable`, as the JDK collections they stand in for (`ConcurrentHashMap`, `ConcurrentSkipListMap`, `ConcurrentSkipListSet`) are.** They threw `NotSerializableException`, so one in an HTTP session, a cache configured for Java serialization, an RMI call or a Spark task failed where the JDK collection worked.
  * **Form:** each writes a small serialization proxy holding its entries in order, with real nulls (the placeholders that stand for null inside are never written), and the comparator for the sorted ones; reading it rebuilds the collection through the public constructor. A stream naming the class itself is refused. As for `TreeMap`, a comparator that is not `Serializable` cannot be written (`NotSerializableException`). A descending view comes back as a descending view of a copy; a range view as a copy of what it holds, without its bounds. (`ConcurrentNavigableSetNullSafe` now records whether a view is descending, as the map did.)
  * **Nothing changes for code that does not serialize.** json-io and n-cube do not use Java serialization.
  * **Tests.** `NullSafeConcurrentSerializationTest` (5): each class round-trips with null keys, values and elements, a custom comparator (`String.CASE_INSENSITIVE_ORDER`, kept), descending views and empty collections, and keeps working; a non-serializable comparator is refused; one map held twice inside another serialized object comes back as one object. Four fail on the old code.
* **CHANGE**: **A float converts to every type as the decimal it prints: an integer type takes that decimal truncated toward zero, as BigDecimal and String take it.** `1.5e10f` is 15000000000 as a `Long`, a `BigInteger` and a `BigDecimal` (`1.5E+10`). `Float` → `BigInteger` widened the float to a double and took the double's digits (`3.4e38f` gave 339999995214436420000000000000000000000, neither the float's decimal nor its binary value); the integer types took the binary value (`1.5e10f` gave 15000000512, `1e18f` 999999984306749440). Below 2^24 (16,777,216) every whole float is exact, and fractions truncate as before, so only larger floats change.
  * **One decimal on every JDK:** the decimal is the one `Float.toString` prints from JDK 19 on, computed by `FloatDecimal` (Schubfach) with no printing, so JDK 8 gives the same answers. `Float` → `BigDecimal` uses it too: JDK 8 printed 19 floats with a longer decimal (`9.9E-44` as `1.0E-43`), and the scale is the one `new BigDecimal(Float.toString(f))` has. `FloatDecimal.printedDecimal` matched JDK 21's `new BigDecimal(Float.toString(f))`, value and scale, for every finite float; `printedLong` matched its `longValue()` for 398 million.
  * **Doubles:** the same rule, in the next entry.
  * **Cost:** `Float` → `BigDecimal` 39 → 16 ns (JDK 21), 51 → 15 ns (JDK 8); `Float` → `BigInteger` 4-5x faster; `Float` → `Long` on par below 2^24, about 7 → 12.7 ns above it (the decimal is computed).
  * **Tests.** `ConverterFloatingWholeNumberTest` (4): large floats as the decimal they print, as `Long`, `BigInteger`, `AtomicLong`, `Integer`; smaller values, fractions, NaN and range unchanged; the JDK 19 decimal on JDK 8; `Long`, `BigInteger` and `BigDecimal`-then-`Long` agree for 20,000 random floats of every magnitude. Two fail on the old code on JDK 21, three on JDK 8. `ConverterEverythingTest`'s `214748368f` entries (`Long`, `Integer`) now expect 214748370: it prints `2.1474837E8`.
* **CHANGE**: **A double converts to every type as the decimal it prints, the one JDK 19+ prints, on every JDK.** An integer type takes that decimal truncated toward zero, as `BigDecimal` and `String` take it: 2^60 as a double (it prints `1.152921504606847E18`) is 1152921504606847000 as a `Long`, a `BigInteger` and a `BigDecimal`; it was 1152921504606846976 as a `Long`, its binary value. Below 2^53 every whole double is exact, and fractions truncate as before, so only larger doubles change. The double -2^63 (what `Long.MIN_VALUE` becomes) still converts to `Long.MIN_VALUE`: it prints `-9.223372036854776E18`, just past a `long`, but its value is not, and a cast, Jackson and Python all give it back; it is the one double where the two disagree.
  * **One decimal on every JDK.** JDK 8 through 18 print many doubles differently from JDK 19+: 76% of whole doubles beyond 2^53 (`4.8796343143351112E18` for `4.879634314335111E18`), and some everyday ones -- `1.0E23` as `9.999999999999999E22`, `2.0E23` as `1.9999999999999998E23`, `8.41E21` as `8.409999999999999E21`. `Double` → `String`, `BigDecimal` and `BigInteger` took those digits, so they differed between JDKs. They now give the JDK 19 decimal everywhere, and `Float` → `String` the JDK 19 float text (JDK 8 printed 19 floats longer, `9.9E-44` as `1.0E-43`). `MultiKeyMap` takes a fractional double as that decimal too, so a key matches the same `BigDecimal` on every JDK.
  * **`DoubleDecimal`** (internal, new): the Schubfach algorithm for doubles, as `FloatDecimal` is for floats, written from Giulietti's paper; its text, `BigDecimal` (the scale `new BigDecimal(Double.toString(d))` has) and long. Checked against JDK 21's `Double.toString` on about 7 billion doubles -- 4 billion random bit patterns, 2 billion whole doubles around and above 2^53, 426 million across every exponent, 600 million decimal-looking values and their neighbors -- with no difference in the text, the `BigDecimal` (value and scale) or the long. `FloatDecimal` gained the float text the same way. On JDK 19+ the text comes from `Double.toString` / `Float.toString` themselves, which print the same and are faster there; the `BigDecimal` and long never print.
  * **Speed** (Converter, ns per value, median of 3 fresh JVMs, JDK 21 / JDK 8): `Double` → `BigDecimal` 61-75 → 14-24 / 53-100 → 13-21; → `BigInteger` 61-82 → 17-27 / 72-97 → 16-45; → `String` unchanged on JDK 21 (the JDK prints it there), 18-62 → 14-34 on JDK 8 (2.2x for full-precision values, 2 ns slower for whole doubles above 2^53); `Float` → `String` unchanged on JDK 21, 34 → 24-26 on JDK 8; → `Long` unchanged below 2^53, about 5 → 13-14 above it (the decimal is computed).
  * **Tests.** `DoubleDecimalTest` (5): the JDK 19 specification as the oracle, checked with `BigDecimal`, so it holds on JDK 8 -- the text reads back as the double, no shorter decimal does, none of its length is closer -- on 100,000 random bit patterns, 120,000 decimal-looking values and neighbors, every power of 2 and of 10 with neighbors, the smallest subnormals and 40,000 whole doubles; the doubles JDK 8 prints differently; the layout. Eight planted defects (tie to odd, no shorter decimal, the interval below a power of 2, the subnormal digit, either notation boundary, a one-digit `BigDecimal` scale, the long's cast) each fail it on JDK 8. `FloatDecimalTest` gains the float text (every 997th bit pattern). `ConverterFloatingWholeNumberTest` (7, three new) adds doubles: large ones as the decimal they print, smaller ones and fractions unchanged, -2^63 still `Long.MIN_VALUE`, the JDK 19 decimal for `String`, `BigDecimal` and `BigInteger` on JDK 8, and `Long`, `BigInteger` and `BigDecimal`-then-`Long` agreeing for 20,000 random doubles. Two fail on the old code on JDK 21, three on JDK 8. `ConverterDoubleToBigIntegerTest`'s comparison with the JDK's own printing (`%.0f`) now runs on JDK 19+ only: on JDK 8 that printing is the old decimal (`1e23` gave 99999999999999990000000).

* **BUG FIX**: **java-util works in an OSGi framework, and json-io with it.** Three faults, found by running both bundles in Apache Felix 7.0.5 on JDK 21 and JDK 8:
  * **The bundle could not resolve**, in every release from at least 4.102.0: `Unsafe` names `sun.misc.Unsafe` and the `ReflectionFactory` classes as strings, which the bundle tool turned into required imports of `jdk.internal.reflect`, `sun.reflect` and `sun.misc` -- packages no framework exports. They are optional imports now; `Unsafe` already copes without them.
  * **`ClassUtilities.getClassLoader(anchor)` returned the thread's context class loader whatever it could see.** Under OSGi that is usually the launcher's loader, which sees no bundle: json-io's own config classes (its custom writers and readers), the templates behind `CompactMap.builder()` and the classes json-io reads by name went unresolved. The context loader is still the answer when it sees the anchor class -- it is the anchor's loader or a child of it, or it resolves the anchor's name to that very class (a bundle importing its package, Equinox's context finder); otherwise the anchor's own loader is. An ordinary thread, a web application's thread and Equinox are unchanged.
  * **`ClassUtilities.forName` with a loader that does not delegate to the JDK's** -- a bundle's sees only `java.*` and what it imports -- now also finds the JDK's own classes by name. json-io names `sun.util.calendar.ZoneInfo` (the class of every `TimeZone`) in its config, and without it wrote a `TimeZone` it could not read back. Only the JDK's classes are added (the bootstrap and platform loaders), never the application classpath, and a loader that does delegate to the JDK keeps its own verdict, so one that filters JDK classes still does.
  * **`com.cedarsoftware.util.internal` is exported to json-io only**, as the module descriptor's qualified export does: a bundle wires to it only by asking for the mandatory attribute `friend=json-io`.
  * **Speed:** `getClassLoader` 0.5 ns (JDK 21) and 0.9 ns (JDK 8) before and after, with the application's or a child context loader; a `forName` miss is no slower.
  * **Tests.** `ClassUtilitiesContextLoaderTest` (8): a context loader that cannot see the anchor, or holds another copy of its class, is passed over; one that sees it (a child, an importing loader) is kept, and the answer follows the thread's loader; a loader that does not delegate to the JDK finds its classes, but not the application's; a filtering loader keeps its verdict. Four fail on the old code. `JarLayoutIT` (the packaged jar): no JDK-internal import is required, and the internal package's export carries the attribute; it fails on the old manifest. End to end, in a scratch harness: Felix starts both bundles, and json-io writes and reads JSON (doubles, a `TimeZone`, a builder `CompactMap`, a `CaseInsensitiveMap`), TOON and the tokenizer's text, on JDK 21 and JDK 8. Before, java-util did not resolve; with only its manifest fixed, json-io found neither `DoubleDecimal`, its writers nor `ZoneInfo`.
* **BUG FIX (security)**: **`RegexUtilities`' pattern caches are bounded.** They kept every pattern ever compiled, and patterns compiled from data -- the Converter's `String` → `Pattern`, json-io reading a `Pattern` -- came from outside, so a stream of distinct ones grew memory without limit (the invalid-pattern sets too). Each cache (plain, case-insensitive, with flags, and the two invalid-pattern sets) now keeps the 1,000 most recently used (`LRUCache`); a pattern in use stays cached.
  * **Cost:** a cache hit 2.2 → 3.0 ns on JDK 21, 2.9 → 2.2 ns on JDK 8.
  * **Tests.** `RegexUtilitiesBoundedCacheTest` (3): every cache stays within the bound under 3,000 distinct patterns, the Converter's too, and a pattern in use keeps its compiled instance. Two fail on the old code.
* **BUG FIX**: **A double count of seconds converts to a date or time as the decimal it prints.** `Date` and `Calendar` multiplied the double by 1000 and truncated: 1.001 seconds gave 1000 ms and 64.1 gave 64099 (the products are 1000.9999999999999 and 64099.99999999999) -- 741 of the first 100,000 millisecond values lost one. `Instant`, `Duration`, `Timestamp`, `ZonedDateTime`, `OffsetDateTime`, `LocalDateTime`, `LocalDate`, `LocalTime` and `OffsetTime` rounded the double's binary fraction to the nanosecond, which at today's epoch seconds is off by hundreds of nanoseconds: 1700000000.123 gave .122999907. They now take the decimal the double prints: 1001 ms, 64100 ms, .123. A `Date` still keeps whole milliseconds, truncated toward zero; at a `long`'s ends the double's own value still converts (`Date(Long.MIN_VALUE)` round-trips through seconds, as -2^63 does to `Long`).
  * **`DoubleDecimal.printedLong(v, scale)` and `printedNanos(v)`** (internal): the printed decimal scaled, and its fraction in nanoseconds, without a `BigDecimal`.
  * **Cost:** `Double` → `Date` 6 → 14 ns on JDK 21 (4.6 → 13 on JDK 8), → `Instant` 7 → 17 ns (6.7 → 15): the decimal is computed.
  * **Tests.** `ConverterDoubleSecondsTest` (4): milliseconds for `Date` and `Calendar` (truncation kept, toward zero), nanoseconds for `Instant`, `Duration`, `Timestamp` and the zoned and local types, and `Date(Long.MIN_VALUE)`'s round trip. Three fail on the old code. `DoubleDecimalTest` gains the two helpers against `printedDecimal` on 200,000 values.
* **BUG FIX**: **`DateUtilities` reads an offset zone in brackets with a `UTC` or `UT` prefix** -- `[UTC+05:00]`, `[UT-03:00]`, `[UTC+05:00:30]` -- as java.time writes `ZoneId.of("UTC+05:00")`. Only `[GMT+hh:mm]` was read; `[UTC+05:00]` failed with "other characters present", so the Converter could not read back the text it wrote for a `ZonedDateTime` in such a zone.
  * **Tests.** `DateUtilitiesOffsetZoneIdTest` (2): six offset zones read with their zone and instant, and the Converter's round trip of a `ZonedDateTime` in four. Both fail on the old code.
* **ADDED**: **`TypeUtilities.inferKeyType(Type)`**: a Map container type's key type, through its class hierarchy, as `inferElementType` gives its value: `Map<Long, String>` gives `Long`, and `StringMap<Integer>`, where `StringMap<V> extends HashMap<String, V>`, gives `String` -- its one argument is the value's. A container class whose hierarchy fixes the key gives that key (`Names extends TreeMap<String, Integer>`: `String`); a raw `HashMap` gives null. Worked out once per container class, beside the value and element types. json-io used the container's first argument as the key, which parsed `StringMap<Integer>`'s key `"k"` as an int.
  * **Tests.** `TypeUtilitiesInferKeyTypeTest` (4): plain, fixed, reversed, deeper and multi-valued map types; a class whose hierarchy fixes the key; raw and non-map types; `inferElementType` agreeing on the value.
* **BUG FIX**: **The security check judges an array name by its element.** `sun.util.calendar.ZoneInfo` (the class of every `TimeZone`) is exempt from the rule that blocks JDK-internal packages, but the name `sun.util.calendar.ZoneInfo[]` was refused ("For security reasons, cannot load"), so a `TimeZone[]` named that way could not be read. `SecurityChecker.isSecurityBlockedName` now strips the `[]`s first: an exempt class's array is exempt, and a blocked class's array (`java.lang.Runtime[]`) is refused at the first check rather than when its element is loaded.
  * **Tests.** `ClassUtilitiesSecurityTest.testForName_arrayOfAnExemptInternalClassLoads`: fails on the old code.
* **TEST**: **`RegexSdfStreamReviewTest` no longer fails under load.** After its deliberately runaway matches it runs trivial ones to show the pool threads are free again, with the same 75 ms budget, which a loaded machine (the full suite) sometimes exceeded. Those now get 5 seconds; a thread stuck for good still fails it.
* **BUG FIX**: **Number text in another script's decimal digits converts to every number type.** Text in Arabic-Indic, Devanagari or fullwidth digits converted to `Integer`, `Long`, `Short` and `Year` (the JDK's `Integer.parseInt` reads such digits), but `Double`, `Float`, `BigInteger` and `BigDecimal` refused it, as did decimal text for the integer types and number text for the date and time types (integer text as milliseconds, decimal text as seconds). All now read it, as the JDK's own parsers do. ASCII text parses as before -- the other scripts' digits are looked for only when it does not -- so it costs nothing. Another script's decimal separator is not read.
  * **Tests.** `ConverterUnicodeDigitsTest` (4): every number type, decimal text, number text for `Instant` and `Duration`, and text that is no number still refused. Three fail on the old code.
* **ADDED**: **`String` → `Number`.** Whole text -- an optional sign and digits -- is a `Long` within a long's range and a `BigInteger` beyond it; any other text `BigDecimal` reads (`"5.5"`, `"1e3"`, `".5"`) is a `BigDecimal`; anything else (`"abc"`, `"0x1F"`, `"NaN"`) is refused. Surrounding whitespace is ignored, blank is 0, and another script's digits read as their ASCII digits. There was no `String` → `Number` conversion, so a field declared `Number`, or a type variable bounded by `Number`, could not take number text.
  * **Tests.** `ConverterStringToNumberTest` (4): Long bounds, BigInteger beyond them, BigDecimal forms, refusals. Three fail on the old code. `ConverterEverythingTest` entries for the pair.
* **CHANGE**: **Blank text converts to null for every type without a zero**, as it already did for the dates and times. `Year` gave year 0; `Duration`, `Period`, `UUID`, `Currency`, `Class`, an enum, `File`, `Path`, `Color`, `Dimension`, `Insets`, `Point` and `Rectangle` threw. An empty `byte[]` or `char[]` converted to `File` or `Path`, which goes through its text, is null too. The number types and `Boolean` keep `0` and `false`; `Character` and `Map` are unchanged.
  * **Tests.** `ConverterBlankTextTest` (2): null for each of those types from `""`, spaces and other whitespace, and zero and false kept. The first fails on the old code. Fourteen tests that pinned the old answers (the exceptions, year 0) now expect null.
* **CHANGE**: **`String` → `UUID` ignores surrounding whitespace**, as the number types do: `" f0000000-0000-0000-0000-000000000001\n"` was refused.
  * **Tests.** A `ConverterEverythingTest` entry, failing on the old code.
* **PERFORMANCE**: **`String` → `MonthDay` and `YearMonth` read their common shapes directly**, with no exception and no `DateTimeFormatter`: `"--12-25"`, `"12-25"`, `"12/25"`, `"12.25"`, `"12 25"`, `"2024-12-25"`, and `"2024-12"` for `YearMonth`. Every `MonthDay` shape but the ISO one went through a `DateTimeParseException` first. A month or day out of range, or a date that does not exist, takes the general path as before, so the answers and errors are unchanged: a differential run of 12,200 inputs, valid and not, matched the old code on JDK 21 and JDK 8.
  * **Speed** (ns, JDK 21 / JDK 8): `"12-25"` 310 → 16 / 335 → 17; `"2024-12-25"` 312 → 13 / 361 → 18; `"--12-25"` 69 → 7 / 137 → 10; `"2024-12"` 64 → 11 / 135 → 12.
  * **Tests.** `ConverterMonthDayTextTest` (2): each shape, leap days, and the refusals and their messages. Three planted defects (month and day swapped, the leap year ignored, month 13 accepted) each fail it.
* **PERFORMANCE**: **`ConcurrentList` inserts and removes in the middle in place**: the shorter side moves one slot, and the list keeps exactly the buckets its elements span. `add(index, e)` and `remove(index)` copied the whole list into an `ArrayList` and rebuilt every bucket from it.
  * **Speed** (an insert and a remove at random middle positions, ns, JDK 21 / JDK 8): 100 elements 1,270 → 122 / 787 → 107; 1,000: 6,230 → 834 / 2,356 → 633; 10,000: 63,775 → 8,922 / 25,169 → 5,816; 100,000: 666,023 → 73,040 / 242,545 → 67,515.
  * **Tests.** `ConcurrentListMiddleOpsTest` (2): 200,000 random operations against `ArrayList`, across bucket boundaries and with the head below zero, checking the contents and that only the spanned buckets are held. Four planted defects (shift order, a missing bucket, the end slot's bucket not freed, an insert one off) each fail it.
* **PERFORMANCE**: **`ConcurrentList.get(index)` takes no lock while no write is under way.** It reads between two readings of a version that every write moves (odd while a write holds the lock), and takes the read lock only when a write overlapped the read. The read lock's shared counter was contended by every reader, so concurrent reads serialized on it.
  * **Speed** (ns per `get()` per thread, JDK 21 / JDK 8): 1 reader 7.7 → 3.2 / 7.9 → 3.0; 8 readers 2,000-2,400 → 3.7 / 2,200-2,450 → 3.4; 8 readers alongside a writer 974-995 → 9-11 / 920-1,012 → 17. A write (an `addLast` and a `removeFirst`) costs 0.6-0.7 ns more: 7.4 → 8.1 / 6.8 → 7.4.
  * **Tests.** `ConcurrentListLockFreeGetTest` (2): readers hammer `get()` while writers slide the window from both ends and insert and remove in the middle, across buckets; a read that mixed two states of the list would see a slot a write had emptied, or step back. Three planted defects (no re-check, writers that do not move the version, a version that is even during the write) each fail it, every run; the real code passed ten runs on JDK 21 and JDK 8.
* **TESTING**: **The linked caches leave no link in an entry they unlink** (a guard against GC nepotism). `LRUCache`'s `LOCKING` strategy and `TTLCache` keep their entries in a linked list. A removed node that still pointed at its neighbors would keep them reachable for as long as it lingered: an old-generation node, live until the next full collection, holding young ones, and a chain of such nodes holding a cache's whole history. Both already clear the links; nothing checked that they do.
  * **Tests.** `CacheUnlinkedNodeTest` (9) covers every path that unlinks: remove, eviction for size, replacement of a key, expiry, `clear()`, and `compute()` returning null. Four planted defects (the `LOCKING` remove and clear, the `TTLCache` unlink and clear, each leaving the links) each fail it.
* **TESTING**: **The packaged jar is now run as a bundle inside an OSGi framework.** `OsgiBundleIT` (5, failsafe, after `package`) starts Apache Felix, installs and starts the jar, and uses it from inside: it resolves and starts, converts (`"42"` → `Integer`, `0.1d` → `BigDecimal`, `"12-25"` → `MonthDay`), finds a JDK-internal class by name through the bundle's own loader (the class of every `TimeZone`), passes over a context class loader that cannot see the bundle, and builds a case-insensitive `CompactMap` from the builder. Until this release the jar could not even resolve in a framework, and nothing ran it in one.
  * **The framework is test scope and kept off the unit tests' class path** (`classpathDependencyExcludes`): `ClassUtilitiesOsgiAbsentTest` checks the behavior when no OSGi classes are present.
  * **Planted defects caught:** the manifest requiring the JDK-internal packages again (the bundle does not resolve), using the context class loader whatever it can see, and no fallback to the JDK's loaders. Each fails it. It passes on JDK 21 and JDK 8.
* **TESTING**: **`ConverterEverythingTest` runs every row it declares.** Its table maps a (source, target) pair to rows, and 45 pairs were declared twice, so the second declaration replaced the first and 144 rows never ran. Among them were `URI` → `URL`'s three successful conversions (only its refusal of a `urn:` ran), `Dimension` → `Map`'s, and rows for the atomics, the primitives, `BigDecimal` and `BigInteger`. A pair declared again now adds its rows to the earlier ones. 53 rows were the same in both declarations and now run once; 12 later declarations held only such rows and are gone. The other 91 rows are new to the run and all pass: 262 more cases (12,902 → 13,164).
  * **Planted defect caught:** `URI` → `URL` dropping the port and the fragment passed the old table, and fails four cases of the new one.
* **TESTING**: **143 per-type conversion tests that repeated a `ConverterEverythingTest` row, or another test in their own file, are removed, with three empty test classes.** `PathConversionsTest` 34, `FileConversionsTest` 31, `ColorConversionsTest` 18, `PointConversionsTest` 17, `InsetsConversionsTest` 15, `RectangleConversionsTest` 13, `DimensionConversionsTest` 11, `EnumConversionsTest` 4; `MonthDayConversionsTest`, `LocalDateConversionsTest` and `InstantConversionsTest` held no tests. Each removed test was matched to the table row it repeats, or to the test in its own file. The tests that stay check what the table does not: blank text, other text forms and map keys, error messages, refused conversions, identity, and conversion to a concrete enum (the table converts only to the abstract `Enum`, and expects a refusal). One removed test could not fail when its conversion threw: it caught the exception and logged it; `testInsetsNativeToStringRoundTrip` checks the same conversion.
  * **Coverage is unchanged.** Measured from a fresh JaCoCo file for each run (JDK 21), every class of the converter and geometry packages has identical instruction, branch, line and method counts, as does every other class except three concurrent ones, whose counts vary from run to run with thread timing (none dropped).
* **TESTING**: **`ConverterEverythingTest` checks that `File`, `Path`, `Color`, `Dimension`, `Point`, `Rectangle` and `Insets` convert to their own type as the same object.** They are now in the table's list of immutable types, whose same-type rows assert the identical instance; the seven per-type `*_identity` tests, one value each, are removed.
  * **Planted defect caught:** `File` → `File` and `Insets` → `Insets` returning a copy passed the old table, and fail six rows of the new one.
#### 4.112.0 - 2026-09-23
* **BUG FIX (regression since 4.1.0)**: **`SafeSimpleDateFormat` could not parse a numeric field followed by the locale's decimal separator** -- `yyyy-MM-dd HH:mm:ss.SSS`, `yyyy-MM-dd'T'HH:mm:ss.SSSZ`, `dd.MM.yyyy`, `yyyy.MM.dd`, `HH.mm` under a '.' locale, `ss,SSS` under a ',' locale. Parsing `"2026-09-24 13:33:28.144"` threw `ParseException: Unparseable date`, where `SimpleDateFormat` with the same pattern parses it. The default `NumberFormat` was the locale's general number format (grouping off), which reads `28.144` as ONE number: the seconds field swallowed the milliseconds and the pattern's `.` found nothing left to match. `SimpleDateFormat` installs the locale's INTEGER format, which stops at the separator. **Why it surfaced now:** the defect predates 4.0.0, but there the constructor put that number format only on the constructing thread's formatter; every other thread got a stock `SimpleDateFormat`, so request threads never saw it. The 4.1.0 copy-on-write rewrite builds every thread's formatter from one configuration, which carried the bad default to all of them, and to the static `getDateFormat()`. The default is now exactly `SimpleDateFormat`'s own -- `NumberFormat.getIntegerInstance(locale)`, grouping off, integer-only parsing -- so parsing and formatting match `SimpleDateFormat` on every thread. **Millisecond times parse again**, to the exact millisecond. As in `SimpleDateFormat`, `S` is a count of milliseconds, not a fraction (`28.5` is 5 ms; `28.144123` adds 144,123 ms): for fractional seconds of any length use `DateUtilities.parseDate()` or `java.time`. A number format the caller sets with `setNumberFormat()` is used as given, as before. `SafeSimpleDateFormatNumberFieldTest` (48): every pattern above against `SimpleDateFormat` as the oracle, on the constructing thread, another thread and the static accessor, formatting unchanged, exact milliseconds, a concurrent millisecond round trip, and the caller's number format honoured. Against 4.111.0, 22 of its first 42 fail -- every dotted or comma pattern on every path -- and the patterns without a separator, formatting, and the caller's-format case pass.
* **BUG FIX (robustness)**: **`toString()` on a java-util container no longer dies with `StackOverflowError` when the graph it holds contains a cycle.** A map holding a list that holds the map, a parent/child pair, a `Map.put()` whose return value is stored back -- these graphs are rarely built on purpose, which is why they surface in production rather than in tests. n-cube hit exactly this in its execution trace (6.73.0). Of java-util's 24 Map, Set and List implementations, **15 overflowed** on at least one of four shapes (holding itself; a loop through a plain JDK map; through a plain JDK list; through a second instance of the same class), for one of four reasons:
  * **A wrapper delegated its `toString()`** to the container it wraps -- `TrackingMap`, `CaseInsensitiveSet`, `CompactSet` (and `CompactCIHashSet` / `CompactCILinkedSet` / `CompactLinkedSet`), `LRUCache`. The inner container's self-guard compares against the INNER container, never the wrapper, so **even a wrapper holding itself** overflowed.
  * **A direct-only guard** -- `e == this ? "(this ...)" : e` -- which a two-step loop walks straight past: `MapUtilities.mapToString()` (and so `CompactMap`, `ConcurrentHashMapNullSafe`, `ConcurrentNavigableMapNullSafe`, which delegate to it), `ConcurrentSet`, `ConcurrentList`, `LockingLRUCacheStrategy`.
  * **No guard at all** (`TTLCache`), or the JDK's direct-only guard inherited unchanged (`CaseInsensitiveMap`, `ClassValueMap`, `IdentitySet`, `ConcurrentNavigableSetNullSafe`).
  * **A view that hashed values.** `ThreadedLRUCacheStrategy.entrySet()` and `MultiKeyMap.entrySet()` built a hash set of entries, and `Map.Entry.hashCode()` hashes the VALUE -- so `entrySet()`, and the `toString()` that walks it, recursed before rendering a single character. Both now snapshot keyed by KEY (as `LockingLRUCacheStrategy` already did), which also stops them hashing every value on every call.
  * **The fix** is one shared walk (package-private `CycleSafeToString`) that every java-util container now renders through. The containers being rendered sit on a per-thread path, by **identity** (`hashCode()` on a cyclic map is itself unbounded recursion) and as a **path** rather than a visited set, so a node reached twice by different routes is a diamond, not a loop, and still renders in full. Because the path is shared by all of them, a loop is seen however many steps away it closes and whichever container it closes through. The container it closes at renders as **`(cycle)`** -- the marker n-cube's trace already uses -- while direct self-containment keeps each class's existing marker (`(this Map)`, `(this Collection)`, `ConcurrentSet`'s `(this Set)`, `MultiKeyMap`'s `(this Map ♻️)`).
  * **Nothing acyclic changes.** Nested maps and collections whose `toString()` is the JDK's standard one are walked rather than delegated to -- that is what lets the walk see through them -- and render identically; a container with its own `toString()` format keeps it; arrays are still printed, never expanded. Verified by generating `toString()` for the same ordinary contents on the previous release and on this one, and by a test pinning every container's output to the JDK's rendering of its own iteration order. **One ordering change:** a THREADED `LRUCache` now renders (and iterates `entrySet()`) in cache order rather than the arbitrary order of its old hash set of entries. **One inherent limit:** a loop made entirely of containers that render themselves -- synchronized or unmodifiable wrappers, `Hashtable`, `Vector` -- never re-enters java-util, so it behaves exactly as it does in the JDK.
  * **Bounded on a densely cross-linked graph.** A path prints every route through a graph that does not repeat a container -- exactly what the JDK prints for a DAG -- but in a clique of maps, each holding all the others, the number of such routes grows factorially: ten such maps rendered ~100 million characters and eleven exhausted the heap (killing a test JVM outright), where the JDK would have overflowed the stack at once. So once a render has printed `(cycle)` -- which the JDK never prints -- and has rendered 100,000 values (`LOOP_BUDGET`), every container it has not yet expanded prints as `...`. A clique of 10 or of 200 maps now renders in about 0.6-0.9 MB and 10 ms. Anything the JDK can print, acyclic or holding only itself, never meets the budget, however large; a loop that is not dense, such as a tree whose nodes point back to their parent, renders in full up to that size. `MultiKeyMap`'s own walk of the collections and arrays in its values spends the same budget.
  * **`LockingLRUCacheStrategy`** printed a map holding itself as `(this Collection)`; it now prints `(this Map)`. It also no longer calls its values' `toString()` while holding the cache lock -- `entrySet()` snapshots under the lock and rendering happens after it is released.
  * `MultiKeyMap`'s emoji rendering is unchanged; its value formatter, which walked collections and arrays itself with no memory of where it had been, now shares the same path, so a value list or array that contains itself renders `[x, (cycle)]`.
* **BUG FIX**: **`MultiKeyMap` overflowed on a collection KEY that contains itself.** Arrays were already handled -- `expandWithHash` tracks the containers it has visited -- but `process1DCollection` hashed each element *before* checking whether it was a nested collection, and for a collection that contains itself `hashCode()` never returns. It now checks first, as its array twins `process1DObjectArray` and `flattenCollectionN` already did; the hash it skipped was discarded anyway, since a nested key is re-hashed structurally.
* **BUG FIX**: **`GraphComparator.applyDelta()` looped forever on an exception whose cause chain is a cycle** (A caused by B caused by A), appending to its error text until the heap ran out. The walk now remembers the causes it has written, as `ExceptionUtilities.getDeepestException()` already did. `GraphComparator.Delta.toString()` also renders its source and target values through the cycle-safe walk, since they come from the caller's object graphs.
* **BUILD**: Test-scope `json-io` 4.110.0 → **4.111.0**, by the trail-by-one rule (java-util tests against the latest released json-io). Held, with the reason in `pom.xml`: `agrona` 1.23.1 (2.x dropped Java 8), `assertj-core` 3.27.7 (4.0.0-M1 is a milestone), `junit-jupiter` 5.14.4 (JUnit 6 requires Java 17; this project compiles `release 8`), `mockito` 4.11.0 (JDK support). Every plugin is already at its latest version. java-util still ships with **no runtime dependencies**.
* **TESTING**: `MapUtilitiesCycleTest` (22) and `ContainerToStringCycleTest` (130) -- every container against all four cycle shapes, its views, and its acyclic output. Run against the previous release, 85 of 129 cases fail -- every cycle check -- and the 44 that pass there are exactly the preservation checks (acyclic output, and the direct-self and view cases that were already correct). The `applyDelta` case was left out of that run because on the previous release it never finishes, and so were the three clique cases, which on the previous release exhaust the heap and take the test JVM down with them.

#### 4.111.0 - 2026-09-18
* **CONVERSION (correctness)**: **`"on"` converted to `false`.** The truth set accepted `true`, `t`, `1`, `y` and `yes` but not `on` — so the word that says true came back **false**: an inversion rather than a rejection, and a silent one, since anything unrecognised returns `false` rather than throwing. Same defect as 4.110.0's `"yes"`, and the last common spelling that was missing. `on` is what a checkbox, a feature flag and a properties file write; it is what PostgreSQL's own boolean input accepts alongside `true`/`yes`/`t`/`y`/`1`; and n-cube's decision-table equality already treats `on`/`off` as a boolean pair — so the two libraries disagreed about the same word. `on` now joins `yes`, case-insensitively. `off` needed no entry, being already `false` through the catch-all exactly as `no` is, but it is now pinned by a test so that holds deliberately rather than by accident. `StringBooleanTruthSetTest`.
* **CONVERSION (usability)**: **Surrounding whitespace no longer decides whether a value converts.** `" 42 "` converted cleanly to `Double` and threw for `Integer`, `Long`, `Short`, `Byte`, `BigInteger` and `BigDecimal` — an inconsistency inherited from the JDK, where `Double.parseDouble` ignores leading and trailing whitespace by contract and `Integer.parseInt` does not. One abstraction should give one answer, so all eight String→number conversions now trim before parsing. **For numbers this can only turn a throw into a value:** a string with no surrounding whitespace is unchanged by `trim()`, and a blank one already returned zero through the blank-aware `isEmpty()` guard, so no numeric call that succeeds today changes its result. `String`→`Boolean` trims as well, and there the change is visible rather than additive: `" true "` returned **`false`** — the same silent inversion as the spellings above — and now returns `true`; it is trimmed only after the `"true"`/`"false"` fast path misses, so the common unpadded case pays nothing. Whitespace INSIDE a value is untouched and still rejected — `"-  5"`, `"4 2"`, `"1 000"` are not numbers and `"tr ue"` is not a boolean — and that is pinned by tests so the change is not later read as tolerating whitespace anywhere. Deliberately **not** applied to `Character`, where `" "` is a legitimate space value that trimming would silently turn into NUL (also pinned). The boolean words and the whitespace rule are now documented in `userguide.md` under *Supported Conversions*, rather than left to be discovered. `StringNumericTrimTest`, `StringBooleanTruthSetTest`.
* **BUILD**: Test-scope dependencies: `json-io` 4.108.0 → **4.110.0**, `guava` 33.6.0-jre → **33.7.1-jre**, `commons-collections4` 4.5.0 → **4.6.0** (verified still compiled for Java 8). Held deliberately, each now carrying the reason in `pom.xml`: `agrona` stays at 1.23.1 (2.x dropped Java 8), `mockito` at 4.11.0 (JDK support), `assertj-core` at 3.27.7 (4.0.0-M1 is a milestone, not a release), and `junit-jupiter` at 5.14.4 (JUnit 6 requires Java 17; this project compiles `release 8`). `maven-bundle-plugin` 6.1.0 → 6.2.0. java-util continues to ship with **no runtime dependencies** — every dependency is test scope.

#### 4.110.0 - 2026-08-02
* **CONVERSION (correctness)**: **`Converter.convert()` silently inverted `"Yes"` and silently mangled every `Float`.** Both were found while building decision-table type inference in n-cube, which asks the Converter whether a value survives a type change; it could not trust either answer.
  * **`"yes"`/`"Yes"`/`"YES"` converted to `false`.** The truth set accepted `"true"`, `"t"`, `"1"` and `"y"` — but not the spelled-out word. So `"y"` was `true` while `"Yes"` was **`false`**: not a rejection, an **inversion**, and a silent one, since anything unrecognised returns `false` rather than throwing. Any data written the long way read as the opposite of what it said, which for the No/Yes answers commonly held in decision-table output columns would have flipped every affirmative. `"yes"` now joins `"y"`, case-insensitively; `"no"` was already `false` (though only because everything unmatched is).
  * **`Float` → `Double` and `Float` → `BigDecimal` widened through the bits instead of the decimal.** `3.7f` became `3.700000047683716` and `0.1f` became `0.10000000149011612`, because `Float.doubleValue()` faithfully preserves the binary approximation rather than the number the author wrote. Widening now goes through `Float.toString()`, which by contract emits the shortest decimal that uniquely identifies the float, so `3.7f` becomes `3.7`. **Nothing is lost:** every value still converts back to the identical float, including `Float.MIN_VALUE` and `Float.MAX_VALUE`; what changes is only *which* of the doubles that map back to that float you land on, and the one that prints as the float prints is the one that was meant. `BigDecimal` was the worse of the two — a type whose entire purpose is exact decimal representation was being handed the binary error. **Behaviour change at the extremes:** `Float.MAX_VALUE` → `Double` is now `3.4028235E38` rather than `3.4028234663852886E38`. The `Float`→`BigDecimal` test cases had been chosen as exactly-representable values with the comment *"Changed to avoid float precision issues"* — the defect was being worked around rather than reported.
  * Integral targets are unaffected: `Float`/`Double`/`BigDecimal` → `Long`/`BigInteger` still truncate, which is inherent to discarding a fraction.
  * New cases live in the shared `ConverterEverythingTest` table, so they are exercised through every path — direct conversion, JSON-IO round-trip, and TOON.
  * **`BigDecimal` → `String` no longer strips the scale.** It called `stripTrailingZeros()` first, so `new BigDecimal("1.50")` rendered as `"1.5"` — which broke round-tripping, because `BigDecimal` equality **includes scale**, so `BigDecimal → String → BigDecimal` returned a value not `equals()` to the original. Scale is information: it is what separates a money amount written to the cent from the same quantity written to the tenth. Now `toPlainString()`, which keeps the scale and still never emits scientific notation — the property the old implementation was really relying on. **Behaviour change:** any caller rendering a `BigDecimal` through the Converter now sees trailing zeros preserved. The blast radius is exactly one registration; nothing else reaches this method, and the `stripTrailingZeros()` in `DeepEquals` is deliberately untouched because *comparison* should treat 1.50 and 1.5 as equal even though *rendering* should not.
* **BUILD**: `maven-jar-plugin` 3.5.0 → 3.5.1, `maven-bundle-plugin` 6.0.2 → 6.1.0. JUnit (5.14.4), Mockito (4.11.0) and agrona (1.23.1) are each already the newest release in the series their JDK-8 support pins them to; `json-io` stays one release behind (4.108.0) by the trail-by-one rule that keeps the two projects from cycling.
* **TESTING**: **The `performRelease` benchmarks now detect performance regressions instead of printing numbers nobody compares — and the four slowest test classes got 9x faster.** They previously burned ~110 seconds of every release build, and roughly 80 of those seconds sat in tests with **no assertions at all**: they could not fail, could not detect a regression, and produced absolute millisecond figures that are not comparable between a dev laptop, a CI runner, and the release machine. A human had to read the log and remember last release's numbers.
  * **New `PerfRatchet` test harness** records each benchmark as a **ratio against a yardstick workload measured in the same JVM run, interleaved trial-for-trial** — `CaseInsensitiveMap` against the `LinkedHashMap` it wraps, Cedar's `MultiKeyMap` against Apache Commons', the caches against `HashMap`/`LinkedHashMap`. Machine speed divides out, so `1.85x` means "1.85 times the cost of the JDK equivalent" on any hardware. 19 metrics are tracked in `src/test/resources/perf-baseline.properties`.
  * **Nothing fails the build.** Verdicts are `OK` / `IMPROVED` / `REGRESSED` / `NEW`, logged and written to `target/perf-report.tsv`; a regression is a WARNING, not a test failure, because turning normal variance into a red build is how a perf signal gets ignored. `-Dperf.strict=true` opts into failing, for a machine whose numbers you trust.
  * **The ratchet locks in gains.** `-Dperf.baseline.update=true` writes improvements back and never writes a regression, so the bar only moves toward faster. `-Dperf.baseline.reset=true` writes every metric in either direction, because a pure ratchet cannot repair itself — a baseline first captured from an unluckily *fast* reading would report a regression forever and update mode by definition will not raise it back.
  * **Self-calibrating bars.** Each metric publishes the interquartile scatter of its own trials, and the regression/improvement bar is the configured floor *or* that scatter, whichever is larger. A metric whose trials disagreed by 35% cannot credibly report a 25% change; one that measured to 3% is held to the tight floor. This is what lets noisy allocation-heavy benchmarks coexist with precise ones without slackening the threshold for everybody. An improvement must also clear the noise floor before it can be ratcheted in, so a lucky outlier cannot move the bar out of reach.
  * **Measurement fixes found along the way.** Warmup must cover *every* variant sharing a lambda body before any is measured (`PerfRatchet.warmAll`), or whichever runs first absorbs the JIT cost for all of them — that artifact alone was inflating one metric's noise from ~10% to 63%. Baselines must be calibrated from a **full suite** run: the same metrics scattered 2-15% in isolation and 5-68% alongside the other ~20,000 tests. `LRUCache`'s THREADED strategy is deliberately **not** tracked — its ratio ranged 1.14x-3.48x across identical runs because a background cleanup thread services its reads, and that variance is invisible within a single run, so it would report confident regressions on nothing but thread scheduling.
  * **`CaseInsensitiveMapTest` 53.1s → 0.63s.** `testPerformance`/`testPerformance2` copied a 10,000-entry map 100,000 times each — **two billion entry copies** — to print four numbers. They were already a matched CaseInsensitiveMap-vs-LinkedHashMap pair; they simply never divided one by the other. Separately, `testCaseInsensitiveMapPerformanceComparison` ran every workload **twice**: `timeMapOperations` for a fixed 2000&nbsp;ms, then `countOps` — a byte-for-byte copy of it plus a counter — for another 2000&nbsp;ms. Since the loop budget *was* 2000&nbsp;ms, the "time" it measured was always ≈2000 by construction, which is why every line of its output read "operations in 2,000 ms". **Half that test's 24 seconds computed a constant.**
  * **`MultiKeyMapPerformanceComparisonTest` 25.1s → 0.90s.** A 6x7 matrix up to 250,000 entries, ten iterations, both implementations: ~105 million map operations, plus a `Thread.sleep(100)` and an explicit `System.gc()` per configuration (4.2 seconds of sleeping, 42 forced collections). Two of the seven data sizes accounted for 80% of the work while adding nothing the smaller sizes did not show.
  * **`LRUCacheTest` 21.8s → 7.5s.** `testCacheBlast` put 10,000,000 entries per strategy into a **1000-entry** cache — 99.99% evicted on arrival — to assert one number; 200,000 puts is still 200x capacity. `testSpeed` put another 10,000,000 per strategy (~800MB each, per its own comment) and asserted nothing. The `@AfterEach` `System.gc()` meant ~60 forced full collections across the class and is gone.
  * **`TTLCacheTest` 10.1s → 2.7s.** 9.4 of its 10.1 seconds were `Thread.sleep`. One test already demonstrated the fast idiom (200ms TTL, 100ms purge, 350ms wait) while four others used 10x larger values to prove the same properties; they now match. Total sleep is 1.9s.
  * **Two findings worth knowing.** `CaseInsensitiveMap(TreeMap)` is ~1.2-1.3x **slower** than plain `TreeMap(String.CASE_INSENSITIVE_ORDER)` — you pay for key wrapping *and* case-insensitive comparison — so a sorted case-insensitive map is better served by the JDK directly; the `HashMap`/`LinkedHashMap`-backed variants are 3x and 7x *faster* than that same TreeMap. And `CaseInsensitiveMap`'s copy constructor runs at parity (~1.0x) with `LinkedHashMap`'s, confirming the `CaseInsensitiveString` key-reuse optimization pays off. Both are now tracked.
  * `scripts/extract-perf-results.sh` renders the tracked metrics from the TSV, since the summary block is printed by a JVM shutdown hook that fires after Surefire stops capturing stdout and so never reaches the deploy log. **No production code changed** — this is test infrastructure only. Suite green: 20,380 tests (release mode), 20,377 (normal).

#### 4.109.0 - 2026-07-26
* **SECURITY (critical)**: **`ClassUtilities.newInstance()` did not enforce its own denylist on the named-parameter path — fixes the constructor-injection RCE reported against `json-io` 4.108.0.** json-io performs no security checks of its own; it reads a class name from untrusted input (`@type`) and delegates instantiation here, so the fix belongs in java-util. Released in lock-step with `json-io` 4.109.0.
  * **The enforcement hole (the actual vulnerability).** `newInstance(Converter, Class, Object)` routes a `Map` of arguments to `newInstanceWithNamedParameters()`, which **never called `SecurityChecker.verifyClass()`** — only the positional fallback did. A class the checker itself reported as blocked was therefore constructed anyway: `isSecurityBlocked(EvilLoader) == true` (a `ClassLoader` subclass, blocked via the supertype walk) while `newInstance(EvilLoader.class, {"marker": ...})` ran its constructor. The report characterized the defect as an incomplete denylist, but the reported PoC flowed entirely through this unchecked path, so **adding the gadget class to the list would not have closed it.** The check now happens once at the public entry point, outside every `try` (a `SecurityException` raised deeper down was being swallowed by the fallback handlers that retry on `Exception`), and is re-asserted inside `newInstanceWithNamedParameters` as defense in depth. Specifically, `ClassPathXmlApplicationContext(String... configLocations)` was reachable because the varargs branch of named matching widens a single attacker string into a `String[1]`, so every parameter matched and the constructor fired `refresh()` — fetching the attacker's bean XML and instantiating `ProcessBuilder` with `init-method="start"` inside a Spring child context, beyond this gate's reach.
  * **`verifyClass()` ignored the blocked-package prefixes.** The prefix rules (`javax.script.`, `jdk.nashorn.`) lived only in `isSecurityBlockedName()` and were consulted only when loading *by name*; a class already in hand from a blocked package passed. Name, identity, prefix, and supertype checking are now unified behind one gate used by both the loading and instantiation paths.
  * **The enclosing class of an inner class was constructed ungated** (`newInstance` instantiates it to supply the outer instance). It now gets its own verdict, verified outside the surrounding catch-all that would otherwise downgrade the refusal into a silent fall-through.
  * **Denylist expanded to cover the gadget families**, listed by *interface/supertype name* so one entry covers every implementation, present or future, without java-util depending on the library: indirect loaders (`org.springframework.context.ApplicationContext`, `org.springframework.beans.factory.BeanFactory`), JNDI (`javax.naming.`, `com.sun.jndi.`, `com.sun.rowset.JdbcRowSetImpl`), bytecode carriers (`javax.xml.transform.Templates`, `com.sun.org.apache.xalan.`), `java.io.ObjectInputStream`, constructors that perform network I/O (`Socket`, `ServerSocket`, `DatagramSocket`, `URLConnection`) or create/truncate a file (`FileOutputStream`, `FileWriter`, `RandomAccessFile`), further scripting engines (Rhino, BeanShell, Jython, `groovy.lang.GroovyShell`), and `java.rmi.`/`javax.management.remote.`/`jdk.internal.`. Read-side file I/O (`FileInputStream`, `FileReader`) is deliberately left constructible — opening a read handle is not a side effect on the order of spawning a process or truncating a file. `java.lang.invoke.MethodHandle`/`MethodHandles` were deliberately **not** added: both are non-instantiable (abstract / no public constructor), so blocking them would only break resolution of an ordinary `MethodHandle`-typed field; `MethodHandles$Lookup`, the object that actually grants reflective module-opening power, remains blocked.
  * **New configuration**, because expanding a denylist can break a consumer who legitimately constructs one of these types: `SecurityChecker.addBlockedClass(String)`, `addBlockedPackage(String)`, `allowClass(String)`, and `clearSecurityOverrides()`, plus the `classutilities.security.blocked.classes`, `classutilities.security.blocked.packages`, and `classutilities.security.unblocked` system properties. Precedence is an explicit unblock first, then any blocking rule, then allowed. `allowClass` overrides built-ins and is documented as at-your-own-risk. Programmatic rules are consulted *ahead of* the `ClassValue` verdict cache, which is what lets them take effect immediately for already-checked classes with no invalidation machinery; the default configuration (no programmatic rules) adds one volatile boolean read to the pre-existing lookup.
  * Pinned by 14 new tests that reproduce the reported payload against a dependency-free stand-in for `ClassPathXmlApplicationContext` — shaped with the same varargs constructor and constructor side effect, and compiled with `-parameters` as Spring's own jars are, so name matching really is viable and the test cannot pass for the wrong reason. All 20,376 tests pass, as do all 4,748 `json-io` 4.109.0 tests (both Spring modules included) against this build.
* **BUILD**: `json-io` test-scope dependency 4.107.0 → 4.108.0 (the "latest released json-io minus 1" rule shifts forward now that json-io 4.108.0 is out). Test scope only — no consumer-facing surface.

#### 4.108.0 - 2026-07-09
* **TESTING**: Added `FastReader` buffer-boundary regression coverage — differential sweeps that exercise the internal 8192-char buffer refill boundary (and 16384) for `readUntil`, `readLine` (including a `\r\n` line ending straddling the boundary), bulk `read(char[])`, and the borrowed `readUntil` + fallback pattern. They reconstruct the stream and compare against the original, confirming `FastReader` handles delimiters and line endings correctly across a refill. **No behavioral change.** Released in lock-step with `json-io` 4.108.0 (which fixes a slow-path escape bug that was in json-io's `CharStreamTokenizer`, not here). The `json-io` test-scope dependency remains `4.107.0` — it always trails java-util by one release to avoid the java-util↔json-io dependency cycle.

#### 4.107.0 - 2026-07-06
* **DOCUMENTATION**: `ClassValueMap` and `ClassValueSet` Javadoc, `userguide.md`, and `README.md` now document the critical usage constraints uncovered by profiling. These are **specialists**, not general-purpose faster `Map<Class,V>`/`Set<Class>`: they win only for a *few, pre-populated, read-mostly* class registries. The docs now spell out (1) the **non-local cost** — every `Class` has one small, `PROBE_LIMIT`-bounded ClassValue cache array shared across *all* `ClassValue` instances, so many instances keyed on the same classes thrash each other and the "faster than `ConcurrentHashMap`" advantage inverts past ~10; (2) that **lazy get-then-`put` population is an anti-pattern** because `put()` invalidates the whole ClassValue (≈O(N²) as classes warm up); and (3) that the **strong-referencing backing store forfeits `ClassValue`'s leak-safety**. No behavioral change.
* **PERFORMANCE (concurrency)**: `ClassUtilities.trySetAccessible` no longer serializes concurrent callers on a single lock. Its `accessibilityCache` was a `Collections.synchronizedMap(WeakHashMap)` probed on **every** object instantiation and field injection; under multi-threaded deserialization this monitor was the dominant contention point — 13-thread profiling of concurrent json-io reads showed ~74% of worker threads BLOCKED on it, and after the change profiling shows zero threads blocked on it. It is now a lock-free `ConcurrentHashMap`. The map only ever stores failures (members whose module refuses to open — JPMS-sealed platform/JDK internals on the bootstrap loader, which are never unloaded), so dropping the weak keys retains no application or dynamically-loaded (e.g. Groovy) classloaders; caching logic is otherwise unchanged. Single-threaded performance is unchanged.
* **PERFORMANCE**: `ReflectionUtils.getAllConstructors(Class)` no longer allocates on a cache hit. It previously wrapped the class in a `SortedConstructorsCacheKey` on every call just to probe the cache, and passed a capturing compute lambda that was allocated at the call site even when unused — both per-object costs on hot reflection/deserialization paths (e.g. json-io's per-instance construction). The cache is now keyed directly by `Class` (which already has identity `equals`/`hashCode`, so the anti-poisoning/security semantics are unchanged), with a `get()`-first fast path that allocates nothing on hits; the redundant `SortedConstructorsCacheKey` class was removed.

#### 4.106.0 - 2026-07-04
* **API DEPRECATION**: **`UrlUtilities` is deprecated and scheduled for removal in java-util 5.0.** A GitHub/GitLab/web code-search sweep found no independent consumer: json-io and the rest of java-util do not use it, `jdereg/n-cube` already refactored off it, and the only remaining callers are a dormant `web-spider` (last touched 2019, pinned to java-util 1.34.0) and a stale collaborator fork of old n-cube. A dependency-bloat research corpus even flags the class as *unused* where java-util appears transitively. The class wraps the legacy `HttpURLConnection` stack with manual cookie stores, an all-trusting `TrustManager`/`HostnameVerifier`, and global mutable user-agent/referrer state. New code should use `java.net.http.HttpClient` (JDK 11+) for HTTP and `IOUtilities` for stream transfer/compression/`URLConnection` input; the naive-SSL and cookie helpers have no planned replacement (they are unsafe for production). Class-level `@Deprecated` only in the 4.x line — behavior unchanged; the class remains present and functional through 4.x and will be deleted in the 5.0 major release (removing a public class is a binary-breaking change reserved for a major bump).
* **DOC / PERFORMANCE**: `Traverser`'s three `@deprecated` javadocs pointed callers at `{@link #traverse(Object, Set, Consumer)}` — a signature that resolves only to the *private* internal overload, so the suggested replacement was uncallable; they now point at the public `traverse(Object, Consumer, Set)`. `TrackingMap`'s `readKeys` tracking set is presized with load-factor headroom (`size/0.75 + 1`) so tracking every key of a fully-read map never rehashes (`new HashSet<>(size)` under-sizes the table and rehashes at 0.75×size). Reviewed `TypeUtilities` and `Unsafe` in the same pass — no defects found (the type-resolution cache tolerates null values, `getAllDeclaredFields`'s custom-filter path is cache-keyed by the stable singleton filter, and `Unsafe`'s ReflectionFactory→sun.misc.Unsafe fallback chain with per-class constructor caching is correct). Note: `TypeUtilities.ParameterizedTypeImpl.getActualTypeArguments()` deliberately returns its internal array (documented perf choice; library consumers treat it read-only) — left as-is.
* **SECURITY**: **`RegexUtilities` timeout protection now actually stops runaway matches.** Same defect class fixed in `DateUtilities` earlier this release: `java.util.regex` never checks interrupts, so `Future.cancel(true)` left each timed-out match burning a pool thread's CPU until backtracking completed. `RegexUtilities`' executor is *bounded* (≤8 threads), so a handful of ReDoS inputs permanently saturated the pool — after which **every** `safe*` operation failed, including trivial ones (the "protection" converted ReDoS into a total regex outage). All five timeout-protected operations (`safeMatches`/`safeFind`/`safeReplaceFirst`/`safeReplaceAll`/`safeSplit`) now match over an interrupt-checking `CharSequence`, so cancellation aborts the match promptly and the thread returns to the pool. Pinned by a test that fires 10 catastrophic matches (more than the pool's thread count) and verifies trivial operations still work — note the attack pattern uses sequential unbounded quantifiers, since JDK 9+'s loop memoization defuses the classic `(a+)+$` form. Also fixed the "rejected due timeout" message typo.
* **BUG FIX**: **`SafeSimpleDateFormat` — `clone()` no longer entangles the copy with the original, and serialization works.** The inherited `DateFormat.clone()` made a shallow copy sharing the `AtomicReference` configuration state, so `clone.setTimeZone(...)` silently reconfigured the original (and vice versa); `clone()` now detaches the state. The class has always declared `serialVersionUID` (with `DateFormat` being `Serializable`), but its internal `State`/`NFSig` classes weren't — `writeObject` threw `NotSerializableException`; both are now `Serializable` and round-trip with pattern/timezone intact. Also `StreamGobbler._result` is now `volatile`: it's written by the gobbler thread and read by the launcher, and `Executor` joins with a *deadline* — a timed-out join read the field with no happens-before edge. And `SystemUtilities.OS_NAME` (public constant) now lowercases with `Locale.ROOT` — under a Turkish default locale, "Windows" lowercased to "wındows" (dotless ı), breaking any consumer's `contains("windows")` check. Reviewed `StringUtilities` in the same pass — no defects found (the equalsIgnoreCase/hashCodeIgnoreCase fold consistency, Levenshtein buffers, and repeat() overflow guards are all sound).
* **BUG FIX**: **`MultiKeyMap.size()`/`isEmpty()`/`longSize()` no longer transiently collapse toward 0 during a resize.** `resizeInternal` zeroed `atomicSize` and re-incremented it once per entry while rehashing under the all-stripes lock — but the size accessors are lock-free, so a concurrent reader could observe `isEmpty() == true` (or any partial count) on a fully populated map mid-resize. The entry count is invariant during a resize, so the counter is no longer touched — which also removes n atomic RMWs from the global-pause window. Pinned by an insert-only reader/writer test asserting size monotonicity across multiple resizes.
